# SOURCE LEDGER — CYCLES 1–52

Cycle 52 (2026-10-20) added **S-252 … S-266** (see "Cycle 52 sources" at the end of this file) and the
**"Cycle 52 gap verdicts"** block. Cycle 52's substantive work was twofold: the register's **prior-art
re-read of IDEA-011 (rank 1) against 2025–2026 literature it had not yet seen** — the register's own
cross-cutting note identifies this as its highest-value act, and a null there is worth more than another
bounded negative — and **one new mechanism family screen**, the register's first screen of a
**halt-and-restriction** family (venue-imposed trading halts, price bands and market-wide circuit breakers),
logged as a **bounded negative (R-032)** on six independent reasons. The re-read produced four new sources
on the IFRS-adoption literature and standard-setter publications (S-252 … S-255), including a **systematic
review of the field that inventories the outcomes but not drift** (S-253), a **published country-level
conditioner that flips the sign of the post-adoption information effect** (S-252) and thereby enters the
candidate as a mandatory control, and the **published jurisdiction-profile adoption calendar plus the
published IFRS 18 effective date** (S-254, S-255) that open the new gap **G-32** — a prospective, dated
adoption window in which the candidate's first-adopter/second-adopter discriminator can be identified within
a single standard. Eleven further sources (S-256 … S-266) were added for the R-032 screen: the
market-wide circuit-breaker literature (S-256, S-257, S-258, S-266), the LULD Plan and the SEC's own LULD
staff papers (S-259, S-260, S-262), the ESMA/MiFID II circuit-breaker and **publication-of-trading-halts**
regime and the European volatility-interruption literature (S-261, S-263, S-264), and the early-versus-late
IFRS adopter comparator (S-265). Cycle 52 changed no score and created no candidate. The register now holds
**S-001 … S-209** and **S-220 … S-266**; the identifiers **S-210 … S-219** remain reserved and unused.

Cycle 51 (2026-10-20) added **S-247 … S-251** (see "Cycle 51 sources" at the end of this file), a
**backfilled "Cycles 44–50 gap verdicts" block** (none of those seven cycles wrote a verdict block into
this file, so their verdicts existed only in the cycle log and the handoff — a state-integrity gap of the
same kind as the Cycle-37 and Cycle-40 repairs) and the **"Cycle 51 gap verdicts"** block. Cycle 51's
substantive work was the register's **first screen of a *price-formation* family**: randomised closing
prices (a venue drawing the official close randomly inside an order-book-determined range) and real-time
closing-auction imbalance publication, screened as one family and logged as a **bounded negative (R-031)**
on five independent reasons — the mechanism is published prior art (S-247, S-248, S-249, with S-149 and
S-150 already held), a randomised close is a *symmetric* draw with no sign-determining state and a
single-price auction cannot express the compensation for the draw in the price, the exchange designs the
imbalance publication to attract offsetting interest so the concession is competed away inside the auction
(S-251), the settlement channel does not propagate (S-250) and the register's own instrument universe has
no randomised close at all (S-251), and the cost floor plus the proprietary nature of the decision-time
state leave nothing harvestable. Cycle 51 also repaired two defects in the rejection log — the missing
**R-025** entry (its identifier was consumed in Cycle 45 but never written) and the orphaned **R-029**
rationale that had been left under the R-030 entry — and corrected that log's own header counts against
the file (the header had claimed 28 rejections where the file held 29 entries, R-025 having none).

All entries were located and metadata-checked during Cycle 1 and Cycle 2 (both 2026-10-19) by web
search against publisher landing pages, DOI resolvers, regulator and provider sites, or the
publisher-hosted PDF. "Verification" records how the entry was confirmed, not whether the cited
paper's *results* are correct. Cycle 2 added the entries under "Cycle 2 additions", "Cycle 2
additions, part 2", "Cycle 2 additions, part 3" (the funding-currency and global-liquidity
corpus, S-055 … S-073) and "Cycle 2 additions, part 4" (the intraday FX fixing literature,
S-074 … S-081, added in the Cycle-2 G-09 closure pass); the source gaps section carries the
verdicts on every gap, including the ones that could not be closed, and the three mis-reported
identifiers caught in the G-09 pass.

Cycle 5 added "Cycle 5 additions" (S-095 … S-100), "Cycle 5 additions, part 2" (S-101 … S-111, the
  EU ETS family) and "Cycle 5 additions, part 3" (S-112 … S-125, the settlement-deadline cluster, the
T+1 window and the decision-time state), and the "Cycle-5 verdicts" block, which supersedes the
earlier gap verdicts for current-state purposes while leaving the original cycle-by-cycle gap text in
place at the end of the file. Cycle 5's decisive findings are recorded in the Cycle-5 verdicts block:
**G-10 is closed against IDEA-007** (the cross-sectional funding-state → currency-return claim is a
published, positive result — S-064 — and the mechanism's own literature has closed in the same
direction — S-095, S-096, S-097); **G-11 is closed against IDEA-006 and IDEA-012** (a settlement
deadline cluster is a published price determinant — S-112; the securities settlement cycle is a
published price-impact determinant — S-113, S-114, S-115; the post-US-close FX window is documented
as a cost rather than an edge — S-117, S-118, S-119, S-120); Cycle 5 re-opened the general question
as **G-12**: can a *published* state change the *sign* or *existence* of a published effect, or only
its measurement quality?; and Cycle 5's second general question, **G-13**: is an official state
usable as a *decision-time* variable, or only as an explanatory decomposition? (established instance:
the EU ETS aggregate balance is published in late May/June of the following year — S-121 — and was
  therefore unavailable at the pre-2023 surrender deadline). The register now holds **S-001 … S-125**.

re-derived the G-27 counts at **filer level** rather than by phrase (2,419 distinct broker-dealer
filers in the phrase corpus as a lower bound; 66 listed filers at quarterly and 96 at annual cadence;
the private-AP capital state **annual**; and **FR Y-9C is not indexed by EDGAR's full-text search at

Cycle 37 (2026-10-20) added **S-231 … S-238** (see "Cycle 37 sources" at the end of this file), the

and IDEA-014's state-free existence limb is confirmed inconclusive. Cycle 37 also logged the
Cycle-36 ex-dividend family screen **for real** (R-017) and ran a new mechanism family screen on
the **Rule 201 short-sale price-test circuit breaker**, a genuinely different template from the
compliance-deadline family, which is a **bounded negative** (R-018) because the effect is published

identifiers **S-210 … S-219** remain reserved and unused.

Status vocabulary used in dossiers:
- **Primary theory** — model or formal argument supporting the mechanism.
- **Prior empirical result (author-reported)** — the authors' own reported evidence. Not reproduced here.
- **Venue documentation** — primary rules/regulations/methodology documents.
- **Independent replication** — a separate team re-deriving the earlier result. None are claimed here.

---

### S-001 — Harris, L. and Gurel, E. (1986)
"Price and Volume Effects Associated with Changes in the S&P 500 List: New Evidence for the
Existence of Price Pressures." *The Journal of Finance* 41(4): 885–897.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1540-6261.1986.tb04550.x
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the price-pressure hypothesis — non-informational index-driven demand pushes prices
  above equilibrium and the effect partially unwinds; documents the announcement-day price and volume
  pattern around S&P 500 constituent changes.
- **Caveats:** small event sample; early-era index-fund scale; the magnitude of every price-pressure
  effect it estimates scales with the size of index-linked assets, which has grown by orders of
  magnitude since 1986.
- **Verification:** Wiley journal landing page (title, authors, abstract, date).

### S-002 — Beneish, M. D. and Whaley, R. E. (1996)
"An Anatomy of the 'S&P Game': The Effects of Changing the Rules." *The Journal of Finance* 51(5):
1909–1930.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1540-6261.1996.tb05231.x
Full text mirror used for reading: https://repec.udesa.edu.ar/pub/Finanzas/Journals/Journal%20of%20Finance/51/5/2329543.pdf
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the five-day pre-announcement regime and the "S&P game" — risk arbitrageurs buy after
  the announcement and sell into the index funds' effective-date demand; part of the price increase
  reverses after inclusion; also documents the overnight/intraday split of the effect.
- **Caveats:** sample covers 1986–1994 with a materially different index-fund asset base and a
  different index-provider rule set.
- **Verification:** publisher landing page (DOI) plus the full-text PDF.

### S-003 — Chen, H., Noronha, G. and Singal, V. (2004)
"The Price Response to S&P 500 Index Additions and Deletions: Evidence of Asymmetry and a New
Explanation." *The Journal of Finance* 59(4).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1540-6261.2004.00683.x
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the *asymmetry* — permanent price increase for additions, no permanent decline for
  deletions — and the investor-awareness explanation offered for it.
- **Caveats:** awareness is proposed, not identified; the deletion side is confounded by index funds
  that must sell into a one-sided order book.
- **Verification:** Wiley journal landing page (title, authors, abstract).

### S-004 — Woglom, G. (2002)
"Index Inclusion Effects on Stock Prices: Is There Any Evidence of a 'Holdings Effect'?" Federal
Reserve Board FEDS Paper 2002-48.
URL: https://www.federalreserve.gov/pubs/feds/2002/200248/200248pap.pdf
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** a model separating trading effects from fundamental effects on S&P 500 additions —
  an announcement price increase, a rise in post-addition volatility, and a growing fraction of the
  announcement gain reversed in subsequent weeks.
- **Caveats:** paper, not a journal article at the time of retrieval; reversal is partly attributed to
  a higher risk-adjusted discount rate rather than pure price pressure.
- **Verification:** Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis PDF (author, date, abstract, results text).

### S-005 — Chen, Y., Koutsantony, C., Truong, C. and Veeraraghavan, M. (2013)
"Stock price response to S&P 500 index inclusions: Do options listings and options trading volume
matter?" *Journal of International Financial Markets, Institutions and Money* 23: 379–401.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.intfin.2012.09.008
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** announcement abnormal returns for 344 S&P 500 inclusions, 1996–2010, and the finding
  that *abnormal options trading volume before* the announcement is associated with smaller
  announcement abnormal returns (i.e., options markets pre-price the event).
- **Caveats:** association, not a mechanism test; sample restricted to S&P 500 additions.
- **Verification:** ScienceDirect landing page and EconPapers record (title, authors, venue, DOI).

### S-006 — Dash, S. and Liu, B. (2008)
"Capturing the Index Effect via Options." SSRN Working Paper 1321725.
URL: https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=1321725
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported), non-refereed.
- **Supports:** the index effect is large in the *options* of affected stocks — at-the-money call
  premiums of 20–30× the corresponding stock price move, with a larger effect for additions from
  outside the S&P 1500 family; documents announcement-to-effective option returns.
- **Caveats:** both authors were affiliated with S&P at the time (stated in the paper), so treat as
  interested-party evidence; a working paper, not peer reviewed; reported strategy returns are the
  authors' own and are not reproduced here.
- **Verification:** SSRN abstract page (title, authors, date, abstract).

### S-007 — Hollstein, F. and Simen, C. W. (2021)
"The Index Effect: Evidence from the Option Market." Auckland University Finance and Research
Working Paper Series.
URL: https://acfr.aut.ac.nz/__data/assets/pdf_file/0020/530750/Announcement_paper.pdf
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported), working paper.
- **Supports:** delta-hedged option positions also respond to inclusion/exclusion news; the inclusion
  effect is permanent while the exclusion effect is transitory; the equity response is stronger for
  "outsider" additions (moving in from outside the S&P 400) than for intra-family promotions.
- **Caveats:** working paper; the authors' own test design (placebo and risk adjustment) is not
  independently audited.
- **Verification:** University of Auckland repository PDF (title, authors, date, abstract).

### S-008 — S&P Dow Jones Indices (venue documentation)
"S&P U.S. Indices Methodology" (index maintenance, timing of changes, announcements, share freeze,
rebalancing).
URL: https://www.spglobal.com/spdji/en/documents/methodologies/methodology-sp-us-indices.pdf
- **Type:** Venue documentation (primary rules).
- **Supports:** the institutional clock the mechanism depends on — constituent changes are typically
  announced **one to five days** before implementation; S&P 500 / MidCap 400 / SmallCap 600
  announcements are published at **05:15 PM ET**; changes take effect **after the close on the third
  Friday** of the quarter-end month; a "share freeze" applies in the week before the effective date.
- **Caveats:** the methodology is revised by the provider at its discretion; a specific event's rules
  must be read from the announcement itself and the version of the methodology in force at that date.
- **Verification:** provider-hosted PDF read directly (sections: Index Maintenance, Timing of Changes,
  Announcements, Rebalancing, Share Freeze).

### S-009 — Brunnermeier, M. K. and Pedersen, L. H. (2009)
"Market Liquidity and Funding Liquidity." *The Review of Financial Studies* 22(6): 2201–2238.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rfs/hhn098
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the coupling of market liquidity to funding liquidity; destabilising margins;
  liquidity spirals; and the specific predictions that speculators' capital and volatility act as
  *state variables* for market liquidity and risk premia, with a nonlinear effect when capital is
  already low.
- **Caveats:** a stylised model; the empirical margins evidence is historical (1987/1990/1998/2007
  crisis episodes) and documented largely from anecdote and public margin schedules.
- **Verification:** Oxford Academic landing page (volume, issue, pages, DOI) and author PDF.

### S-010 — Andersen, L., Duffie, D. and Song, Y. (2019)
"Funding Value Adjustments." *The Journal of Finance* 74(1): 145–192.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/jofi.12739
(NBER working paper version: https://www.nber.org/papers/w23680)
- **Type:** Primary theory.
- **Supports:** funding value adjustments (FVAs) and margin value adjustments (MVAs) are debt-overhang
  costs to a dealer's shareholders; dealer quotations therefore embed funding costs; this creates a
  measurable, structural wedge between fundamental value and quoted price, and it explains why
  covered-interest-parity violations persist.
- **Caveats:** the paper analyses OTC dealer balance sheets; it is a valuation-theory result, not
  evidence that any particular quoted spread is mispriced.
- **Verification:** RePEc record (journal, volume, issue, pages, DOI) plus the NBER working-paper page.

### S-011 — Cont, R., Kukanov, A. and Stoikov, S. (2014)
"The Price Impact of Order Book Events." *Journal of Financial Econometrics* 12(1): 47–88.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/jjfinec/nbt003
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported) + primary modelling.
- **Supports:** short-horizon price changes are driven mainly by order-flow imbalance (OFI), with a
  roughly linear impact relation whose slope is inversely proportional to market depth; a square-root
  volume/price-impact relation follows from a scaling argument but is noisier.
- **Caveats:** 50 US stocks on NYSE TAQ data over a specific period; impact coefficients are
  state-dependent and the paper does not model intermediary balance-sheet capacity.
- **Verification:** Oxford Academic landing page (volume, issue, pages, DOI) and the DOI record.

### S-012 — Carr, P. and Wu, L. (2009)
"Variance Risk Premia." *The Review of Financial Studies* 22(3): 1315–1341.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rfs/hhn038
- **Type:** Primary method + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the variance-swap rate is a near-exact model-free synthesis of option prices and
  approximates the risk-neutral expected variance; realized minus implied variance is the variance
  risk premium; the premium is strongly negative for US equity indices and individual stocks.
- **Caveats:** the authors' own sample of five indices and 35 stocks; the synthesis is an
  approximation with a small model error.
- **Verification:** Oxford Academic DOI record and the NYU-hosted article PDF (abstract, results).

### S-013 — Bollerslev, T., Tauchen, G. and Zhou, H. (2009)
"Expected Stock Returns and Variance Risk Premia." *The Review of Financial Studies* 22(11):
4463–4492.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rfs/hhp008
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the variance risk premium is a state variable for aggregate equity returns, with the
  strongest predictive power at a quarterly horizon; the premium is large relative to other common
  predictors; the result requires model-free implied variance and high-frequency realized variation.
- **Caveats:** the authors note the predictive power of other predictors has diminished over time;
  the 1990–2007 sample has limited crisis episodes.
- **Verification:** Oxford Academic landing page (volume, issue, pages, DOI) and the author PDF.

### S-014 — Bakshi, G., Kapadia, N. and Madan, D. B. (2003)
"Stock Return Characteristics, Skew Laws, and the Differential Pricing of Individual Equity
Options." *The Review of Financial Studies* 16(1): 101–143.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rfs/16.1.0101
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the negative skew of individual equity returns and the systematic pricing of
  individual options at higher implied volatility than index options, consistent with investor
  demand for insurance-like payoffs at the single-name level.
- **Caveats:** a model with hedging-demand primitives; the empirical single-name option results
  predate the post-2010 explosion in single-name option volume and zero-days-to-expiration trading.
- **Verification:** DOI and bibliographic details confirmed from the reference list of a retrieved
  peer-reviewed article; abstract content corroborated by multiple independent citing records.

### S-015 — Santa-Clara, P. and Yan, S. (2010)
"Crashes, Volatility, and the Equity Premium: Lessons from S&P 500 Options." *The Review of
Economics and Statistics* 92(2): 435–451.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1162/rest.2010.11549
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the equity risk premium is substantially higher in high-crash-risk states than in
  low-crash-risk states, and option-implied crash measures predict the *state* rather than a
  static premium — evidence that crash-linked risk is priced on average and rewarded asymmetrically.
- **Caveats:** the premium is a time-average of very fat-tailed states; a naive harvesting strategy
  is exposed to the same crash states that generate the premium.
- **Verification:** DOI and bibliographic details confirmed from the reference list of a retrieved
  peer-reviewed article (Kell & Jiang, SSRN 2019482).

### S-016 — Barro, R. J. (2006)
"Rare Disasters and Asset Prices in the Twentieth Century." *The Quarterly Journal of Economics*
121(3): 823–866.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1162/qjec.121.3.823
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the disaster-insurance framing of asset pricing — investors pay an insurance premium
  for protection against rare disasters, which is the canonical theoretical basis for a
  negatively-skewed risk premium and for the demand that creates it.
- **Caveats:** calibrated to a specific disaster-proxy data set; the insurance-price interpretation
  of a measured premium is a model-based inference, not a direct observation.
- **Verification:** DOI and bibliographic details confirmed from the reference list of a retrieved
  peer-reviewed article (Kell & Jiang, SSRN 2019482).

### S-017 — Acharya, V. V., Shin, H. S. and Yorulmazer, T. (2009)
"A Theory of Slow-Moving Capital and Contagion." (CEPR Discussion Paper 7147; NBER Finance WP
FIN-08-029; SSRN 1331610.)
DOI: https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.1331610
- **Type:** Primary theory.
- **Supports:** equilibrium with slow-moving capital — arbitrage capital only moves when
  fire-sale discounts are deep enough, producing occasional fire sales, limited stand-by capital,
  and contagion across fundamentally unrelated assets that draw on a common pool of arbitrage
  capital.
- **Caveats:** a stylised equilibrium model; the "stand-by capital only moves when discounts are deep"
  result is a theorem under specific assumptions, not a measured parameter.
- **Verification:** CEPR publication page (title, authors, date, abstract), SSRN record, and the NYU
  Faculty Digital Archive entry (FIN-08-029).

### S-018 — Pedersen, L. H., Mitchell, M. L. and Pulvino, T. C. (2007)
"Slow Moving Capital." *American Economic Review* 97(2): 215–218.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1257/aer.97.2.215
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the empirical fact-pattern that institutional capital moves slowly, so arbitrage
  opportunities persist and price pressures unwind only gradually.
- **Caveats:** a short AER Papers & Proceedings article; a fact-pattern rather than a full model.
- **Verification:** DOI and bibliographic details confirmed from the reference list of a retrieved
  peer-reviewed article (Acharya, Shin & Yorulmazer 2009).

### S-019 — Duffie, D. (2010)
"Presidential Address: Asset Price Dynamics with Slow-Moving Capital." *The Journal of Finance*
65(4): 1237–1267.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1540-6261.2010.01569.x
- **Type:** Primary theory.
- **Supports:** the canonical framework for prices with slow-moving capital — the price impact of
  a flow shock decays over time, the decay is governed by how fast capital reallocates, and
  deviations from fundamentals can persist for long horizons.
- **Caveats:** a survey/presidential address rather than a single hypothesis test.
- **Verification:** DOI and bibliographic details confirmed from the reference list of a retrieved
  peer-reviewed article.

### S-020 — He, Z. and Xiong, W. (2012)
"Delegated asset management, investment mandates, and capital immobility." *Journal of Financial
Economics* 107(2): 239–258.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jfineco.2012.08.010
- **Type:** Primary theory.
- **Supports:** investment mandates and career concerns make delegated capital immobile; managers
  trade toward their benchmark-relative objectives, producing flow-driven price pressure and
  comovement.
- **Caveats:** theory; the empirical content is the mechanism, not a test of any single strategy.
- **Verification:** DOI and bibliographic details confirmed from the reference list of a retrieved
  peer-reviewed article.

### S-021 — Malliaris, S. G. and Yan, H. (2021)
"Reputation Concerns and Slow-Moving Capital." *The Review of Asset Pricing Studies* 11(1).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rapstu/raab006
- **Type:** Primary theory.
- **Supports:** an explicit statement that capital is sometimes slow (leaving opportunities
  unexploited) and sometimes fast (relocating abruptly and moving prices with no fundamental news),
  with the switch driven by managers' reputation incentives.
- **Caveats:** theory; the "state-dependent speed of capital" is a model outcome, not a measured
  series.
- **Verification:** Oxford Academic landing page (title, authors, abstract, date, DOI).

### S-022 — Kondor, G. (2009)
"Risk in Dynamic Arbitrage: The Price Effects of Convergence Trading." *The Journal of Finance*
64(2): 631–655.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1540-6261.2009.01445.x
- **Type:** Primary theory.
- **Supports:** convergence-trading strategies are exposed to a crowding risk factor: as more capital
  runs the same convergence, the price effects that generate the profit shrink and the risk of
  simultaneous exit rises.
- **Caveats:** theory; the crowding factor is a model construct, not an observable index.
- **Verification:** DOI and bibliographic details confirmed from the reference list of a retrieved
  peer-reviewed article (Malliaris & Yan 2021).

### S-023 — Abreu, A. and Brunnermeier, M. K. (2002)
"Synchronization risk and delayed arbitrage." *Journal of Financial Economics* 66(2–3): 341–360.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/s0304-405x(02)00227-1
- **Type:** Primary theory.
- **Supports:** arbitragers who fail to act in time are forced into synchronised trades later,
  which raises the price of liquidity and amplifies deviations; the delay in arbitrage is
  self-reinforcing.
- **Caveats:** theory; the amplification result is model-dependent.
- **Verification:** DOI and bibliographic details confirmed from the reference list of a retrieved
  peer-reviewed article (Acharya, Shin & Yorulmazer 2009).

### S-024 — Vayanos, D. and Woolley, P. (2013)
"An Institutional Theory of Momentum and Reversal." *The Review of Financial Studies* 26(5):
1087–1145.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rfs/hht014
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** flows between funds generate momentum (through flow inertia and rational
  underreaction to expected future flows) and reversal (through flows pushing prices away from
  fundamental value) — the same institutional flow can produce both signs depending on the state.
- **Caveats:** a calibrated model; the Sharpe ratios quoted for momentum and value in the paper are
  model outputs, not measured performance.
- **Verification:** Oxford Academic landing page and the LSE Research Online record (volume, issue,
  pages, DOI).

### S-025 — Mitchell, M. L. and Pulvino, T. C. (2001)
"Characteristics of Risk and Return in Risk Arbitrage." *The Journal of Finance* 56(6):
2135–2175.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/0022-1082.00401
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the risk-arbitrageur population (merger-spread positions) is a measurable, sizeable
  agent whose capital is committed to convergence positions and who therefore has a systematic
  exposure to the continuation (not resolution) of the spread.
- **Caveats:** pre-2010 sample; today's merger-arb and index-arb capital is far larger and more
  heterogeneous than the 1990s population studied.
- **Verification:** DOI and bibliographic details confirmed from the reference list of a retrieved
  peer-reviewed article.

### S-026 — Brown, G. W., Howard, P. D. and Lundblad, C. T. (2021)
"Crowded Trades and Tail Risk." *The Review of Financial Studies* 34(7).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rfs/hhab107
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** security-level *crowdedness* can be measured from hedge fund holdings; the
  high-minus-low crowdedness spread is distinct from standard risk factors; exposure to crowdedness
  is associated with larger drawdowns during industry distress.
- **Caveats:** the crowding measure is built from 13F-style holdings with reporting lags (up to a
  quarter), so it is a slow, stale state variable; results are on hedge fund positions, not on all
  arbitrage capital.
- **Verification:** Oxford Academic landing page (title, authors, abstract, date, DOI).

### S-027 — Da, Z., Engelberg, J. and Gao, P. (2011)
"In Search of Attention." *The Journal of Finance* 66(5).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1540-6261.2011.01679.x
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** a direct, timestamped measure of investor attention (Google search volume); an
  attention increase predicts higher prices over the next two weeks and an eventual reversal within
  the year; the attention proxy is distinct from volume-based attention measures.
- **Caveats:** Russell 3000 sample 2004–2008; the Google search index is a retail-attention proxy and
  its coverage and stability have changed since 2010.
- **Verification:** Wiley landing page (title, authors, abstract, date) and the author-hosted PDF.

### S-028 — Lou, D., Polk, C. and Skouras, S. (2019)
"A tug of war: Overnight versus intraday expected returns." *Journal of Financial Economics*
134(1): 192–213.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jfineco.2019.03.011
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the decomposition of strategy premia into overnight and intraday components, and the
  finding that the *spread* between the two components forecasts time variation in the strategy's
  close-to-close performance — i.e., the clientele composition, not the signal, drives when a
  known anomaly is harvestable.
- **Caveats:** the authors note that transaction costs reduce the attractiveness of the raw
  overnight/intraday patterns; the t-statistics reported are the authors' own.
- **Verification:** ScienceDirect landing page, LSE Research Online record (volume, issue, pages,
  DOI).

## Cycle 2 additions (S-029 … S-043)

Added 2026-10-19. Same verification standard as Cycle 1: every entry was located and its metadata
checked against a publisher landing page, a DOI record, a repository record, or the document
itself. "Verification" records how the entry was confirmed, not whether the cited paper's
*results* are correct.

### S-029 — S&P Dow Jones Indices (venue documentation)
"S&P GSCI Methodology" (contract selection, contract weights, roll schedule, market-disruption
adjustment).
URL: https://www.spglobal.com/spdji/en/documents/methodologies/methodology-sp-gsci.pdf
- **Type:** Venue documentation (primary rules).
- **Supports:** the institutional clock the mechanism depends on — the S&P GSCI Spot Index rolls
  monthly from the **5th to the 9th business day**, in per-day proportions of 80/20, 60/40,
  40/60, 20/80 and 0/100 between the roll-out and roll-in contracts; a documented rule for
  *holding* roll weights if a market disruption event occurs; and the existence of sibling
  families with different conventions (S&P GSCI Pre-Roll, rolling 1st–5th business day; S&P GSCI
  Enhanced, with a partly dynamic, commodity-specific schedule).
- **Caveats:** the provider revises the methodology at its discretion, and the version in force on
  the event date governs; the Enhanced family is not the benchmark assumed in the candidate.
- **Verification:** document text read from the provider-hosted PDF through a text extraction of
  that exact URL (roll-weight tables and the page marker "S&P Dow Jones Indices: S&P GSCI
  Methodology 13"). A direct fetch of the PDF was refused by the publisher with HTTP 403 (bot
  protection); the identical roll schedule was cross-checked against the provider's "S&P GSCI
  Reference Guide" PDF (https://www.spglobal.com/spdji/en/documents/methodologies/methodology-sp-gsci-quick-guide.pdf),
  retrieved from the same domain.

### S-030 — Commodity Futures Trading Commission (regulator documentation)
"Commitments of Traders: Explanatory Notes" (Explanatory Notes for the COT reports, including the
Supplemental report and the "Index Traders" category).
URL: https://www.cftc.gov/MarketReports/CommitmentsofTraders/ExplanatoryNotes/index.htm
- **Type:** Venue documentation (regulator's own definitions).
- **Supports:** the participant, the constraint and the flow, stated by the regulator: the
  Supplemental report "shows an additional category of 'Index Traders' in selected agricultural
  markets", whose members are "generally replicating a commodity index by establishing long
  futures positions in the component markets and then rolling those positions forward from future
  to future using a fixed methodology". The page also states the contamination caveats — the
  category "will include some long and short positions where traders have multi-dimensional trading
  activities" and "will not include some traders who are engaged in index trading" — the
  reporting-threshold convention (reportable positions normally cover 70–90% of total open
  interest), the "Percent of Open Interest" definition, and the crop-year aggregation convention.
- **Caveats:** the Index Traders category exists only in the Supplemental report and, historically,
  only in selected agricultural markets; the definitional caveats above are material for any state
  variable built on it.
- **Verification:** page fetched and read directly; the quoted passages are verbatim from the page.

### S-031 — Commodity Futures Trading Commission (regulator documentation / data access)
"Commitments of Traders — Historical Compressed" (annual data files for the Legacy, Disaggregated
Futures-Only, Disaggregated Futures-and-Options-Combined, Traders in Financial Futures and
Supplemental "Commodity Index" reports) and the "COT Public Reporting Environment" (programmable
access to the same datasets).
URLs: https://www.cftc.gov/MarketReports/CommitmentsofTraders/HistoricalCompressed/index.htm ·
https://publicreporting.cftc.gov/
- **Type:** Venue documentation (data-access record).
- **Supports:** that the Disaggregated Futures-Only series is published as complete annual files
  "from September 2009", that the Supplemental commodity-index ("CIT") report is published as a
  distinct line for the covered markets, and that all of it is machine-readable and public.
- **Caveats:** the series start date bounds any cross-section; the exact report publication
  timing (the release schedule page,
  https://www.cftc.gov/MarketReports/CommitmentsofTraders/ReleaseSchedule/index.htm, is the
  authoritative reference and was **not** fetched this cycle) must be confirmed by a validator
  before any lag assumption is frozen.
- **Verification:** the Historical Compressed page and the COT Public Reporting Environment
  landing page were retrieved from cftc.gov and their dataset listings read; the definitional
  content was read directly on S-030.

### S-032 — Bessembinder, H., Carrion, A., Tuttle, L. and Venkataraman, K. (2016)
"Liquidity, resiliency and market quality around predictable trades: Theory and evidence."
*Journal of Financial Economics* 121(1): 142–166.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jfineco.2016.02.011
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the economics of trading *around* a predictable large order — competition among
  strategic traders improves market quality and increases the liquidator's proceeds; and the USO
  roll evidence: on roll days there are narrower bid-ask spreads, greater depth and improved
  resiliency; the order imbalance "is almost entirely reversed within one minute in the expiring
  contract and within three minutes in the second nearest-to-expiration contract on roll days";
  and "the large volume of trading associated with the roll transactions leads to substantial
  trade execution costs that average three percent per year".
- **Caveats:** one fund (USO) in one contract (WTI); uses CFTC account-level data, which is not
  publicly reproducible; the 2016 vintage predates the 2018–2025 commodity structure.
- **Verification:** publisher landing page (volume, issue, pages, DOI, abstract) plus the
  author-hosted working PDF (https://www.ou.edu/content/dam/price/Finance/energyfinanceconference/Liquidity%20and%20Market%20Quality%20around%20Predictable%20Trades.pdf)
  and the SMU-hosted journal PDF.

### S-033 — Irwin, S. H., Sanders, D. R. and Yan, L. (2023)
"The order flow cost of index rolling in commodity futures markets." *Applied Economic
Perspectives and Policy* 45(2): 1025–1050.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1002/aepp.13297
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the roll treated as a natural experiment; the spread between nearby and deferred
  futures prices "decreases significantly in the early and growth phases of financialization
  (1991–2011), with the spreads reversing back after rolling is completed"; and — decisive for
  this candidate — "spread impacts disappear in the post-financialization period (2012–2019)", with
  the authors attributing the decline to the increase in liquidity supply from electronic trading.
- **Caveats:** the set of futures markets studied is specific; the attribution to electronification
  is the authors' argument rather than an identified mechanism; the result is an aggregate
  statement over a panel that is dominated by liquid contracts.
- **Verification:** publisher landing page (volume, issue, pages, DOI, abstract, publication
  history: received 23 July 2021, accepted 13 May 2022, version of record 8 June 2022) plus the
  RePEc/IDEAS record.

### S-034 — Yan, L., Irwin, S. H. and Sanders, D. R. (2022)
"Sunshine vs. predatory trading effects in commodity futures markets: New evidence from index
rebalancing." *Journal of Commodity Markets* 26: 100195.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jcomm.2021.100195
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the S&P GSCI annual rebalancing as a natural experiment for predictable index
  order flow; the finding that "the impact of order flows from financial investors on commodity
  futures prices is modest and temporary, consistent with the prediction of sunshine trading
  theory"; that the induced flows are economically significant ("often exceeding several hundred
  million dollars of notional value"); and the authors' statement that their finding of little or
  no price impact during front-running periods is inconsistent with predatory-trading theory.
- **Caveats:** the *annual* rebalancing, not the monthly roll; 24 commodities over 2004–2019; the
  "modest and temporary" characterisation is the authors' own.
- **Verification:** publisher landing page (abstract text) and the RePEc/IDEAS record (volume,
  article number, DOI, keywords).

### S-035 — Mou, Y. (2011)
"Limits to Arbitrage and Commodity Index Investment: Front-Running the Goldman Roll." PhD
dissertation, Columbia University, 15 July 2011; working-paper version hosted by the CFTC as
Public Law and Economics study 33.
URLs: https://www.cftc.gov/sites/default/files/idc/groups/public/@swaps/documents/file/plstudy_33_yu.pdf ·
https://academiccommons.columbia.edu/doi/10.7916/D8DR32JG/download
SSRN: https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.1716841
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported), **non-refereed** (doctoral thesis / working
  paper).
- **Supports:** the description of the mechanism that the practitioner call "the Goldman roll" —
  "the SP-GSCI rolls futures forward from the fifth to the ninth business day in each month" —
  and the claim that the mechanical rolling "exerts large and time-varying price pressure on the
  relative prices of the contracts involved"; and the state-dependence that is the pivot of the
  present candidate: "the profitability of the strategies is positively correlated with the net
  result of two opposite forces: the size of index investment and the amount of arbitrage capital
  employed".
- **Caveats:** not peer reviewed; the sample ends March 2010; the reported Sharpe ratios and
  returns are the author's own and are **not** reproduced here; the headline result is contradicted
  by later peer-reviewed work (S-032, S-033, S-037).
- **Verification:** the CFTC-hosted PDF read (title, author, date, abstract, and the §3.1
  description of the Goldman roll); the SSRN abstract page (DOI, abstract, revision dates); and
  the Columbia Academic Commons dissertation record (title, author, degree, 2011).

### S-036 — Stoll, H. R. and Whaley, R. E. (2010)
"Commodity Index Investing and Commodity Futures Prices." *Journal of Applied Finance* 20(1): 7–46.
Working-paper version hosted by the CFTC (study 45). SSRN: https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.1478195
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the contrary finding that anchors the contested state of the literature — "(a)
  commodity index investing is not speculation; (b) commodity index rolls have little futures price
  impact, and inflows and outflows from commodity index investment do not cause futures prices to
  change"; and the scale of the passive complex, measured on 30 June 2009: 170,256 contracts of
  long open interest categorised as commodity index traders across 33 markets, about $161bn
  notional, roughly 17% of the open interest in those markets, of which 21.8% were direct fund
  positions and the remainder swap-dealer hedging of OTC index exposure.
- **Caveats:** the snapshot is from 2009, before the passive complex reached its later scale; the
  CIT measure mixes direct fund positions with swap-dealer hedges; the study was written in the
  context of a Senate staff report, and is the authors' own assessment.
- **Verification:** the CFTC-hosted PDF read (executive summary and Panels A–C, including the
  170,256 / $161bn / 17% figures) and the SSRN record (journal, volume, issue, pages, abstract).

### S-037 — Hamilton, J. D. and Wu, J. C. (2015)
"Effects of Index-Fund Investing on Commodity Futures Prices." *International Economic Review*
56(1): 187–205. NBER Working Paper 19892.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.3386/w19892
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the second contrary finding — under a futures-arbitrage model in which index-fund
  buying affects prices by changing the risk premium, index-investor notional positions should
  predict excess returns, but the authors "find no evidence that the positions of traders in
  agricultural contracts identified by the CFTC as following an index strategy can help predict
  returns on the near futures contracts", and the oil relation, while partly supported in the
  earlier data, "appears to be driven by some of the dramatic features of the 2007-2009 recession,
  with the relation breaking down out of sample". Also documents the descriptive scale ("up to
  half of the open interest in outstanding agricultural commodity futures contracts was held by
  institutions characterized by the CFTC as commodity index traders", citing Stoll & Whaley) and
  the reporting convention for the SCOT data (released Friday for positions as of the preceding
  Tuesday).
- **Caveats:** 2015 publication; the oil sub-result is period-specific; all findings are the
  authors' own.
- **Verification:** NBER working-paper page (DOI, issue date, abstract, published-version
  citation) plus the NBER PDF and the UCSD author PDF.

### S-038 — Gorton, G. B., Hayashi, F. and Rouwenhorst, K. G. (2013)
"The Fundamentals of Commodity Futures Returns." *Review of Finance* 17(1): 35–105.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rof/rfs019
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the state variable this candidate's carry bound must be measured against —
  commodity futures risk premia vary with the level of physical inventories, the convenience
  yield is a decreasing, nonlinear function of inventories, and "price measures, such as the
  futures basis, prior futures returns, prior spot returns, and spot price volatilities reflect
  the state of inventories and are informative about commodity futures risk premia"; and a
  directly relevant caution for the candidate's state variable: the authors "find no evidence
  that the positions of participants in futures markets predict risk premiums on commodity
  futures".
- **Caveats:** 31 commodities, 1971–2010; a structural model; the finding about positions is
  about *risk premiums*, not about *price pressure around a flow*.
- **Verification:** publisher landing page (volume, issue, pages, DOI, abstract) and the
  RePEc/EconPapers records.

### S-039 — Bessembinder, H. (1992)
"Systematic Risk, Hedging Pressure, and Risk Premiums in Futures Markets." *Review of Financial
Studies* 5(4): 637–667.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rfs/5.4.637
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the rival mechanism that must be excluded before a spread move is attributed to
  the passive roll — hedging pressure: "returns in foreign currency and agricultural futures vary
  with the net holdings of hedgers, after controlling for systematic risk", supporting market
  segmentation between futures and asset markets.
- **Caveats:** 1992 vintage; a rival explanation rather than support for the candidate.
- **Verification:** publisher landing page (volume, issue, pages, DOI, abstract) and the JSTOR
  record.

### S-040 — Investment Company Institute (industry data publication)
"Trends in Mutual Fund Investing" (monthly statistical report) and the ICI statistics landing
page, which also lists the monthly "Combined Active and Index Mutual Funds and ETFs" report.
URLs: https://www.ici.org/research/stats · https://www.ici.org/research/stats/trends_08_26
- **Type:** Venue documentation (industry data publication).
- **Supports:** the closest *public, citable* series for the **scale of passive assets** that the
  index-reconstitution and roll mechanisms scale with — monthly total net assets of US mutual
  funds and the monthly active-versus-index report — and therefore the practical substitute for
  the missing benchmark-specific series (see G-07).
- **Caveats:** the headline totals explicitly exclude ETFs and funds that invest primarily in
  other mutual funds; the active/index split is **category-level**, not benchmark-specific; the
  series measures US-domiciled fund assets, not global assets tracking an index.
- **Verification:** the statistics landing page and two monthly release pages retrieved and read
  (totals, the exclusion footnote, the publication dates, and the listing of the combined
  active/index report).

### S-041 — Federal Reserve Bank of New York (regulator/venue documentation)
"Secured Overnight Financing Rate (SOFR) Data" and the related reference-rate pages (Broad General
Collateral Rate, Tri-Party General Collateral Rate, SOFR Averages & Index).
URL: https://www.newyorkfed.org/markets/reference-rates/sofr
- **Type:** Venue documentation (official rate publication).
- **Supports:** a **daily, public** measure of the cost of overnight Treasury-collateralised
  funding, published each business day at approximately 8:00 a.m. ET — the most direct candidate
  ingredient for the public funding-state proxy that IDEA-002 lacks (source gap G-01).
- **Caveats:** SOFR is a secured Treasury rate, not a dealer funding cost, and in normal times
  it is a policy-set administered rate rather than a market-clearing price; it is a *state
  indicator*, not a substitute for disclosed funding value adjustments.
- **Verification:** page fetched and read directly (definition, publication timing, and the
  related-series list).

### S-042 — Federal Reserve Bank of New York (regulator/venue documentation)
"Repo Operations" (daily standing-repo-facility and reverse-repo operation results) and
"Primary Dealer Statistics" (regular public balance-sheet disclosure of the primary dealers).
URLs: https://www.newyorkfed.org/markets/desk-operations/repo ·
https://www.newyorkfed.org/markets/counterparties/primary-dealers-statistics
- **Type:** Venue documentation (official operations and counterparty disclosure).
- **Supports:** the two remaining public ingredients of a funding-state proxy — daily evidence
  of *funding stress* (standing-repo take-up and awarded rates) and a public, recurring
  balance-sheet disclosure of the primary dealers, i.e. the intermediaries whose funding value
  adjustments IDEA-002 would need to be validated against.
- **Caveats:** standing-repo take-up is distorted by stigma and facility design, so it is an
  imperfect stress gauge; primary dealer statistics are quarterly and aggregated across firms, so
  they cannot validate a *daily* FVA series.
- **Verification:** the Repo Operations page was retrieved (description of daily repo and reverse
  repo operations and their results); the Primary Dealer Statistics path was confirmed from the
  New York Fed markets index — an earlier guessed path returned "Page Not Found" and was
  corrected to /markets/counterparties/primary-dealers-statistics. The release contents of the
  Primary Dealer Statistics data files were **not** inspected this cycle.

### S-043 — Cboe Global Markets (venue documentation / exchange research commentary)
"Volatility Insights: Much Ado About 0DTEs" (8 September 2023); "0DTEs Decoded: Positioning,
Trends, and Market Impact" (2 May 2025); "SPX 0DTE Options Jump to Record 62% Share in August"
(2 September 2025); and the 0DTE product resources page.
URLs: https://www.cboe.com/insights/posts/volatility-insights-evaluating-the-market-impact-of-spx-0dte-options/ ·
https://www.cboe.com/insights/posts/0-dt-es-decoded-positioning-trends-and-market-impact/ ·
https://www.cboe.com/insights/posts/spx-0-dte-options-jump-to-record-62-share-in-august/ ·
https://www.cboe.com/tradable-products/0dte/
- **Type:** Venue documentation (exchange-published research and statistics).
- **Supports:** the characterisation of the post-2010 change in option market structure that
  source gap G-05 concerns — SPX zero-days-to-expiration share of SPX options volume rising from
  about 5% in 2016 to over 40% after the introduction of Tuesday/Thursday expiries in 2022, about
  50% in August 2023, and 59–62% in 2025; retail estimated at roughly 50–60% of SPX 0DTE volume;
  about 98% of SPX 0DTE volume traded electronically; more than 95% of 0DTE customer volume in
  limited-risk formats with only 4% in naked short options; and the exchange's own estimate that
  net market-maker gamma hedging is de minimis (about 0.2% of SPX daily liquidity in the 2025
  note; net gamma exposure of $170–670 million through the day in the 2023 note).
- **Caveats:** exchange-published research is interested-party commentary, not peer-reviewed
  evidence; the gamma and retail-share figures are the exchange's own estimates; 0DTE is primarily
  an *index*-option phenomenon and single-name 0DTE adoption is later and smaller, so this
  source does not by itself settle the single-name question.
- **Verification:** the four pages were retrieved and read; the figures and dates quoted above are
  as stated on the pages.

## Cycle 2 additions, part 2 (S-044 … S-054) — the two non-equity venue families

### S-044 — Financial Stability Board (international regulator)
"FX Benchmarks: Final Report" (30 September 2014) and the preceding "Foreign Exchange Benchmarks
Consultative Document" (15 July 2014).
URLs: https://www.fsb.org/uploads/r_140930.pdf · https://www.fsb.org/uploads/r_140715.pdf
- **Type:** Venue documentation (regulator report).
- **Supports:** the participant set, the deadline and the *size* of the flow in the end-of-day
  fixing window — "The WM/Reuters (WMR) 4pm London fix … is by far the dominant benchmark being
  used, not just in FX, but also as a key input in multi-currency equity, bond and credit
  indices"; "Most investment mandates are benchmarked against global equity indices (eg MSCI),
  bond indices (eg Barclays, BAML, Citi, JP Morgan) or credit indices (Markit) that use the WMR
  4pm London fixing for FX valuation and transaction purposes"; "While management type pension
  trusts commonly reference the WMR 4pm London fix to execute FX transactions, investment trusts
  tend to conduct transactions following the judgment of fund managers without referencing FX
  benchmarks"; and the quantitative anchor — "For several currencies, there are a few days when the
  1-minute WMR 4pm London trading window can account for over 10% of the platform's daily trading
  volume". The report also lists "the behaviour of market participants around the time of the
  major FX benchmarks (primarily the WM 4pm London fix)" as an explicit area of recommendations.
- **Caveats:** the report is a 2014 policy document, so its market-structure description is a
  decade old and predates the T+1 transition and the 2022 rate shock; it documents the
  *existence* of concentrated fixing-window flow, not its price impact.
- **Verification:** both PDFs retrieved and read (text as quoted); the final report's publication
  date and title are on the FSB's own domain.

### S-045 — LSEG / FTSE International (benchmark administrator and venue documentation)
"WMR FX Benchmarks Methodology" (ground rules PDF) and the administrator's product documentation
"The global standard for currency valuation" (October 2024 and June 2025 versions).
URLs: https://www.lseg.com/content/dam/ftse-russell/en_us/documents/ground-rules/wmr-fx-methodology.pdf ·
https://www.lseg.com/content/dam/ftse-russell/en_us/documents/other/wmr-fx-benchmarks-june-2025.pdf
- **Type:** Venue documentation (benchmark rules).
- **Supports:** the published clock — closing spot rates "Fixed daily Monday–Friday at 4:00 pm
  London time" with coverage of 150+ currencies against the EUR, GBP and USD, closing forwards
  and NDFs at the same time; the calculation window was "extended from 1–5 minutes" on 15 February
  2015 following the FSB/IOSCO recommendations; the FCA "brings 16:00 WMR fix into regulatory
  regime"; the administrator is authorised under the EU Benchmark Regulation; and the
  administrator's published service-alteration notices (for example the Cboe Europe notice
  confirming that "WM/Refinitiv (WMR) FX Benchmark Fixing at 16:00 London will not be published"
  on specified holidays) — i.e. the calendar of non-trading fix days is published.
- **Caveats:** the methodology is revised at the administrator's discretion and the version in
  force on the date governs; the June 2025 and October 2024 decks differ in coverage figures, so
  a validator must pin the version used.
- **Verification:** the methodology PDF and both product PDFs were retrieved and read; the quoted
  wording is as printed in them.

### S-046 — Debelle, G. (2015)
"FX benchmarks", speech delivered at the BIS (13 February 2015), published in the BIS Quarterly
Review.
URL: https://www.bis.org/review/r150213c.htm
- **Type:** Venue documentation (central-bank address).
- **Supports:** the historical construction of the clock — "The longstanding approach … has used a
  set of foreign exchange rates calculated at a particular time of day, generally 4pm London, the
  London 'fix', which is published by WM/Reuters (WMR)"; that "for a number of years, WM has
  calculated the fix over a one minute window centred on 4pm"; the IOSCO/FSB recommendation to
  widen the window to five minutes from 15 February; and the four areas of reform (methodology,
  execution infrastructure, market conduct, central bank reference rates).
- **Caveats:** a speech, not peer-reviewed; it describes the benchmark process, not price impact.
- **Verification:** page retrieved and read; quotations as printed.

### S-047 — Benenchia, M. and co-authors (2024)
"To fix or not to fix: The representativeness of the WM/R methodology that underpins the FX
benchmark rates. A pre-registered report." *Pacific-Basin Finance Journal* 84: 102311.
URLs: https://cris.unibo.it/retrieve/handle/11585/965113/b85e0356-bd09-4bac-b2b5-9b023f8ebe97/1-s2.0-S0927538X24000623-main.pdf ·
https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0927538X24000623
- **Type:** Primary empirical result (author-reported), pre-registered.
- **Supports:** the peer-reviewed treatment of intraday FX market quality *around* the fix: the
  paper analyses the time directly surrounding each daily 4pm fix using "all order book events
  from all the platforms used as data sources for the benchmarks calculated by Refinitiv", and
  states that (Osler & Ronde, and) "Michelberger and Witte (2016) support similar evidence in
  their studies on intraday foreign exchange rates and the inter-dealer order flow relationship
  around the WM/R 4 pm London Fix"; it also records the 2015 methodology revision following the
  FSB consultation.
- **Caveats:** the tick data are proprietary and were provided by the benchmark administrator, so
  the result is not independently reproducible from public data; **the author list was completed in
  the G-09 closure pass** — the SSRN records for the 2024 pre-registered report (SSRN 4718425) and
  for the 2025 follow-up (SSRN 4958561) show the same three authors, Benenchia, Galati and Lepone
  (see S-080), so the Cycle-2 caveat that "the full author list beyond the first author was not
  verified" is now closed; the paper is about the *representativeness of the benchmark*, not about a
  tradeable price-pressure effect.
- **Verification:** the open-access PDF (University of Bologna repository) and the author copy
  (Macquarie University research repository) were retrieved and read; the journal, volume and
  article number were read from the PDF header.

### S-048 — CLS Group (settlement utility documentation)
"T+1, the FX ecosystem, and CLS: What a difference a day makes" (*Shaping FX*, part 1),
30 October 2024.
URL: https://www.cls-group.com/media/ipencxv4/shapingfx06_part1_tplus1-the-fx-ecosystem-and-cls-what-difference-a-day-makes.pdf
- **Type:** Venue documentation (settlement utility's own publication).
- **Supports:** the *settlement-side* deadline — the CLS operating schedule (00:00 CET initial
  pay-in schedule, 06:30 CET revised pay-in schedule, 07:00 CET start of settlement, 09:00 CET
  settlement completion target, currency close deadlines at 12:00 and 13:00 CET, unilateral and
  bilateral rescind deadlines); the T+1 constraint — "In the US example, when the US equity market
  closes at 16:00 EST (22:00 CET), time is limited for the associated currency trading. To align
  to the T+1 securities schedule, FX instructions should be submitted to CLS by the beginning of
  the day of settlement – 00:00 CET on day T+1 … In extremis, there is a reduced window of only
  two hours to arrange the submission to CLS"; and the netting statistic "CLS settlement members
  fund only around 1% of the total value of their payment instructions on a typical day".
- **Caveats:** the publication is the settlement utility's own communication (an interested
  party), not an independent study; it describes the schedule and its consequences but does not
  measure price impact.
- **Verification:** PDF retrieved and read; quoted figures and times as printed.

### S-049 — CLS Bank International (settlement utility rules)
"CLS Bank International Rules", 21 July 2025.
URL: https://www.cls-group.com/media/cwzk1bjy/cls-bank-international-rules_21_july_2025.pdf
- **Type:** Venue documentation (binding rules).
- **Supports:** the *hard* constraint — the rulebook's defined deadlines: the "Currency Close
  Deadline" at which the CLS System will, in the ordinary course of business, reject instructions
  on the Settlement Processing Queue that have not settled; the "Approved Payment System Closing
  Time"; the CLSNow cut-off; and the consequence of failure ("The CLS System will reject each
  CLSNow Instruction that does not Settle by its CLSNow Cut-off Time").
- **Caveats:** the rules are continuously amended, so the version in force on the validation date
  governs.
- **Verification:** PDF retrieved; definitions read as printed.

### S-050 — King, M. R., Osler, C. L. and Rime, D. (2013)
"The market microstructure approach to foreign exchange: Looking back and looking forward."
*Journal of International Money and Finance* 38: 95–119. DOI:
https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jimonfin.2013.05.004
Earlier version: "Foreign exchange market structure, players and evolution", Norges Bank Working
Paper 2011/10 (SSRN 1935858), published as a chapter in the *Handbook of Exchange Rates*
(chapter 1, "Foreign Exchange Market Structure, Players, and Evolution").
- **Type:** Primary theory + survey of prior results.
- **Supports:** the market-structure facts the candidate rests on — the size and composition of the
  market, the motives, incentives and constraints of the major players, and the structural changes
  of the preceding decades; the abstract's framing — "Research on foreign exchange market
  microstructure stresses the importance of order flow, heterogeneity among agents, and private
  information as crucial determinants of short-run exchange rate dynamics".
- **Caveats:** a survey chapter, so its claims are second-hand for the individual results; the
  2013 vintage predates the T+1 transition and the 2022 rate shock.
- **Verification:** publisher landing pages (Wiley, ScienceDirect), the RePEc/IDEAS record
  (volume, pages, DOI) and the SSRN record for the working-paper version were all retrieved; the
  survey's own PDF is openly posted by the authors.

### S-051 — Rime, D., Sarno, L. and Sojli, E. (2010)
"Exchange rate forecasting, order flow and macroeconomic information." *Journal of International
Economics* 80(1): 72–88. DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jinteco.2009.03.005
Working-paper versions: Norges Bank Working Paper 2007/02; CEPR Discussion Paper 7225.
- **Type:** Primary empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the intraday/microstructure structure of the FX market and the documented link
  between order flow and exchange rates — "order flow is related to current and expected future
  macroeconomic fundamentals, and can profitably forecast risk-adjusted currency returns" — and
  the market-scale figure that "the FX market is by far the largest financial market in the world,
  with a daily turnover of US dollar 3210 billion, a third of which is in spot transactions".
- **Caveats:** the headline result concerns the forecasting power of order flow, not the fixing
  window; the turnover figure is from the 2007 BIS triennial survey vintage and is quoted as
  such.
- **Verification:** publisher landing page (ScienceDirect, volume/issue/pages/DOI/abstract), the
  RePEc/IDEAS record, and the working-paper PDFs from Norges Bank and the Bank of Canada were
  retrieved.

### S-052 — Adrian, T., Etula, E. and Shin, H. S. (2009, revised December 2015)
"Risk Appetite and Exchange Rates." Federal Reserve Bank of New York Staff Report No. 361
(January 2009; revised December 2015; previously circulated as "Global Liquidity and Exchange
Rates").
URLs: https://www.newyorkfed.org/research/staff_reports/sr361.html ·
https://www.newyorkfed.org/medialibrary/media/research/staff_reports/sr361.pdf ·
SSRN: https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.1338121
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported; a staff report, not
  peer-reviewed).
- **Supports:** the funding-liquidity channel in FX — "We present evidence that the growth of
  U.S.-dollar-denominated banking sector liabilities forecasts appreciations of the U.S. dollar,
  both in-sample and out-of-sample, against a large set of foreign currencies"; "We provide a
  theoretical foundation for a funding liquidity channel in a global banking model where exchange
  rates fluctuate as a function of banks' balance sheet capacity"; "Our empirical evidence shows
  that this channel is separate from the more familiar 'carry trade' channel"; and the caveat
  that "the financial crisis of 2007-09 induced a structural shift in our forecasting variables,
  when we control for this shift, the forecasting relationship is preserved".
- **Caveats:** a staff report (peer review is informal); the sample is largely pre-2010, so the
  2020 and 2022 episodes are outside it; the state is built from aggregates with a lag, and the
  candidate must specify its own construction; the authors' own results are not reproduced here.
- **Verification:** the NY Fed landing page (title, authors, report number, revision dates, JEL
  codes, abstract), the staff-report PDF (title page and abstract) and the SSRN record (DOI,
  abstract) were all retrieved and read.

### S-053 — Adrian, T., Etula, E. and Groen, J. J. (2011)
"Financial amplification of foreign exchange risk premia." *European Economic Review* 55(3):
354–370.
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the peer-reviewed version of the same authors' intermediary-capacity mechanism in
  FX, i.e. the amplification of FX risk premia by financial intermediaries' balance-sheet
  capacity, which is the closest peer-reviewed anchor for the funding-liquidity channel.
- **Caveats:** the record retrieved is a bibliographic one (RePEc/IDEAS listing of the article as
  cited in S-052); the article itself was **not** read in this cycle, and the page range and
  issue should be confirmed against the publisher before formal citation.
- **Verification:** title, authors, journal, volume, issue and page range read from the RePEc
  reference list of S-052's IDEAS record; the article text was not retrieved.

### S-054 — Bank for International Settlements (international organization data)
Locational banking statistics (data portal, tables and overview) and the regular statistical
releases "BIS international banking statistics and global liquidity indicators".
URLs: https://data.bis.org/topics/LBS/data · https://www.bis.org/statistics/bankstats.htm ·
https://www.bis.org/statistics/rppb2510.htm
- **Type:** Venue documentation (official statistics).
- **Supports:** the *published state variable* for the funding-liquidity channel — cross-border
  positions by location of the banking office, by currency denomination, by residence and by
  sector of counterparty, published quarterly on a fixed release calendar, together with the
  global liquidity indicators; the BIS's own note that "dollars are increasingly raised in the
  home country" and that "cross-border liabilities play a large role" in where dollar funding is
  raised; and the publication mechanics (quarterly frequency, stated release dates, machine-
  readable bulk downloads in CSV and SDMX).
- **Caveats:** quarterly frequency and a reporting lag; the locational view is not the same as the
  consolidated view, and revisions occur; the global liquidity indicator definitions are
  constructed by the BIS and should be read before use.
- **Verification:** the BIS Data Portal locational banking statistics pages and the statistical
  release page were retrieved and read; the release-cadence and unit information was read from the
  overview page.
