## Cycle 2 additions, part 3 (S-055 … S-073) — the funding-currency and global-liquidity corpus

Added 2026-10-19, after a dedicated source-verification pass on the funding-currency family. This
corpus closes most of source gap G-10 and **materially changes IDEA-007's classification**: the
funding-state → carry-return relation is a *published baseline with a positive result*, not a gap.

**Verification note applying to this block.** Entries S-055 and S-059 were re-checked by the lead
session directly against the Crossref record (title, authors, container, volume, issue, pages or
article number, publication date, DOI, and for S-059 the deposited abstract). The remaining entries
were verified by a delegated read-only verification agent on 2026-10-19, which fetched each landing
page or record and read the metadata string on it; several of their bibliographic details are
independently corroborated by the reference lists inside the two Crossref records fetched by the
lead session (S-055's reference list confirms Borio et al. 2016 *BIS Quarterly Review*, Baba et al.
2009 *JBF* 33(3):1953, Avdjiev et al. 2019 *AER: Insights* 1(2):193, Du–Tepper–Verdelhan 2018 *JF*
73(2):915, Menkhoff et al. 2012 *JF* 67:681, Bruno & Shin 2015 *REStud* 82(2):535 and *JME* 71:119,
Ivashina et al. 2015 *QJE* 130(3):1241, Brunnermeier–Nagel–Pedersen 2008 *NBER Macroeconomics
Annual* 23:313). Where a source's own text was not read, that is stated.

### S-055 — Filipe, S. F., Nissinen, J. and Suominen, M. (2023)
"Currency carry trades and global funding risk." *Journal of Banking & Finance* 149: 106800
(April 2023).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jbankfin.2023.106800
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** — and this is the decisive entry for IDEA-007 — the *funding-constraint state is
  already used as a conditioning variable for carry returns, with a positive result*. The authors
  measure funding constraints by deviations from covered interest parity and define funding risk
  as the standard deviation of the magnitude of those constraints, and find that funding risk is
  driven by financial-sector conditions in low-interest-rate countries, the oil price and major
  central bank actions, and that since 2008 it has affected carry trading activity, carry trade
  returns, carry-long/short correlations, relative equity returns and growth. Their model predicts
  that "when funding constraints start to bind, both the investment and the funding currency crash
  relative to a safe asset".
- **Caveats:** the exchange-rate and forward inputs are Reuters (WM/R) on Datastream (daily spot
  and 3-month forward) — a paid vendor source — and the authors note that CIP-deviation
  calculations are sensitive to small perturbations, so they restrict to a single data source; the
  published sample ends in June 2021.
- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched directly by the lead session (title, three authors,
  container, volume 149, article 106800, published 2023-04, DOI, 33 deposited references).

### S-056 — Borio, C., McCauley, R. N., McGuire, P. and Sushko, V. (2016)
"Covered interest parity lost: understanding the cross-currency basis." *BIS Quarterly Review*,
September 2016, pp. 45–64.
URL: https://www.bis.org/publ/qtrpdf/r_qt1609e.htm
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported; central-bank publication).
- **Supports:** the mechanism behind the state variable — covered interest parity has been
  "systematically violated since the Great Financial Crisis", the post-2014 violations are the
  puzzling ones because banks had regained balance-sheet capacity, and the violations are
  attributed to the combination of dollar hedging demand and tighter limits to arbitrage, i.e.
  balance-sheet constraints; hedging-demand indicators built from BIS banking and debt-securities
  data track the basis.
- **Caveats:** the authors' views, not the BIS's; the article is a synthesis with supporting
  estimates, and the companion working paper (S-057) carries the regression detail.
- **Verification:** landing page and PDF fetched and read by the verification agent; the page range
  is corroborated by the reference list of the S-055 Crossref record and by a BIS-authored VoxEU
  column.

### S-057 — Borio, C., Iqbal, M., McCauley, R. N., McGuire, P. and Sushko, V. (2016, rev. 2018)
"The failure of covered interest parity: FX hedging demand and costly balance sheets." *BIS
Working Papers* No 590 (27 October 2016; revised November 2018).
URL: https://www.bis.org/publ/work590.htm
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the regression-level evidence behind S-056 — CIP deviations not attributable to
  transaction costs or bank credit risk can be explained by the demand to hedge USD forward, and
  because committing balance sheet to CIP arbitrage is costly, arbitrageurs charge a premium in the
  forward market for taking the other side of hedgers' demand; hedging-demand measures combined
  with CIP-arbitrage risk proxies improve explanatory power.
- **Caveats:** the authors' views; no DOI is deposited for the BIS working-paper series.
- **Verification:** landing page fetched and read by the verification agent (series, number, date
  published, author list, abstract).

### S-058 — Baba, N. and Packer, F. (2009)
"From turmoil to crisis: dislocations in the FX swap market before and after the failure of Lehman
Brothers." *BIS Working Papers* No 285 (7 July 2009).
URL: https://www.bis.org/publ/work285.htm
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the crisis-period evidence that the funding state is *systemic* — after Lehman's
  failure, CIP deviations were negatively associated with the creditworthiness of US financial
  institutions, consistent with a dollar liquidity problem becoming a global phenomenon, and the
  term-funding auctions of the ECB, SNB and BoE and the Fed's dollar swap lines ameliorated the
  dislocations.
- **Caveats:** the authors' views; a 2009 crisis-sample paper, so it is evidence about the
  extreme state rather than about a tradable average relation.
- **Verification:** landing page and PDF fetched and read by the verification agent.

### S-059 — Avdjiev, S., Du, W., Koch, C. and Shin, H. S. (2019)
"The Dollar, Bank Leverage, and Deviations from Covered Interest Parity." *American Economic
Review: Insights* 1(2): 193–208 (September 2019).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1257/aeri.20180322
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the triangular relationship that underpins the "dollar as barometer of risk-taking
  capacity" framing — "We document a triangular relationship in that a stronger dollar goes hand in
  hand with larger deviations from covered interest parity (CIP) and contractions of cross-border
  bank lending in dollars. We argue that underpinning the triangle is the role of the dollar as a
  key barometer of risk-taking capacity in global capital markets" — and the cross-border dollar
  lending channel that the BIS banking statistics (S-054) measure.
- **Caveats:** the paper's state is the *deviation*, not a published series; it is descriptive of a
  triangle rather than a trading rule.
- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched directly by the lead session, including the deposited
  abstract quoted above, the four authors and their affiliations, volume 1, issue 2, pages 193–208,
  publication date 1 September 2019, and the DOI.

### S-060 — Avdjiev, S., Eren, G. and McGuire, P. (2020)
"Dollar funding costs during the Covid-19 crisis through the lens of the FX swap market." *BIS
Bulletin* No 1 (1 April 2020).
URL: https://www.bis.org/publications/bulletin-1-dollar-funding-costs-during-covid-19-crisis-through-lens-fx-swap-market
- **Type:** Venue documentation (central-bank bulletin).
- **Supports:** the operational dashboard framing — indicators of dollar funding costs in FX
  markets rose sharply during the pandemic, reflecting both demand and supply factors; dollar
  funding demand had grown in preceding years with corporate and portfolio-investor hedging needs
  outside the US; March 2020 crimped intermediary supply of dollar funding; the indicators narrowed
  after central banks deployed dollar swap lines.
- **Caveats:** descriptive bulletin; the authors' views; the "indicators" are the FX-swap/basis
  measures, which are market-quote constructs and not a published statistical series.
- **Verification:** landing page and PDF fetched and read by the verification agent.

### S-061 — Nissinen, J. (2022)
"Risk, Return and the Funding Currency." SSRN working paper.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.4168957
- **Type:** Primary theory (author-reported; preprint).
- **Supports:** an explicit *definition* of the funding currency — low interest rates, low currency
  funding frictions, ample central bank liquidity and favourable net foreign portfolio positions —
  and the result that the risk–return relation in currency markets is currency-dependent, with the
  security-market-line slope steepest and the fit tightest in the funding currency, rationalised by
  a stylised model in which the pricing kernel is anchored in the currency with the most
  favourable funding conditions; when funding is freely available across currencies, as during
  Covid-19, currency-specific pricing lines converge.
- **Caveats:** an SSRN preprint (not peer reviewed); the model is a stylised two-asset adaptation
  of Gabaix and Maggiori (2015).
- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched and read by the verification agent (title, author,
  posted-content type, 2022 date, DOI).

### S-062 — Dao, M. C., Gourinchas, P.-O. and Itskhoki, O. (2025)
"Breaking Parity: Equilibrium Exchange Rates and Currency Premia." NBER Working Paper 34443
(November 2025); also issued as IMF Working Paper 2025/153.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.3386/w34443 · https://doi.org/10.5089/9798229015615.001
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the joint modelling of covered and uncovered premia under intermediary VaR
  balance-sheet constraints, with hedged and unhedged currency supplied by banks — in the time
  series, covered premia change infrequently and in concert across currencies, driven by aggregate
  financial-market conditions, while uncovered premia move frequently with currency-specific demand
  shocks proxied by dealer banks' net currency futures positions; dealer net futures positions
  account for most spot-exchange-rate variation for every currency.
- **Caveats:** working-paper series; the dealer-position proxy is a data-construction question the
  candidate would inherit if it adopted this state.
- **Verification:** NBER landing page and Crossref records fetched and read by the verification
  agent.

### S-063 — Liao, G. Y. and Zhang, T. (2025)
"The Hedging Channel of Exchange Rate Determination." *The Review of Financial Studies* 38(1): 1–38
(published 23 October 2024; issue 2025). Earlier: Fed IFDP 1283 (2020), DOI 10.17016/ifdp.2020.1283.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rfs/hhae072
- **Type:** Primary theory (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the hedged-demand channel of exchange-rate determination — the demand to hedge
  currency exposure, rather than interest-rate parity arbitrage alone, drives exchange-rate
  adjustment — which is the channel that S-056, S-057 and S-064 all build on.
- **Caveats:** the paper's own abstract was **not** read in this cycle (publisher landing page not
  fetched); the characterisation above is taken from the deposited abstracts of the citing works
  (S-064, S-073's reference list) and is therefore second-hand here.
- **Verification:** Crossref bibliographic record fetched and read by the verification agent (title,
  authors, container, volume, issue, pages, publication date, DOI); the abstract is not deposited.

### S-064 — Wang, Y. (2026)
"Deviations from Covered Interest Parity, Dollar Funding Pressure, and Currency Risk Premia."
SSRN working paper 6152329 (posted 2 February 2026; paper dated 30 January 2025; 78 pages;
author: Yirong Wang, University of Bristol). Earlier versions: AEA Annual Meeting 2026 programme
paper; Paris-DEC (December 2025) conference paper.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.6152329
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported); preprint, not peer reviewed.
- **Supports:** **the decisive source for the Cycle-5 closure of G-10.** The Cycle-2 entry recorded
  only the truncated Crossref abstract; the full content, read in Cycle 5, states the candidate's
  residual claim as a published, positive result. Verbatim from the deposited abstract: "This paper
  examines the relationship between dollar hedge funding pressure and the cross-section of currency
  risk premium. Motivated by the currency hedging channel proposed by Liao & Zhang (2025), I use the
  deviations of Covered Interest Rate Parity (CIP) as a measure of imbalances between excessive
  dollar hedging demand and in nominal interest rate (carry trade) and global imbalances in trade
  and capital flows in postcrisis period." The stated hypothesis: "Currencies with higher dollar
  funding pressure (higher unconditional level of basis) earn higher expected excess returns."
  The author's own reported results (AEA 2026 programme version, Research Highlights): "A global
  high-minus-low basis factor (HMLx) earns 4–4.5% risk premium per annum"; "HMLx explains a large
  share of the cross-sectional variation in currency excess returns"; "Basis-sorted portfolios
  subsume carry, external imbalance factors, business gap, value, and momentum"; "Relative to the
  carry, HMLx improves cross-sectional R² by at least 10%". Data: G10 currencies, January 1999 to
  January 2025, split into precrisis (Jan 1999 – Aug 2008) and postcrisis (Aug 2009 – Jan 2025)
  sub-samples; one-month IBOR rates as risk-free rates; the log-form dollar-denominated basis of
  Du, Tepper and Verdelhan (2018) (S-069).
- **Caveats:** a **preprint / job-market paper, not peer reviewed**; the results are the author's
  own and are not reproduced here. The state it conditions on is the *market-quote* cross-currency
  basis, which the register established is **not** published anywhere (G-10 finding (a)); a validator
  would have to construct it from paid vendor forwards. The factor is therefore a *published
  baseline with a positive result on the cross-section*, and it subsumes carry rather than adding
  to it.
- **Verification:** Crossref record for DOI 10.2139/ssrn.6152329 fetched and read (title, author,
  posting date, truncated deposited abstract); the SSRN landing page was read through the search
  index (title, author, "78 Pages Posted: 2 Feb 2026", suggested citation with the DOI); the
  **full** abstract and results were read from the AEA Annual Meeting 2026 programme page
  (aeaweb.org/conference/2026/program/paper/AzRFfdDA) and from the Paris-DEC December 2025
  conference PDF, which reproduce the abstract and the research highlights verbatim. The SSRN page
  itself returned HTTP 403 to a direct fetch; that is recorded here rather than glossed.

### S-065 — Menkhoff, L., Sarno, L., Schmeling, M. and Schrimpf, A. (2012)
"Carry Trades and Global Foreign Exchange Volatility." *The Journal of Finance* 67(2): 681–718.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1540-6261.2012.01728.x
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the *volatility-state* baseline for carry — high-interest-rate currencies are
  negatively related to innovations in global FX volatility, so they deliver low returns when
  volatility unexpectedly spikes while low-rate currencies hedge by yielding positive returns;
  volatility risk dominates liquidity risk in this setting.
- **Caveats:** the conditioning state here is *volatility*, not funding — this is a rival state
  variable for the same outcome, and any funding-state candidate must show it adds to it.
- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched and read by the verification agent (container, volume,
  issue, pages, DOI, deposited abstract); the bibliographic details are corroborated by the
  reference list of the S-055 Crossref record fetched by the lead session.

### S-066 — Miranda-Agrippino, S. and Rey, H. (2020)
"U.S. Monetary Policy and the Global Financial Cycle." *Review of Economic Studies* 87(6):
2754–2776 (2020). NBER Working Paper 21722 (November 2015).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/restud/rdaa019 · https://doi.org/10.3386/w21722
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the global-financial-cycle channel — US monetary policy shocks induce comovements in
  international financial variables, one global factor explains a large share of the variation in
  risky asset prices worldwide and declines significantly after a US monetary contraction, and
  tightening leads to deleveraging of global intermediaries and retrenchments of international
  credit flows.
- **Caveats:** the US-policy driver is a *different* state variable from the dollar funding state;
  this is a rival explanation for any dollar-appreciation result.
- **Verification:** Crossref and NBER records fetched and read by the verification agent. Note: this
  paper is frequently mis-attributed to Gourinchas and Rey; the authorship here is
  Miranda-Agrippino and Rey.

### S-067 — Gourinchas, P.-O. and Rey, H. (2013, published 2014)
"External Adjustment, Global Imbalances and Valuation Effects." NBER Working Paper 19240 (July
2013); published as Chapter 10 of *Handbook of International Economics*, vol. 4 (2014), pp. 585–645.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.3386/w19240
- **Type:** Survey of prior results (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the "global imbalances" framing that the register's baseline row refers to — the
  stylised facts of country external balance sheets and the determinants of long-term capital flows.
- **Caveats:** a survey chapter, so its claims are second-hand; it is the framing reference rather
  than a test.
- **Verification:** NBER landing page fetched and read by the verification agent, including the
  published-version citation.

### S-068 — Bruno, V. and Shin, H. S. (2013; published 2015)
"Capital Flows, Cross-Border Banking and Global Liquidity." NBER Working Paper 19038 (May 2013);
published as "Capital flows and the risk-taking channel of monetary policy", *Journal of Monetary
Economics* 71: 119–132 (2015), DOI https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jmoneco.2014.11.011; and "Cross-border
banking and global liquidity", *Review of Economic Studies* 82(2): 535–564 (2015), DOI
https://doi.org/10.1093/restud/rdu042.
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the cross-border banking channel that the published state (S-054) measures — a
  model of gross capital flows through the international banking system in which the leverage cycle
  of global banks determines the transmission of financial conditions across borders, with global
  factors dominating local factors in a 46-country panel.
- **Caveats:** the authors' views; note that the working-paper title and the two published titles
  differ, and that **no paper by these authors titled "Global liquidity and the FX market" in the
  Journal of Financial Economics could be verified** — see the G-10 verdict.
- **Verification:** NBER landing page and Crossref records fetched and read by the verification
  agent; the *REStud* bibliographic details are corroborated by the reference lists of the two
  Crossref records fetched by the lead session (S-055 and S-059).

### S-069 — Du, W., Tepper, A. and Verdelhan, A. (2017; published 2018)
"Deviations from Covered Interest Rate Parity." NBER Working Paper 23170 (February 2017); published
as "Deviations from covered interest rate parity", *The Journal of Finance* 73(2): 915–940 (2018),
DOI https://doi.org/10.1111/jofi.12620.
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the canonical empirical treatment of CIP deviations as a systematic, persistent
  post-crisis phenomenon, and the reference point for treating the level of CIP deviation as a
  measure of the funding constraint faced by the marginal currency arbitrageur.
- **Caveats:** the *Journal of Finance* page was not fetched (the DOI is corroborated by the
  reference lists of the two Crossref records the lead session fetched directly, S-055 and S-059);
  the working-paper record is verified via Crossref.
- **Verification:** Crossref records fetched and read by the verification agent; bibliographic
  corroboration by the lead session's reference lists as above.

### S-070 — Committee on the Global Financial System (2020)
"US dollar funding: an international perspective." *CGFS Papers* No 65 (18 June 2020).
URL: https://www.bis.org/publications/cgfs-paper-65-us-dollar-funding-international-perspective
- **Type:** Venue documentation (official committee report).
- **Supports:** the institutional baseline for treating dollar funding conditions as a systemic
  state variable — the dollar "dominates international finance as a funding and investment
  currency. Although the United States accounts for one quarter of global economic activity, around
  half of all cross-border bank loans and international debt securities are denominated in US
  dollars" — together with a review of how funding demand and supply evolved and how stress
  episodes transmitted.
- **Caveats:** a committee synthesis rather than an estimation exercise; no DOI is deposited.
- **Verification:** landing page fetched and read by the verification agent (series, number, date,
  quoted framing text).

### S-071 — Ranaldo, A. and de Magistris, P. S. (2022)
"Liquidity in the global currency market." *Journal of Financial Economics* 146(3): 859–883
(December 2022).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jfineco.2022.09.004
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** a *settlement-based* liquidity state for the global currency market, built from
  CLS-based FX settlement metrics alongside market-microstructure measures, linked to currency risk
  premia and volatility — i.e. the register's own settlement-infrastructure theme already has a
  published state variable.
- **Caveats:** the publisher landing page returned HTTP 403 during verification, so the abstract
  was not read; the characterisation above is drawn from the verified title and the paper's verified

- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched and read by the verification agent (title, authors,

### S-072 — Financial Stability Board (2020)
"Holistic Review of the March Market Turmoil." Report to the G20 (17 November 2020).
URL: https://www.fsb.org/2020/11/holistic-review-of-the-march-market-turmoil/
- **Type:** Venue documentation (regulator report).
- **Supports:** the official account of the March 2020 liquidity stress as a *state* — large and
  persistent imbalances in the demand for, and supply of, liquidity; some open-ended investment
  funds facing incentives to redeem ahead of others; dealers struggling to absorb large asset sales,
  amplifying turmoil in short-term funding markets; and dysfunction exacerbated by substantial sales
  of US Treasuries by leveraged non-bank investors and foreign holders; absent central bank
  intervention the stress would have worsened significantly.
- **Caveats:** a policy review attributing dysfunction to interacting non-bank intermediation
  mechanisms rather than to a single channel.
- **Verification:** landing page and full-text PDF fetched and read by the verification agent.

### S-073 — Lim, J. J. and Long, X. (2026)
"The dollar squeeze and economic growth." *Journal of Macroeconomics* 87: 103740 (March 2026).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jmacro.2026.103740
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** an application in which the cross-currency basis is used directly as a shock
  variable in a panel VAR / IV-local-projection model, with the reported result that positive basis
  shocks reduce output and that easier dollar funding conditions can be contractionary outside

- **Caveats:** the article landing page was **not** fetched (only the Crossref record), so the
  abstract text is as rendered in the publisher listing; the macroeconomic outcome is far from the
  register's trading horizon and is cited here as an example of the basis being used as a state
  variable, not as support for a trading rule.
- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched and read by the verification agent (title, authors,
  container, volume, article number, print date, DOI).

## Cycle 2 additions, part 4 (S-074 … S-081) — the intraday FX fixing literature (G-09 closure)

Added in the Cycle-2 G-09 closure pass. **Every item below was re-verified directly against the
Crossref API, the arXiv record, the NBER record or the publisher listing by the session that
wrote this section** — none of it is taken on trust from a search summary, and three of the DOIs
that a read-only verification agent proposed for these papers were wrong (they are recorded in
the G-09 gap entry below).

### S-074 — Michelberger, P. S. and Witte, J. H. (2016)
"Foreign exchange market microstructure and the WM/Reuters 4 pm fix." *The Journal of Finance and
Data Science* 2(1): 26–41.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jfds.2016.01.002 · preprint: arXiv:1501.07778
(https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1501.07778)
- **Type:** Primary empirical result (author-reported), peer-reviewed, open access (CC BY-NC-ND).
- **Supports:** the fix-window microstructure — the paper "study[ies] the market microstructure
  around 4pm" and shows that "market dynamics can be distinguished from other times during the
  day through increased volatility and size of movements", and concludes that "our findings question
  the aggregate benefit to the client base of using the 4pm fix in its current form". This is the
  paper that S-047 cited but that the register had not read; it is the peer-reviewed statement that
  the fix window has a *measurable, distinct* price signature.
- **Caveats:** the full text was **not** read in this pass (abstract, metadata and reference list
  only); the paper is open access, so a future agent can read it in full. The result is
  author-reported and not reproduced here. It speaks to the *size and volatility* of moves around
  the fix, not to the sign of a tradeable concession.
- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched and read (title, authors, container, volume, issue,
  pages, print date, DOI, licence); arXiv record 1501.07778 fetched and read (v1 30 January 2015,
  v2 29 February 2016; title, authors, abstract, "20 Pages, 6 Figures, 3 Tables"); the abstract is
  quoted as printed on both.

### S-075 — Melvin, M. and Prins, J. (2015)
"Equity hedging and exchange rates at the London 4 p.m. fix." *Journal of Financial Markets* 22:
50–72.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.finmar.2014.11.001 · working-paper version: "Exchange Rates,
Equity Hedging, and the London 4pm Fix", SSRN 2019274 (2012),
https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.2019274
- **Type:** Primary empirical result (author-reported), peer-reviewed (working-paper version read).
- **Supports:** **the published result that closes IDEA-006's aggregate claim.** From the abstract
  of the working-paper version: "A key institutional feature of the foreign exchange market is the
  London 4pm 'fix.' … Passive hedging strategies for global equity portfolios tend to be traded at
  the London 4pm fix. … **Trading intensity and volatility are highest at the fix. In addition,
  because of the large number of passive hedging trades executed at the fix, hedging flows predict
  the direction of temporary exchange rate movements at this time of day.** We focus on end of month
  'fixing' prices where hedging and rebalancing activity concentrates and find that **hedging
  demand has a strong, but temporary, impact on exchange rates.**" That is, in the register's terms,
  a *price-insensitive, deadline-driven flow whose price impact is temporary* — exactly the
  mechanism and the pattern IDEA-006 claimed to be measuring for the first time.
- **Caveats:** the quoted wording is the **2012 working-paper** abstract; the 2015 published
  article's own abstract and results were not re-read in this pass, so pagination and DOI come
  from the Crossref record while the quotation comes from the SSRN record. The end-of-month
  concentration is a specific regime, and the impact documented is that of *hedging* demand; the
  generalisation to all deadline-constrained flow is the candidate's own claim.
- **Verification:** Crossref records for both the published version and the SSRN version fetched
  and read (titles, authors, container, volume, pages, dates, DOI; the SSRN record carries the full
  deposited abstract).

### S-076 — Evans, M. D. D. (2018)
"Forex trading and the WMR Fix." *Journal of Banking & Finance* 87: 233–247.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jbankfin.2017.09.017 · working-paper version: "Forex Trading and
the WMR Fix", SSRN 2487991 (2014).
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported), peer-reviewed.
- **Supports:** the microstructure model of the fix and the **negative serial correlation** result:
  the publisher's article text states "My empirical findings concerning the presence of negative
  serial correlation in price changes around the WRM Fix have been confirmed by Ito and Yamada
  (2015)"; the paper "extends the Portfolio Shifts (PS) model developed in Lyons (1997), Evans and
  Lyons (2002) … to include a round of trading where the Fix benchmark price is determined", and
  links the fix order to the optimal-execution literature ("a dealer with a large Fix order faces
  a similar optimal execution problem"). It also states the "Melvin and Prins (2015)" result as
  "intraday returns are positively related to their estimated flows before the Fix and negative
  related after the Fix".
- **Caveats:** the quotation is from the publisher's article-preview text and the SSRN listing
  excerpt; the full text was not read in this pass. The working-paper abstract states the paper
  "undertakes a detailed empirical analysis of the how forex rates behave around the Fix drawing on
  a decade of tick-by-tick data for 21 currency pairs" — i.e. the underlying data are proprietary.
  The result is author-reported and not reproduced here.
- **Verification:** Crossref record for the published version fetched and read (title, author,
  container, volume, pages, print date, DOI); the publisher article-preview text and the SSRN
  2487991 listing were read through the search-result excerpts; the SSRN 2487991 DOI was returned
  by the same Crossref query.

### S-077 — Ito, T. and Yamada, M. (2015)
"Was the Forex Fixing Fixed?" NBER Working Paper 21518 (September 2015).
URL: https://www.nber.org/papers/w21518 · PDF:
https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w21518/w21518.pdf
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported), working paper.
- **Supports:** the pre/post-fixing reversal and the institutional description of the clock. The
  working paper states that "The paper emphasized the negative autocorrelation of the Forex rate
  between the pre- and post-fixing periods, particularly at the end-of-month trading day", and
  describes the institutional facts: "'Fixing' of the exchange rate (price) is a rule among the
  Forex market participating institutions to set a reference/settlement price for the day. Major
  fixings occur at 9:55 am Tokyo time for transactions between Japanese banks and their customers,
  and at 4:00 pm London time for transactions between European and US banks and their customers."
  Its stated purpose is to "econometrically analyze price and volume behaviors at around fixing
  times to see whether there is suggestive evidence of price manipulation" — and it also documents
  the pre-existing discussion of the dealer-information problem (Ito, Lyons and Melvin 2002; Evans
  and Lyons 1999).
- **Caveats:** a working paper; **no peer-reviewed version was located in this pass**, so the
  working paper is the cited version. Its primary question is manipulation, not a price concession,
  but its reported negative autocorrelation is the same reversal pattern the register's candidate
  would have to claim. The result is author-reported and not reproduced here.
- **Verification:** the NBER working-paper PDF and the two revision PDFs were retrieved and the
  quoted lines read as printed; the NBER landing page for WP 21518 was also retrieved.

### S-078 — Evans, M. D. D. and Lyons, R. K. (2002)
"Order Flow and Exchange Rate Dynamics." *Journal of Political Economy* 110(1): 170–180.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1086/324391 · earlier version: NBER Working Paper 7317 (1999).
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** the general order-flow/price-impact result that any flow-driven intraday claim
  must be positioned against, and the model that S-076 extends to the fix. It is the register's
  "the price impact of a flow is a published law of FX microstructure" anchor.
- **Caveats:** the paper is **not** about fixings or about time-of-day patterns; it is cited here
  as the general mechanism and the rival explanation (a flow-driven price move is the expected
  result of order flow, not evidence of a special deadline), not as evidence about the clock.
- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched and read (title, authors, container, volume, issue,
  pages, print date, DOI); the NBER working-paper record 10.3386/w7317 was returned by the same
  query.

### S-079 — Osler, C. L. (2003)
"Currency Orders and Exchange Rate Dynamics: An Explanation for the Predictive Success of
Technical Analysis." *The Journal of Finance* 58(5): 1791–1819.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/1540-6261.00588 · working-paper version: "Currency Orders and
Exchange-Rate Dynamics: Explaining the Success of Technical Analysis", SSRN 923370.
- **Type:** Primary theory (author-reported model and evidence).
- **Supports:** the **rival mechanism** for any intraday FX pattern: clustered stop-loss and
  take-profit orders at technical levels generate predictable price dynamics without any
  deadline-driven flow. It is the control IDEA-006 must beat — an intraday pattern around the fix
  may be order clustering rather than a deadline concession, and a candidate that cannot separate
  the two is not a discovery.
- **Caveats:** the publisher elided the abstract in the record used for verification, so the
  claim above rests on the title and on the paper's standard treatment of order clustering, **not**
  on a verified abstract quotation; the full text was not read in this pass. Cite the mechanism,
  not a number.
- **Verification:** Crossref records for the journal version and the SSRN version fetched and read
  (titles, author, container, volume, issue, pages, dates, DOIs); the Semantic Scholar record was
  fetched for the citation count and an open-access pointer.

### S-080 — Benenchia, M., Galati, L. and Lepone, A. (2025)
"To fix or not to fix, the Fix: Reassessing the effectiveness of the 4 pm Fix. A pre-registered
study." *Pacific-Basin Finance Journal* 93: 102652 (October 2025).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.pacfin.2024.102652 · working-paper versions: SSRN 4958561 (2024, this
paper) and SSRN 4718425 (2024, the 2024 PBFJ paper registered as S-047).
- **Type:** Primary empirical result (author-reported), pre-registered, peer-reviewed.
- **Supports:** the **post-reform** reassessment of the fix — the publisher's description states the
  study "critically re-evaluates the effectiveness of the WM/Refinitiv (WMR) methodology for
  Foreign Exchange (FX) benchmark rates following its revision in 2015 … the representativeness,
  attainability, and robustness of the WMR 4 pm fix", using proprietary high-frequency data
  provided by Refinitiv. It also **completes the author list of S-047**, whose Cycle-2 entry
  recorded that "the full author list beyond the first author was not verified in this cycle" —
  the SSRN records show the same three authors (Benenchia, Galati, Lepone) for both papers.
- **Caveats:** like S-047, the data are proprietary and administrator-provided, so the result is
  not reproducible from public data; the study is about the *benchmark's* representativeness, not
  about a tradeable price effect. The article landing page was not fetched, so the description is
  as rendered in the publisher listing.
- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched and read (title, all three authors, container, volume,
  article number, print date, DOI); the SSRN 4958561 and SSRN 4718425 records were returned by the
  same query, which is how S-047's author list was completed.

### S-081 — He, K. D. (2026)
"Currency Returns and Price Discovery around Global FX Fixings." SSRN working paper 7388124
(2026). DOI: https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.7388124
- **Type:** Working paper (author-reported, not peer reviewed).
- **Supports:** the most explicit statement located of the pre-fix run-up / post-fix reversal in
  the dollar: the abstract "looks at the possibility of a W-shaped return pattern, in which the
  dollar climbs before FX fixes and falls after them, in an effort to shed light on the daily
  movements of the U.S. dollar during FX fixing periods", using "the hourly returns of the nine
  most traded currencies over a span of 21 years", and reports that "return reversals occur often
  and statistically significantly for all major currencies" and that "the availability of published
  reference rates largely dictates the timing of these changes".
- **Integrity caveat (important, and the reason this entry is narrow):** the record's **metadata
  and abstract are verified, but its 39-item reference list is not trustworthy.** Two
  spot-checked references — "Ben-David, I. (2025), 'Exchange-traded funds, institutional cash
  flows, and intraday FX price pressure', *Review of Financial Studies* 38(1): 188" and "Ahmad, W.
  (2024), 'Intraday order flow, dealer inventory management, and price discovery in global
  currency markets', *International Review of Economics & Finance* 89: 412" — **could not be found
  in Crossref** by title, author and journal, and no plausible record was returned. **No reference
  from this paper's bibliography may be cited in this register, and the paper must not be used as a
  map of the fixing literature.** Its own claim is treated as an author-reported result that
  *duplicates*, rather than extends, S-075, S-076 and S-077.
- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched and read (title, author, deposited abstract, the full
  39-item reference list, posting date, DOI); the two reference spot-checks were run against the
  Crossref bibliographic query and returned no matching records.

## Cycle 3 additions (S-082)

Added 2026-10-19 in the Cycle-3 originality-gap closure pass. Verification standard as prior
cycles: each entry located and metadata-checked against Crossref / the publisher's own record
before use; "Verification" records how the entry was confirmed, not whether the cited paper's
results are correct.

### S-082 — Lee, S., Ranaldo, A. and Tsuruga, T. (2026)
"Settlement Risk and Currency Markets." *IMF Working Papers* WP/26/156 (July 2026).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.5089/9798229054355.001
SSRN working-paper version: https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.7201787
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported), IMF working paper; SSRN preprint.
- **Supports:** settlement-side mechanics as a *priced* determinant of currency prices — the
  closest published work to IDEA-006's settlement-side residual. Exploits Hungary's November 2015
  adoption of CLS payment-versus-payment (PvP) settlement as a quasi-natural experiment that
  sharply reduced settlement risk while preserving settlement cycles and multilateral netting.
  Difference-in-differences finding: currency excess returns decline by about ten basis points
  after CLS adoption, and exchange rate volatility falls; deviations from triangular arbitrage
  narrow. Conclusion: "settlement risk is a priced friction and a source of limits to arbitrage
  in currency markets." Cites Ranaldo and Santucci de Magistris (2022) (S-071) and related
  CLS-based microstructure work.
- **Caveats:** the paper tests settlement *risk* (PvP adoption), not the specific settlement-side
  *deadline cluster* (CLS currency-close / pay-in cut-offs and the post-US-close T+1 window,
  S-048, S-049), so it does not close IDEA-006's deadline-cluster claim; it supports the
  direction that settlement-side mechanics are priced. Sample is Hungary vs. regional controls
  (HRK, CZK, PLN, RON, RSD); result is author-reported and not reproduced here.
- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched directly (title, three authors, container "IMF
  Working Papers", volume 2026, issue 156, page 1, publication date July 2026, DOI, ISSN
  1018-5941); SSRN record 10.2139/ssrn.7201787 fetched and read (posted 2026-09-24, abstract,
  author list); IMF eLibrary landing page identified as the publisher source.

## Cycle 3 additions (S-083 … S-089)

Added 2026-10-19 in Cycle 3 for the two new dossiers (IDEA-008, IDEA-009). Verification standard:
each entry located and metadata-checked against Crossref / arXiv / the publisher's own record
before use; "Verification" records how the entry was confirmed, not whether the cited paper's
results are correct.

### S-083 — Jones, C. M. and Lamont, O. A. (2002)
"Short-sale constraints and stock returns." *Journal of Financial Economics* 66(2-3):207–239
(November 2002).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/s0304-405x(02)00224-6
NBER Working Paper 8494 (October 2001): https://doi.org/10.3386/w8494
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported), peer-reviewed journal article; NBER working
  paper precursor.
- **Supports:** short-sale constraints as a limit to arbitrage; stocks expensive to short or newly
  entering the borrowing market have high valuations and low subsequent returns; recall risk
  (stock loans are not term loans, lender can recall at any time); short interest as a measure of
  shorting demand. Cited in IDEA-008 for the participant, the constraint and the recall risk.
- **Caveats:** sample is 1926–1933 (a distressed period); results are author-reported and not
  reproduced here; the paper documents the *overpricing* side (expensive-to-short → low returns),
  which is the opposite direction from IDEA-008's forced-covering spike claim.
- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched directly (title, two authors, container "Journal of
  Financial Economics", volume 66, issue 2-3, pages 207–239, publication date November 2002,
  DOI, ISSN 0304-405X, 704 citation count); NBER record 10.3386/w8494 fetched directly (title,
  two authors, issue date October 2001).

### S-084 — D'Avolio, G. (2002)
"The market for borrowing stock." *Journal of Financial Economics* 66(2-3):271–306
(November 2002).
Referenced within S-083's bibliography (listed as an unpublished Harvard working paper, 2001;
the JFE published version exists in the same issue).
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported), peer-reviewed journal article.
- **Supports:** the securities lending market described empirically — loan supply ("shortability"),
  loan fees ("specialness"), and loan recalls from a large institutional lending intermediary
  (4/2000–9/2001 data); recall is rare on average (~2% of loans per month) but days with
  involuntary covering show volume more than twice the stock's average and elevated intraday
  volatility; the lender's option to recall the loan at any time imposes risk on the short seller.
  Cited in IDEA-008 for recall risk and forced-covering volume/volatility.
- **Caveats:** the DOI could not be re-derived via Crossref (a direct title/author query returned
  an unrelated paper); the entry is registered on the basis of (a) its bibliographic listing inside
  S-083's verified reference list and (b) the ScienceDirect abstract page for
  S0304405X02002064 (the adjacent PII in the same JFE issue). Results are author-reported and not
  reproduced here.
- **Verification:** located as reference BIB6 in S-083's verified Crossref record
  (10.1016/s0304-405x(02)00224-6); ScienceDirect record S0304405X02002064 fetched (title "The
  market for borrowing stock", JFE 66(2-3), November 2002). DOI not independently confirmed —
  treat the identifier as pending re-verification.

### S-086 — He, Z., Kelly, B., Lu, D. and Manela, A. (2022/2024)
"Fundamentals of Perpetual Futures." arXiv:2212.06888 (first posted December 2022; v3,
April 2023; v6, August 2024).
arXiv: https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.06888
- **Type:** Prior empirical and theoretical result (author-reported), arXiv preprint.
- **Supports:** perpetual futures have no expiry so no guaranteed convergence to spot; the funding
  rate (periodic payment between longs and shorts, proportional to the futures-spot gap, typically
  settled every 8 hours) keeps the futures price close to spot; deviations from no-arbitrage
  prices in crypto are larger than in traditional markets, comove across currencies, and diminish
  over time; past returns predict futures-spot deviations with R² above 50% (momentum feedback);
  funding-rate arbitrage (long spot, short expensive perpetual) documented; clamp regions where
  the funding rate equals a minimum (e.g. 0.01%). Cited in IDEA-009 for the funding-rate
  mechanism, the crowded-leverage state, and the momentum confound.
- **Caveats:** results are author-reported and not reproduced here; the crypto venue carries
  venue-specific risks (custody, exchange risk, 24/7 trading); different exchanges implement
  funding rates differently (clamp regions, calculation methods), so results are not transferable
  across venues without restatement.
- **Verification:** arXiv record 2212.06888 fetched directly (title, four authors, abstract,
  version history, submission date December 2022); the arXiv HTML and PDF versions were read from
  arxiv.org.

### S-090 — Bianchi, J., Bigio, S. and Engel, C. (2021, rev. 2023)
"Scrambling for Dollars: International Liquidity, Banks and Exchange Rates." NBER Working Paper
No. 29457 (November 2021; revised February 2023); CEPR Discussion Paper 16712; Revise and
Resubmit, Review of Economic Studies.
URL: https://www.nber.org/papers/w29457 ·
https://cepr.org/publications/dp16712
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported), working paper.
- **Supports:** the aggregate dollar-funding-liquidity channel — "an increase in the dollar funding
  risk leads to a rise in the convenience yield and an appreciation of the dollar"; "we present
  empirical evidence on the relationship between exchange rate fluctuations for the G10 currencies
  and the quantity of dollar liquidity"; the model predicts dollar appreciation when banks
  "scramble for dollars" due to settlement frictions in interbank markets. Relevant to G-10:
  this is the *aggregate/time-series* dollar response, not the *cross-sectional* claim about which
  currencies respond most conditional on the interest differential. The cross-sectional residual
  therefore remains open (G-10).
- **Caveats:** working paper; empirical evidence on G10 currencies and dollar liquidity quantity;
  the cross-sectional structure (heterogeneous currency responses conditional on funding
  dependence) is not tested. The paper is "Revise and Resubmit, Review of Economic Studies" per
  the author's website.
- **Verification:** NBER working-paper page fetched and read (title, authors, report number, date,
  abstract); CEPR discussion paper page fetched and read (title, authors, DP number, date);
  author's personal page (javierbianchi.com) confirms R&R status at Review of Economic Studies.

"Shaping FX // 06: The FX ecosystem // Part 2 — What difference has a day made so far?" (30
October 2024) and "CLS declines to delay FX cut-off as US stock changes loom" (Reuters, 9 April
2024); "The Ripple Effect of US Settlement Cycle T+1" (CLS Group, 6 November 2024).

https://www.cls-group.com/news/the-ripple-effect-of-us-tplus1-settlement/

## Cycle 5 additions (S-095 … S-100)

Added 2026-10-19 in the Cycle-5 originality-gap closure pass. Verification standard as prior
cycles: each entry located and metadata-checked against Crossref / the publisher's own record
before use; "Verification" records how the entry was confirmed, not whether the cited paper's
results are correct. Every identifier below was re-derived in this cycle; the three mis-reported
identifiers caught in the G-09 pass and the phantom citations caught in the Cycle-2 closure passes
are the standing reason this rule is enforced.

### S-095 — Moskowitz, T. J., Ross, C. P., Ross, S. Y. and Vasudevan, K. (2026)
"Risk, Specialization, and Covered-Interest Parity." *The Journal of Finance* (published online
13 October 2026; received 12 July 2024, accepted 17 November 2025). Article number jofi.70095.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/jofi.70095
Earlier versions: NBER Working Paper 32707 (July 2024; revised February 2026, titled "Risk,
Specialization, and Covered-Interest Parity", DOI https://doi.org/10.3386/w32707); Federal
Reserve Board Finance and Economics Discussion Series 2024-061, "Quantities and Covered-Interest
Parity" (11 July 2024), DOI https://doi.org/10.17016/FEDS.2024.061.
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported); peer-reviewed journal article
  (the working-paper and FEDS versions are the same paper).
- **Supports:** the *cross-sectional* explanation of covered-interest-parity bases by intermediary
  frictions, and the pass-through of those frictions to prices. Verbatim from the published
  abstract: "Prevailing theories of financial intermediation assume an integrated financial sector
  with frictionless risk-sharing. However, we identify substantial risk-sharing frictions linked to
  intermediary specialization using currency derivatives markets as a laboratory. Using
  confidential supervisory data covering $25 trillion in daily bank exposures, we document imperfect
  hedging in banks' FX-swap intermediation: banks rarely hedge their synthetic dollar lending with
  maturity-matched foreign safe assets. Intermediaries specialize in markets in which they have
  expertise in managing the associated exposures, giving rise to cross-market variation in
  deviations from covered-interest parity. Our results highlight the importance of intermediary
  specialization and its impact on risk premia." The earlier version's abstract states the three
  forces: "1) foreign safe asset scarcity, 2) market power and segmentation of banks specializing
  in different markets, and 3) concentration of demand", and the paper reports magnitudes of the
  order of "a one-standard-deviation difference in the safe asset ratio corresponds to a further 10
  to 18 bps increase in the basis" and "A one standard deviation increase in demand concentration
  has a 7 bps larger basis".
- **Caveats:** the object is the **basis** and the intermediaries' positions in it; the paper's
  data are **confidential supervisory data** (FR 2052a), so the *state* it identifies is not
  published. "Impact on risk premia" in the published abstract is not specified as a currency
  *return* cross-section; the return-based cross-section is claimed separately by S-064. Results
  are author-reported and not reproduced here.
- **Verification:** Crossref record for DOI 10.1111/jofi.70095 fetched and read directly (title,
  four authors, container "The Journal of Finance", publication date 13 October 2026, article number
  jofi.70095, 46 deposited references, deposited abstract, received/accepted dates); NBER landing
  page for WP 32707 fetched and read (title, authors, DOI, revision date February 2026); Federal
  Reserve FEDS landing page and NBER working-paper PDF text for the July 2024 version fetched and
  read (abstract, data description, results magnitudes). The publisher's full text was read only in
  the abstract and the deposited reference list, not in full.

### S-096 — Cenedese, G., Della Corte, P. and Wang, T. (2021)
"Currency Mispricing and Dealer Balance Sheets." *The Journal of Finance* 76(6): 2763–2803.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/jofi.13079
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported), peer-reviewed.
- **Supports:** a **dealer-level** cross-section of currency funding costs driven by a binding
  regulatory constraint. Verbatim from the deposited abstract: "We find dealer-level evidence that
  recent regulation on the leverage ratio requirement causes deviations from covered interest
  parity. Our analysis uses a unique data set of currency derivatives with disclosed counterparty
  identities together with exogenous variation introduced by the U.K. leverage ratio framework.
  Dealers who are affected by the regulatory shock charge an additional premium of about 20 basis
  points per annum for synthetic dollar funding relative to unaffected dealers. This finding holds
  even after controlling for changes in clients' demand. Also, some clients increase their trading
  activity with unaffected dealers with whom they already had a preexisting relationship."
- **Caveats:** the state is **dealer-level regulatory exposure with disclosed counterparty
  identities** — proprietary, not published; the paper prices the *funding* spread, not currency
  returns. Relevant to the register as the closest published demonstration that a *balance-sheet
  constraint* prices currency funding, and as a baseline that any funding-state candidate must
  cite. Results are author-reported and not reproduced here.
- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched directly through the `filter=doi:` endpoint (title,
  three authors, container "The Journal of Finance", volume 76, issue 6, pages 2763–2803,
  publication date 4 October 2021, DOI, deposited abstract). The Crossref reference list of S-095
  independently lists the same article, volume, pages and year.

### S-097 — Adrian, T., Etula, E. and Muir, T. (2014)
"Financial Intermediaries and the Cross-Section of Asset Returns." *The Journal of Finance* 69(6):
2557–2596. DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/jofi.12189
Earlier: Federal Reserve Bank of New York Staff Report No. 464, "Broker-Dealer Leverage and the
Cross-Section of Stock Returns" (July 2010, revised September 2013).
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported), peer-reviewed.
- **Supports:** the register's canonical **intermediary-state asset-pricing** baseline — the
  marginal value of wealth of financial intermediaries as a stochastic discount factor built from
  **shocks to broker-dealer leverage**. Verbatim from the deposited abstract: "Financial
  intermediaries trade frequently in many markets using sophisticated models. Their marginal value
  of wealth should therefore provide a more informative stochastic discount factor (SDF) than that
  of a representative consumer. Guided by theory, we use shocks to the leverage of securities
  broker-dealers to construct an intermediary SDF. Intuitively, deteriorating funding conditions
  are associated with deleveraging and high marginal value of wealth. Our single-factor model prices
  size, book-to-market, momentum, and bond portfolios with an R² of 77% and an average annual
  pricing error of 1% — performing as well as standard multifactor benchmarks designed to price
  these assets."
- **Caveats:** the state is an **estimated** intermediary-leverage shock, not a published series;
  the pricing evidence is for equity and bond portfolios, not a currency cross-section. It is
  registered as the baseline that any "funding state → cross-section" candidate must be measured
  against, and as the strongest prior art on the general mechanism IDEA-002 and IDEA-007 pursue.
- **Verification:** Wiley landing page fetched and read (title, three authors, volume 69, issue 6,
  pages 2557–2596, DOI, deposited abstract); NY Fed Staff Report 464 landing page fetched and read
  (earlier title, authors, date, abstract, published-version pointer); JSTOR record and RePEc
  (ideas.repec.org) record fetched and read for corroborating bibliographic details; the
  Crossref reference list of S-095 lists the same article, volume, pages and year.

### S-098 — Bosch, D. and Pradkhan, E. (2017)
"Trading Activity and Rate of Convergence in Commodity Futures Markets." *Journal of Futures
Markets* 37(9): 930–938 (published online 14 December 2016; print issue September 2017).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1002/fut.21831
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported), peer-reviewed.
- **Supports:** the closest published work to IDEA-005's differentiator, and a documented
  *partial occupation* of it. Verbatim from the deposited abstract: "We analyze whether and how
  the trading activity of different trader types impacts the rate of convergence between spot and
  futures markets for a broad range of commodity futures markets over the 1999–2014 period. There
  is strong evidence that speculators (commodity index traders) increase (reduce) the rate of
  convergence between spot and futures markets."
- **Caveats:** the object is the **rate of convergence between spot and futures**, and the
  conditioning is by **trader type** (index traders versus speculators), not by the joint state
  IDEA-005 claims (passive share of open interest × contract depth) and not by the *roll-period
  spread concession*. It therefore does not test IDEA-005's differentiator, but it does establish
  that "the index complex changes commodity-futures convergence" is a published result. Results are
  author-reported and not reproduced here.
- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched directly (title, two authors with affiliation, container
  "Journal of Futures Markets", volume 37, issue 9, pages 930–938, online publication date
  14 December 2016, print issue September 2017, DOI, deposited abstract, 15 deposited references
  including the Kang–Rouwenhorst–Tang working paper).

### S-099 — Maréchal, L. (2023)
"A tale of two premiums revisited." *Journal of Futures Markets* 43(5): 580–614 (published online
26 January 2023).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1002/fut.22396
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported), peer-reviewed.
- **Supports:** the **closest published test of IDEA-005's state variable**, and a documented
  *contradicting* result that must be recorded. Verbatim from the deposited abstract: "This paper
  investigates the effect of the 'financialization' of commodity markets in terms of pricing. I
  explore whether the emergence of commodity index traders (CITs) affects weekly returns and
  turnover during the roll periods. I split the sample (1994–2017) into prefinancialization
  (1994–2003) and postfinancialization (2004–2017). I directly test whether the CIT market share
  (CIT/open interest) contributes to commodity returns and whether risk adjustments (based on
  momentum, basis, basis-momentum, open interest, crowding, and average factors) alter liquidity
  and insurance premiums documented in Kang, Rouwenhorst, and Tang."
- **Caveats:** the state is the **CIT market share (CIT ÷ open interest)** — i.e. the CFTC
  "Index Traders" share, the same public state variable IDEA-005 uses — but the outcome is
  *commodity returns and turnover during roll periods*, not the nearby-to-deferred spread
  concession, and the *joint* state with contract depth is not tested. The author's own reported
  finding (from the abstract's framing and the located summary) is that the CIT-share effect on
  returns is **not statistically significant**, which is recorded as contradicting evidence for
  IDEA-005. Results are author-reported and not reproduced here.
- **Verification:** Crossref record fetched directly through the `filter=doi:` endpoint (title,
  author and affiliation, container "Journal of Futures Markets", volume 43, issue 5, pages
  580–614, online publication date 26 January 2023, DOI, deposited abstract).

### S-100 — New York Stock Exchange (venue documentation) and Options Clearing Corporation (venue
documentation) — the two public state sources that close the bounded tasks T5 and T7
(a) NYSE, "Trading Data — Auctions" (https://www.nyse.com/trading-data) — publishes aggregate
closing-auction statistics, including the statement that "The New York Stock Exchange closing
auction is the single largest liquidity event of the day – trading $18.9 billion per day, on
average", that "on average, comparing the closing price to the Volume Weighted Average Price (VWAP)
of the last two minutes, S&P 500 stocks have a price change of less than 0.053% with an average
auction size of $37 million per security", and that "At the NYSE, the community of floor brokers
and Designated Market Makers (DMMs) contribute over 35% of close liquidity, on average".
(b) Cboe Global Markets, "U.S. Equities Market Volume Summary"
(https://www.cboe.com/us/equities/market_share/) — a daily, **date-addressable** market-share file
with an explicit "Exclude Auctions" filter, a "Download this day as CSV" control and a
"Download Last 30 Days" control; the page instructs users to "cite Cboe Global Markets as the
source".
(c) Options Clearing Corporation, "Volume and Open Interest — Daily Volume" and "Volume Query"
(https://www.theocc.com/market-data/market-data-reports/volume-and-open-interest/daily-volume) —
daily contract volume by clearing class and a **volume query by symbol**, with the site stating
"Data available for the past 24 months", plus "Historical Volume Statistics" (daily statistics
listed by month) and a public "Series Search / Series Updates" register of listed series and their
expiration dates.
- **Type:** Venue documentation (exchange and clearing-house data publications).
- **Supports:** **T5 (G-03/G-08) and T7 (G-05).** (i) Closing-auction volume statistics *are*
  published by venues — aggregate per-day and per-market; what is *not* located is a free,
  long-history, **per-security** auction-volume series, which remains a commercial product. This
  is what makes G-08 answerable as "aggregate participation is public, per-security history is
  not". (ii) The **single-name** 0DTE volume series that G-05 has been missing is *constructible*
  from public OCC data — daily volume by symbol plus public series/expiration registers — so G-05
  is now a **data** question that is closed and a **literature** question that remains open.
- **Caveats:** venue-published aggregates; the NYSE figures quoted above are current-period
  marketing statistics with no stated sample, so they are registered as *availability* evidence
  only, never as measurements. No price, volume or performance statistic from these pages is used
  as evidence for any candidate.
- **Verification:** the three pages were fetched and read in this cycle (the NYSE auctions page and
  its closing-auction section; the Cboe market-share page including its CSV/30-day controls and
  the per-date form of the URL; the OCC market-data pages and their "Data available for the past
  24 months" and "Volume Query" entries).
