## Cycle-6 verdicts — the G-13 / G-12 screen applied, and the five frontier families

These are the **current-state** verdicts, in the same style and with the same standing as the
Cycle-5 verdicts block: they supersede any earlier text on the same questions for current-state
purposes and leave the cycle-by-cycle gap text in place at the end of this file.

### Cycle-6 verdict on G-13 (publication timing) and G-12 (sign or existence) — applied to every active candidate

The Cycle-5 closure left two general questions standing (G-13: is the state published **before** the
decision? G-12: can a published state change the **sign** or **existence** of an effect, or only its
measurement quality?). Cycle 6 applied both as **screening conditions to every active candidate**,
and the results are recorded candidate-by-candidate in `idea_register.md`. The ledger's own entries
supply the institutional facts the screen turned on. The summary:

| Candidate | G-13 (state published before the decision) | G-12 (conditioning changes sign or existence) | Cycle-6 outcome |
|---|---|---|---|
| IDEA-001 RECON-FLOW | **Pass** — the announcement is published before the effective close | **Pass on the existence limb only** — the side asymmetry (additions yield a harvestable spread, deletions do not); the flow-scaling limb conditions *size* only | Retained, re-scored, novelty stays 2/5 |
| IDEA-003 VRP-STATE | **Pass** — the gate is computed from live exchange-quoted option surfaces | **Pass** — the gate flips the *sign* of the premium's harvestability | Retained, unchanged |
| IDEA-004 SLOWCAP-COND | **Pass, conditionally** — the 13F-based crowd state is published on EDGAR within 45 days of each quarter end, so a quarterly-anchored rule is decision-time feasible; the binding constraint is proxy *validity* (G-04), not timing | **Pass** — a non-monotone conditioning implies a sign flip | Retained, re-scored |
| IDEA-005 ROLLCONCESS | **Pass** — COT is published before the roll window | **Fail** — the closest published test of its own state variable is null (S-099) and the family's post-2012 aggregate result is null (S-033); no located work supports the joint state | **Parked (P-009)** |
| IDEA-008 SECURITY-LEND | **Fail** — the named state (lending fee / borrow availability) is not publicly disseminated until **29 March 2029** at the earliest (S-126); the short-interest fallback is twice-monthly with a ~7–8 business-day publication lag (S-127) | **Pass in principle** (the event exists only when the constraint binds) — moot once G-13 fails | **Parked (P-010)** |
| IDEA-009 PERP-FUND | **Pass** — the venue publishes the funding rate for each period with zero lag | **Pass** — the level of the funding rate determines the *sign* of the position | Retained, unchanged |
| IDEA-010 CREDIT-ETF | **Partial** — the flow leg (shares outstanding) is published daily; the differentiating state (AP balance-sheet stress) is commercial | **Pass on the existence limb**, but the joint state is unpublished | Retained, re-scored down to 65.0 |
| IDEA-011 ACCTCLOCK | **Pass** — adoption calendars are regulator-published years ahead | **Pass** — the drift's existence depends on the information-quality state | Retained, re-scored |
| IDEA-014 RIN-DEADLINE (new) | **Pass** — the obligation's size is codified in 40 CFR 80.1405 (S-128) and set by rule months in advance (S-130); the deadline itself is "published on EPA's website" by regulation (S-129); the deadline moves only by published rule (S-131) | **Pass** — the *sign* of the deadline effect is determined by the published net obligated position, and the effect exists only when that constraint binds | New candidate, 60.0 |

### The five frontier families — screen verdicts (one search round each; all verdicts registered)

The Cycle-5 handoff named five mechanism families that had not been screened. Each was screened in
Cycle 6 against the published literature, one search round each, and **every verdict is registered
here including the negative ones**, as the handoff required.

- **Family 1 — statutory, date-bound obligations outside the EU ETS (the IDEA-013 template in a
  different market). VERDICT: MECHANISM FAMILY UNCLAIMED IN THIS FORM; ONE NEW CANDIDATE WRITTEN
  UP (IDEA-014).** The template — a price-insensitive, date-bound purchase obligation whose
  concession is paid by the obligated party — is published for the EU ETS (S-101: surrender-month
  returns "averaging more than 10% in Aprils"), so that version is a baseline. The **US Renewable
  Fuel Standard** is the same template with a different regulator, a different obligor population
  and, decisively, **a state that is published before the decision**: the obligation's size is
  codified in advance (S-128, S-130), the deadline is a published, derived quantity (S-129), and
  the deadline has already been moved once by published rule (S-131) — a fact that is itself a
  warning to any rule built on it. The RIN price is a studied, peer-reviewed object (S-134, S-135),
  it has a *published* price series (S-132) and a *tradeable* futures venue (S-136). No located
  work presents a **state-conditioned compliance-deadline concession** in this market. The
  controlling negative is cost and liquidity: the RIN market is documented as roughly one-tenth as
  liquid as comparable commodity futures, with transaction costs peaking at five times theirs, and
  as pricing near-expiry vintages *above* longer-lived ones (S-133). **This is a bounded negative:
  one search round was run, and the residual is precisely specified in G-15.**
- **Family 2 — sovereign issuance calendars (US Treasury quarterly refunding). VERDICT: PUBLISHED —
  BASELINE.** The quarterly refunding announcement is an issuer-run, published, recurring event
  (S-143), and its price consequences are already the subject of published work: a paper
  decomposing "debt supply shocks using Treasury auction announcements" (S-142) and a paper on
  "pre-refunding announcement gains" in Treasuries (S-144). The announcement-window question is
  therefore *not* a discovery, and the announcement is a **news** event whose surprise component
  requires expectations that are not published, so the residual is not a state-conditioned
  concession at all. No candidate.
- **Family 3 — fast-payment rails (FedNow, UK RTP, EU TIPS). VERDICT: PUBLISHED MECHANISM, NO
  CANDIDATE STRUCTURE — BASELINE.** The mechanism is published and documented: instant payments
  raise banks' liquidity demand because they remove the ability to delay and net payment flows
  (S-140), and 24/7 settlement is modelled as inducing a precautionary demand for central bank
  balances with upward pressure on the overnight rate (S-141). But the family's decision-time
  object is a **rail's adoption curve**, not a dated obligation: there is no date-bound,
  price-insensitive flow to capture, and the effect is a *level* shift in reserve demand that is
  repriced as it is learned rather than conceded. No candidate.
- **Family 4 — sovereign bond index inclusion and exclusion. VERDICT: PUBLISHED, PEER REVIEWED —
  BASELINE.** Inclusion-driven flows into local-currency sovereign debt indices are a published
  causal object: sovereign yields fall and the currency appreciates (S-137), rebalancing flows move
  prices, deepen the market and appreciate the currency with placebo tests around the event
  (S-138), and the scale and the correlated-yield consequences are documented institutionally
  (S-139). The equity-reconstitution analogue is IDEA-001's own territory. No candidate.
- **Family 5 — physical-delivery calendars in commodity futures. VERDICT: PUBLISHED MECHANICS —
  BASELINE.** The inventory-and-convenience-yield channel that makes a delivery calendar a price
  determinant is already registered in this ledger (S-038, S-039), the roll mechanics that generate
  the delivery-month pattern are peer reviewed (S-145 alongside S-032, S-033, S-034, S-036), and
  the *published* state the family would need — exchange warehouse stocks and delivery calendars —
  is the same published-inventory channel already in the baseline set. No candidate.

### New gaps opened in Cycle 6

- **G-14 (new) — a public borrow-cost / securities-lending-rate series.** Opened in Cycle 6 by the
  IDEA-008 parking. The binding constraint on IDEA-008 is not that no lender reports loan rates —
  it is that **no institution publishes them on a public schedule before 29 March 2029** (S-126).
  Until then, the candidate cannot be specified on public data, and re-specifying it around a
  different state (short interest, short volume, dealer-balance-sheet proxies) would be exactly
  the substitution the register's own rules forbid, because those measures do not measure borrow
  cost. **Unblocking condition, dated:** on or after 29 March 2029, re-read the Rule 10c-1a
  dissemination data (the RNSA's public reporting of Rule 10c-1a information, including loan-rate
  terms) and test whether a public, dated, security-level borrow-cost series exists with a
  publication date that precedes a decision date; only then re-open IDEA-008. Also note that the
  related aggregate short-position regime (Rule 13f-2 / Form SHO) has its own dated start —
  "until January 2, 2028" (S-126) — and is a *different* measure (positions, not borrow cost).
- **G-15 (new) — the RIN compliance-deadline price pattern.** Opened in Cycle 6 by the IDEA-014
  write-up. The question is precisely: **does the published weekly volume-weighted-average RIN
  price series (S-132) show a systematic difference between the pre-deadline and post-deadline
  windows, and is the sign of that difference predicted by the published net obligated position
  (published RVO minus published RIN generation)?** Three things are needed and none is available
  in this repository: (i) the price series itself, by RIN type, around the statutory deadlines
  (S-129), including the two *moved* deadlines in 2026 (S-131); (ii) the net obligated position
  state, which requires EPA's published generation data reconciled against the codified standards
  (S-128, S-130) and the small-refinery exemptions; (iii) a cost estimate for the venue, against
  the published market-quality evidence (S-133). **The sign is not asserted anywhere in IDEA-014** —
  it is the candidate's first empirical test, and it is why the candidate is scored at 60.0 and not
  higher.

  **Cycle 7 (bounded search verdict):** A bounded literature/search round (peer-reviewed RIN literature
  S-134, S-135; market-quality S-133; EPA regulatory texts S-128–S-131; EPA price/generation pages
  S-132) found **no published work that identifies a price pattern around the RFS compliance-reporting
  deadline conditioned on the published net obligated position, or that states the sign of such an
  effect.** EPA acknowledges the market may need time to react to compliance/SRE decisions (S-131) but
  provides no sign/magnitude. The deadline is known to move (S-131). The cost floor is material
  (S-133). **G-15 is therefore closed as a bounded negative on the published-literature side: the
  conditioning variable is unclaimed in the literature, so IDEA-014 is not a re-description of a
  published result; it remains a candidate.** Its sign is still unasserted and remains the candidate's
  first empirical test. Score unchanged at 60.0.
  (S-133). This is a **bounded negative** — novelty/sign remains untested; the search converted
  "unsearched" to "searched once and not found". The candidate stays at **60.0** (rank 7) pending
  data reconstruction/cost estimation; no validation specification written.
- **G-16 (new) — a public, validated proxy for dealer balance-sheet stress in credit.** Opened in
  Cycle 6 by the IDEA-010 re-score. IDEA-010's *differentiating* state is AP balance-sheet stress,
  which is commercial; the published substitutes (the Federal Reserve's standing repo facility
  take-up, TRACE dealer-position and interdealer-spread measures) are *different constructs* and
  have not been validated against any dealer-balance-sheet measure. The register does not permit
  re-specifying around an unvalidated proxy, so IDEA-010 is retained **only** with the condition
  that this validation is performed before any specification is written.

---

## Cycle 8 — primary gaps (bounded-negative closures, 2026-10-20)

- **G-16 — closed as bounded negative (literature side).** Web research confirms no freely available, decision-time public proxy for dealer balance-sheet stress in credit: dealer balance-sheet data remain proprietary/non-public, and published substitutes (standing repo facility take-up; TRACE dealer-position and interdealer-spread measures) are different constructs that have not been validated against any dealer-balance-sheet measure. IDEA-010's precondition therefore remains unmet; IDEA-010 stays retained only on the condition that a validated proxy is later found, and no validation specification is written. (Consistent with the prior handoff's stated finding; the search provider rate-limited during this cycle, so this is a bounded negative, not an exhaustive search.)
- **G-04 — IDEA-004 crowd-depth proxy validity: closed as bounded negative (literature side).** A bounded search confirms no freely available, decision-time public crowd-depth series exists for U.S. equities. The 13F-based crowding measures (S-026, and subsequent commercial implementations) are published with the 45-day filing lag and are stale for intraday/multi-day   decision timing in the sense required; borrow depth and true exit capacity remain broker-gated/proprietary and are not published. The publication cadence is feasible on a quarterly-anchored rule (filing cadence), so the binding constraint is proxy *validity*, not timeliness. IDEA-004 (rank 2) therefore remains constrained by this unclosable data gap (parked in effect on this precondition); no new public proxy was identified.

---

## Cycle 13 additions (S-146 … S-174) — the index-reconstitution corpus, the single-name variance-premium literature, the crowding proxies, and six frontier screens

Cycle 13's centre of gravity is the published 2024–2026 literature on index reconstitution. Four
entries (S-146, S-147, S-148, S-151) together determine the fate of the register's rank-1 candidate,
IDEA-001. The remainder close or bound the open gaps and record the frontier screens.

### S-146 — Greenwood, R. and Sammon, M. (2025)
"The Disappearing Index Effect." *The Journal of Finance* 80(2): 657–698.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/jofi.13410 · working-paper version: NBER Working Paper No. 30748
(December 2022), DOI https://doi.org/10.3386/w30748
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed), with a published working-paper version.
- **Supports:** the S&P 500 *index effect* has collapsed. The abnormal return associated with an
  addition has fallen from an average of 7.4% in the 1990s to **less than 1% over the past decade**;
  deletions, which had large negative abnormal returns in the 1980s–1990s, average **0.1% between
  2010 and 2020**. The working-paper version of the same study reports the same collapse on a slightly
  different vintage of numbers (3.4% in the 1980s, 7.6% in the 1990s, **0.8%** over the past decade;
  deletions **−0.6%** for 2010–2020) — the *magnitudes are version-dependent, the collapse is not*.
  The working paper also states that "there is no apparent relationship between net purchases
  [by index trackers] and the index inclusion effect, for either additions or deletions", and reports
  an average inclusion effect in 2020 of **−3 basis points**.
- **Caveats:** the paper's "index effect" is the cumulative market-adjusted return from the last
  trading day before the announcement to the first trading day after implementation, decomposed into
  an announcement return and an implementation return — i.e. the same window IDEA-001 leg 1 trades.
  The working-paper text was read through the HBS-hosted PDF via the search index; the published
  abstract was read on the Wiley landing page via the search index; the NBER abstract was fetched
  directly.
- **Verification:** NBER working-paper page fetched directly (title, authors, abstract, December 2022
  issue date, DOI, and the "Published Versions" line pointing to JF 80(2) 657–698); Wiley and HBS
  landing pages read via the search index; DOI and journal metadata verified through the Crossref
  REST API.

### S-147 — Pegoraro, L., Sammon, M. and Shim, J. (2026)
"Optimal Index-Linked Rebalancing with Anticipatory Trading." Working paper, May 2026 draft
(SSRN 6772502).
URL: https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=6772502
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported), **working paper — not
  peer-reviewed**.
- **Supports:** the mechanism that explains S-146. Speculators act as *anticipatory liquidity
  providers* around reconstitution: they pre-position before the effective date, which "shifts price
  pressure away from the reconstitution date"; "apparent front-running reflects competitive liquidity
  provision and benefits index investors"; trading costs decline as speculator competition rises;
  short interest **rises on implementation dates and falls afterwards** as speculators cover; the
  authors estimate that avoidable rebalancing costs borne by index-linked investors are small.
- **Caveats:** working paper; the principal application is the S&P 500; the specific empirical
  magnitudes quoted for implementation-day effects were read through the author's summary page and a
  2026 arXiv preprint that cites them, not from the paper's own tables.
- **Verification:** author/institution pages and the SSRN record read via the search index; SSRN
  direct fetch returned HTTP 403.

### S-148 — Chinco, A. and Sammon, M. (2024)
"The passive ownership share is double what you think it is." *Journal of Financial Economics* 157:
103860.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jfineco.2024.103860
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports:** (i) the passive ownership share of U.S. equities was **33.3% in 2021**, roughly double
  the 16% figure implied by index-fund ownership alone; (ii) a **construction method** for
  benchmark-specific tracked assets: for each index, the AUM tracking it is inferred from
  reconstitution-day closing volume, for the Russell 1000, Russell 2000, S&P 500, S&P MidCap 400 and
  Nasdaq 100, over 2000–2021; (iii) passive rebalancing "gets executed at the closing price on
  reconstitution day". This is the published answer to the register's G-02/G-07 question (how one
  might estimate the size of index-linked assets for a *specific* benchmark) and part of the answer
  to G-03.
- **Caveats:** the published estimates are annual point estimates for five indices through 2021, not
  a downloadable daily series; the underlying input is per-security closing-auction volume, whose
  long history is commercial; the paper is about the *size* of passive assets, not about price
  effects of reconstitution.
- **Verification:** journal landing page (title, authors, abstract) read via the search index; DOI,
  volume and article number verified through the Crossref REST API.

### S-149 — State Street Global Advisors (2026)
"Closing time: How passive investing is reshaping equity market microstructure." SSGA Investment
Management Insights, 23 January 2026.
URL: https://www.ssga.com/au/en_gb/institutional/insights/how-passive-investing-reshaping-microstructure
- **Type:** Institutional research / marketing communication from the index manager itself
  (**interested party**).
- **Supports:** the effective-close convention directly, in the index manager's own words: "Index
  funds and ETFs are structurally incentivized to execute trades at the closing auction because
  official net asset values (NAVs) are calculated using closing prices, and any deviation can result
  in tracking error. Consequently, Market-on-Close (MOC) orders have become the dominant mechanism
  for end-of-day execution." Also: the US closing auction was **15.39% of daily volume in December
  2025** versus 8.40% in January 2018; developed-Europe 34.82%; Asia ex-Japan 16.78%.
- **Caveats:** an interested party describing its own practice; not an independent measurement of
  where passive funds actually trade; no rule text, no audit.
- **Verification:** page fetched directly (full text read, including the quoted sentence and the
  volume-allocation table).

### S-150 — Goyal, A., Jegadeesh, N. and Wu, Y. (2026)
"Price Impact in Closing Auctions, Opening Auctions, and Continuous Markets: A Benchmark for Cost of
Trading on Anomalies." *Journal of Financial and Quantitative Analysis* 61(6): 2620–2655.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1017/S0022109026102592
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports:** closing-auction volume has risen from roughly 4% to roughly 10% of ADV (average
  7.10%, median 5.34%); **price impact is lower in the closing auction than in the continuous
  market** for all stocks except Nasdaq micro-caps; closing-auction volume is significantly associated
  with the creation/redemption activity of passive index funds; Friday and triple-witching spikes are
  larger; the closing auction attracts relatively uninformed traders.
- **Caveats:** the passive-attribution result is an association with creation/redemption activity, not
  an identification of index-fund order flow; the price-impact comparison is about *continuous-market
  trades*, not about reconstitution events.
- **Verification:** journal landing page (title, authors, abstract) read via the search index; DOI,
  volume, issue and pages verified through the Crossref REST API.

### S-151 — Sammon, M. and Shim, J. (2026)
"Who Clears the Market When Passive Investors Trade?" *The Review of Financial Studies* 39(10):
3330–3377.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rfs/hhag032
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports:** the counterparty to a passive fund's demand is **the issuing firm itself** (firms are
  the primary sellers who clear index-fund buying), and most demand-side institutions trade *in the
  same direction* as index funds — i.e. passive demand is not offset by an opposing arbitrageur who
  would pay away the concession. This is the structural argument that the price concession at
  reconstitution is not naturally competed away, and it is the strongest published evidence *for*
  IDEA-001's persistence thesis.
- **Caveats:** it is evidence about who the counterparty is, not about the size of any price
  concession in the modern sample; the two papers must be read together with S-146 and S-147.
- **Verification:** journal landing page (title, authors, abstract) read via the search index; DOI,
  volume, issue and pages verified through the Crossref REST API.

### S-152 — Sammon, M. and Shim, J. (2026)
"Index rebalancing and stock market composition: Do indexes time the market?" *Journal of Financial
Economics* 177: 104229.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jfineco.2025.104229 · earlier working-paper version: SSRN 5080459
("Index Rebalancing and Stock Market Composition: Do Index Funds Incur Adverse Selection Costs?")
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed), with a sign-flipped working-paper version.
- **Supports:** a *value-weighted composition* channel (issuance, buybacks and IPOs changing index
  weights) distinct from S&P 500 reconstitution: index funds' long-short rebalancing portfolios earn
  about **4.61% annualised** in the published version, with a rebalancing drag of 46–69 basis points.
- **Caveats:** the working-paper version reports **−4.67%** annualised — the *sign flips between
  versions*, a direct warning to the register about relying on a single pre-publication number; the
  mechanism is composition-driven, not reconstitution-driven.
- **Verification:** DOI and journal metadata verified through the Crossref REST API (title and
  bibliography query); abstracts read via the search index.

### S-153 — Driessen, J., Maenhout, P. J. and Vilkov, G. (2009)
"The Price of Correlation Risk: Evidence from Equity Options." *The Journal of Finance* 64(3):
1377–1406.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1540-6261.2009.01467.x
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (as a bounded negative for IDEA-003's single-name version):** individual equity options
  do **not** embed a negative variance risk premium — single-stock variance risk premia are
  frequently zero or of the *opposite* sign to the index-level premium, and single-stock variance
  swap returns are insignificant; what individual options price is *correlation* risk.
- **Caveats:** 2009 sample (pre-2010), so it is evidence about the *sign* of the single-name
  premium, not about its current magnitude; it is an option-pricing result, not a trading-strategy
  result.
- **Verification:** Wiley landing page (title, authors, abstract) read via the search index; DOI,
  volume, issue and pages verified through the Crossref REST API.

### S-154 — Carr, P. and Wu, L. (2009)
"Variance Risk Premia." *The Review of Financial Studies* 22(3): 1311–1341.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rfs/hhn038
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (as a bounded negative for IDEA-003's single-name version):** variance risk premia on
  individual stocks are frequently zero or positive; the negative premium is a large-index phenomenon
  (the S&P 100 carries a negative premium), and individual-stock returns do not load on a variance
  risk premium factor.
- **Caveats:** as S-153 — a pricing result on an older sample; it does not test a gated trading rule.
- **Verification:** Crossref REST API (title, authors, journal, volume, issue, pages, publication
  date) plus the journal landing page via the search index.

### S-155 — Andersen, L., Duffie, D. and Song, Y. (2019)
"Funding Value Adjustments." *The Journal of Finance* 74(1): 145–192.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/jofi.12739 · working-paper version: NBER Working Paper No. 23680
- **Type:** Primary theory (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for G-01):** the *construct* behind the register's dealer funding-state gap is
  well-defined and peer-reviewed: a dealer's funding value adjustment is a debt-overhang cost to its
  shareholders, and dealer quotations adjust for FVAs in order to align with shareholder interests.
- **Caveats:** the paper is a theory of FVAs; it does not publish a continuous, public FVA series,
  and no such series exists — the closest published dealer-funding-terms measures are the Federal
  Reserve's quarterly **Senior Credit Officer Opinion Survey** (qualitative net percentages) and the
  NY Fed's daily SOFR and SOFR percentile-spread statistics.
- **Verification:** Wiley landing page and NBER working-paper PDF read via the search index; DOI,
  volume, issue and pages verified through the Crossref REST API.

### S-156 — Ben-David, I., Li, J. and Rossi, A. (2023)
"Discontinued Positive Feedback Trading and the Decline of Return Predictability." *Journal of
Financial and Quantitative Analysis* 59(7): 3062–3100.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1017/S0022109023000959 · working-paper version: NBER Working Paper No. 28624
("Discontinued Positive Feedback Trading and the Decline of Momentum Profitability")
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for IDEA-004):** the momentum factor's returns fell from **0.92% per month** (January
  1987 – June 2002) to **0.16% per month** afterwards, with an identified mechanism (positive-feedback
  trading in analyst ratings ended after the mid-2002 Morningstar rating reform); factor-momentum
  strategies show the same decline. Published evidence that the *base effect* which IDEA-004's
  crowding gate is supposed to condition has decayed sharply and for a documented reason.
- **Caveats:** the subject is cross-sectional equity momentum, which IDEA-004's own dossier lists as
  a *rival explanation* for its convergence returns rather than as its base effect; the post-reform
  return is small but still positive.
- **Verification:** Crossref REST API (title, authors, journal, volume, issue, pages) plus the NBER
  working-paper text via the search index.

### S-157 — Lou, D. and Polk, C. (2021)
"Comomentum: Inferring Arbitrage Activity from Return Correlations." *The Review of Financial Studies*
35(6): 3272–3302.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rfs/hhab117 · working-paper version: NBER Working Paper No. 9664
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for IDEA-004, prediction 4, and for the new gap G-18):** crowding/arbitrage-activity
  intensity is inferred from *return correlations* — a measure computable from public price data
  alone, i.e. **decision-time and free**, unlike the 13F-based holdings measure (S-026). This is the
  first candidate for a *timely* crowd-depth proxy found in thirteen cycles of the register.
- **Caveats:** it is a proxy for arbitrage activity, not for the *position concentration* of
  arbitrage capital that S-026 measures; whether it is a valid substitute for the holdings-based
  measure has never been tested in this register — that is exactly what G-18 is opened for.
- **Verification:** Crossref REST API (title, authors, journal, volume, issue, pages) plus the
  journal landing page via the search index.

### S-158 — Gornall, G., Xiao, Y. and Rinaldi, M. (2025)
"Perpetual Futures and Basis Risk: Evidence from Cryptocurrency." Working paper, May 2025
(SSRN 5036933); paper presented at the AEA 2026 Annual Meeting programme.
URL: https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=5036933
- **Type:** Primary theory + prior empirical result (author-reported), **working paper — not
  peer-reviewed**.
- **Supports (for IDEA-009):** the mechanism behind the funding-rate channel is now published
  outside the register: perpetual futures track spot more tightly because continuous funding payments
  discipline the basis; a higher funding rate *attenuates* the sensitivity of the perpetual price to
  demand shocks; and perpetual-futures drawdowns **quickly mean-revert** while quarterly drawdowns
  persist.
- **Caveats:** working paper; the drawdown mean-reversion result is about basis deviations, not about
  a signed, dated funding-level trading rule.
- **Verification:** AEA conference programme pages and the authors' CFTC comment letter read via the
  search index; SSRN direct fetch not performed (HTTP 403 on the ID landing page).

### S-159 — "Perpetual futures as predictors of Bitcoin volatility" (2026)
*Journal of Empirical Finance* 89: 101779.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jempfin.2026.101779
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for IDEA-009):** funding rates provide **incremental predictive information** for spot
  *volatility* at daily, weekly and monthly horizons; the interpretation is that funding rates
  capture imbalanced leveraged positions that are vulnerable to subsequent unwinding.
- **Caveats:** the return *direction* is not the tested object — the paper forecasts volatility, not
  returns, and does not test a signed reversal rule.
- **Verification:** Crossref REST API (title, journal, volume, article number, publication date) plus
  the publisher landing page via the search index.

### S-160 — Dao, M., Tan, B. J. and Zhou, J. (2025)
"Repo Market Volatility and the U.S. Debt Ceiling." IMF Working Paper, June 2025.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.5089/9798229015219.001
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported), working paper.
- **Supports:** Treasury General Account flows are an identified driver of short-term funding-market
  stress: TGA flows triggered by the debt-ceiling mechanism "can create large swings in the repo
  spread" and distort the supply of repo funding, with a projected 20–30 basis point repo-spread
  fluctuation around a debt-ceiling lift-off.
- **Caveats:** working paper; the identification is around debt-ceiling episodes, not a general
  trading rule; the register's G-01 question (a *dealer* FVA proxy) is not answered.
- **Verification:** Crossref REST API (title, authors, IMF Working Papers series, year) plus the IMF
  working-paper landing page via the search index.

### S-161 — Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System (2025)
"The Repo Rate Sensitivity to Treasury Issuance and Quantitative Tightening." FEDS Notes, 12 February
2025.
URL: https://www.federalreserve.gov/econres/notes/Feds-notes/repo-rate-sensitivity-to-treasury-issuance-and-quantitative-tightening-20250212.html
- **Type:** Central-bank research note (staff analysis, not peer-reviewed).
- **Supports:** the published mechanism set for funding-market sensitivity — repo-rate sensitivity to
  Treasury issuance rises as the Fed's balance sheet shrinks, with the SOMA, the ON RRP facility and
  the TGA named as the channels; dealer balance-sheet costs are explicitly modelled.
- **Caveats:** a note, not a refereed paper; its subject is *sensitivity*, not a trading rule.
- **Verification:** landing page read via the search index; the note's own page and PDF are public.

### S-162 — Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System (2026)
"Repo Markets and the Fed's Balance Sheet: Implications for Monetary Policy Implementation." FEDS
Notes, 26 August 2026.
URL: https://www.federalreserve.gov/econres/notes/feds-notes/repo-markets-and-the-feds-balance-sheet-implications-for-monetary-policy-implementation-20260826.html
- **Type:** Central-bank research note (staff analysis).
- **Supports:** the TGCR − IORB spread is the published, decision-time measure of funding-market
  pressure (both series are public and daily); the note quantifies the effect of net Treasury
  issuance on that spread as a function of the liquidity level, and documents the September 2019
  episode in which SOFR rose above 280 basis points.
- **Caveats:** not peer-reviewed; descriptive of the monetary-policy implementation channel.
- **Verification:** landing page read via the search index.

### S-163 — Bushnell, J. B. (2017)
"Carbon Pricing and Revenue Uncertainty in California." Author-hosted manuscript, National Tax
Journal layout (University of California, Davis).
URL: https://bushnell.ucdavis.edu/uploads/7/6/9/5/76951361/a00_bushnell_02.pdf
- **Type:** Prior empirical/institutional result (author-reported); **final journal metadata not
  confirmed** (the Crossref record returned by the title query is a different Bushnell NTJ article).
- **Supports (for the Cycle-13 California screen):** the California price-collar design, and the key
  fact that the Allowance Price Containment Reserve **"has not been called into use"** through 2017,
  with allowance prices at or near the auction floor for most of the programme's life.
- **Caveats:** the manuscript's page header reads "National Tax Journal"; the article number and
  issue could not be verified through Crossref in this cycle, so the citation is to the
  author-hosted version.
- **Verification:** PDF read via the search index; Crossref title query for the exact title returned
  an unrelated record.

### S-164 — California Air Resources Board (2018)
"Reserve Sale Notice — Sale of Greenhouse Gas Allowances from the Allowance Price Containment Reserve
on October 2, 2018" (issued 31 August 2018) and "2018 Annual Allowance Price Containment Reserve
Notice" (issued 1 December 2017).
URLs: https://ww2.arb.ca.gov/sites/default/files/cap-and-trade/reservesale/oct-2018/notice.pdf ·
https://ww2.arb.ca.gov/sites/default/files/cap-and-trade/reservesale/2018_reserve_sale_apcr_notice.pdf
- **Type:** Primary regulatory source (the regulator's own published notices).
- **Supports (the published-state inventory for the California screen):** a Reserve sale is offered
  **only if the Current Auction held in the preceding quarter settled at or above 60% of the lowest
  Reserve tier price**, except for the sale immediately preceding the 1 November compliance
  obligation instrument surrender deadline, which is held "without regard to the settlement price";
  Reserve sales are scheduled in **Appendix C of the Cap-and-Trade Regulation**; notices are posted
  approximately 30 days before each sale; the tier prices and the allowances allocated to each tier
  are published annually in an annual notice.
- **Caveats:** these are 2018 documents; the tier prices and quantities are re-published every year
  and the schedule can be revised by the Executive Officer; the notice states the *offer*, not
  whether any allowances are actually taken up; the specific 2018 tier prices and quantities were
  read through the search index and could not be re-verified by direct fetch (the CARB PDF returned
  an empty response on a second attempt), so **no numeric tier price is asserted anywhere in the
  register**.
- **Verification:** both PDFs read via the search index; both are public CARB documents; a direct
  re-fetch of the annual notice returned a zero-byte response, which is recorded as an unverified
  detail rather than passed on as fact.

### S-165 — Congressional Research Service (2024)
"The California Cap-and-Trade Program: Overview and Considerations for Congress." R48314, 18
December 2024.
URL: https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/PDF/R48314/R48314.2.pdf
- **Type:** Government research report.
- **Supports:** programme design and published state: 48 auctions held by November 2024; prices from a
  $10 floor (2013) to nearly $42 (February 2024), $37 (May 2024) and $32 (November 2024); the
  1 November surrender deadline; the AB 398 "hard" price ceiling with price ceiling units; offset
  limits stepping down from 8% to 4% (2021–2025) and 6% (2026–2030).
- **Caveats:** a secondary summary for Congress; the underlying auction data are CARB's.
- **Verification:** PDF and congress.gov landing page read via the search index.

### S-166 — Erickson, Z., Fang, T. and Howe, K. (2011)
"Accelerated share repurchases." *Journal of Financial Economics* 101(1): 69–89.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jfineco.2011.02.004
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports:** the accelerated-share-repurchase family is documented: ASRs are credible commitments
  to repurchase immediately, the intermediary borrows the shares and covers over a specified period,
  and the *choice* to use an ASR is significantly related to share-price variability and illiquidity,
  with announcement effects.
- **Caveats:** the ASR *discount* and the intermediary's execution are private; the disclosure of
  volumes and dates is quarterly (10-Q), so a decision-time state is weak.
- **Verification:** Crossref REST API (title, authors, journal, volume, issue, pages) plus the
  publisher landing page via the search index.

### S-167 — Guéant, O., Pu, J. and Royer, G. (2015)
"Accelerated Share Repurchase: Pricing and Execution Strategy." *International Journal of Theoretical
and Applied Finance* 18(2): 1550019.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1142/S0219024915500193
- **Type:** Primary theory (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports:** the ASR contract's payoff is an Asian-type option with Bermudan-style exercise dates,
  and the intermediary's problem is an optimal-execution problem with temporary and permanent market
  impact; the ASR "discount" is the market price of that optionality.
- **Caveats:** a pricing model; the resulting signal is private-contract data, not a published series.
- **Verification:** Crossref REST API (title, authors, journal, volume, article number) plus the
  publisher landing page and the arXiv working-paper version via the search index.

### S-168 — Borenstein, S., Bushnell, J., Wolak, F. A. and Zaragoza-Watkins, M. (2019)
"Expecting the Unexpected: Emissions Uncertainty and Environmental Market Design." *American Economic
Review* 109(11): 3953–3977.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1257/aer.20161218 · working-paper version: NBER Working Paper No. 20999
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports:** the California market's price collar is a designed, frequently-binding device: with
  inelastic abatement supply, the allowance price is likely to sit at either the floor or high enough
  to trigger the Allowance Price Containment Reserve, and the probability of the intermediate range is
  low; the register's initial Allowance Price Containment Reserve allocation was of the order of 120
  million allowances at three fixed tier prices (the allocation size is index-rendered; the tier
  prices are not asserted here).
- **Caveats:** a design/estimation study on an older vintage of the programme; it does not price the
  effect of any individual reserve sale; the allocation figure was read through the search index.
- **Verification:** Crossref REST API (title, authors, journal, volume, issue, pages) plus the NBER
  working-paper text via the search index.

### S-169 — U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (2026)
"Roundtable on Options Market Structure — Supporting Data" (staff presentation material, April 2026).
URL: https://www.sec.gov/files/roundtable-options-market-structure.pdf
- **Type:** Regulator staff material (presentation data, not a refereed study).
- **Supports (for G-05):** 0DTE options reached **28% of total options volume** by 2025 (19.6% in
  2022), concentrated in index products and a small set of single names (TSLA, NVDA, AAPL among the
  leaders); by the end of 2025 roughly **69.5% of listed equities** had options trading, and
  single-name 0DTE volume is concentrated in a small number of underlyings.
- **Caveats:** staff presentation material — descriptive counts, not tests; a direct fetch returned
  HTTP 403, so the figures were read through the search index.
- **Verification:** URL and figures read via the search index; SEC direct fetch returned HTTP 403.

### S-170 — Keyrock (2026) — token-unlock event study (market-maker research, quoted in a public
unlock-calendar service)
"Market maker Keyrock analysed more than 16,000 unlock events and found that roughly 90 percent were
followed by negative price pressure, regardless of the event's size or recipient type… prices
typically start declining about 30 days before an unlock… team unlocks were the most disruptive".
URL: https://www.datawallet.com/token-unlock-calendar
- **Type:** Vendor / market-maker research reported by a commercial data service
  (**interested party, not peer-reviewed, not independently verifiable**).
- **Supports (for the Cycle-13 token-unlock screen):** the token-unlock channel is *documented*, not
  unclaimed: the sign (down into the unlock), the pre-event drift window, the recipient-type

- **Caveats:** interested party; no method, sample or data made available; not reproducible from the

### S-171 — Fiechtner, L.-B. and Blanchet, J. (2026)
"Strategic Index Reconstitution: Differential Games, Closed-Loop Equilibria and Mean-Field ..."
arXiv:2609.15901 (14 September 2026).
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15901
- **Type:** Preprint (not peer-reviewed).
- **Supports:** a second, independent 2026 model of *anticipatory trading around index
  reconstitution*, built on S-147's anticipatory-liquidity framing; it quotes the S&P 500

  average abnormal return of about −0.09% for additions from outside the index family).

### S-172 — Lee, C. (2025)
"Return-Decay Residuals and Tail-Risk Forecasting: Timing Artifacts, Conditional Inference, ..."
arXiv:2512.11913 (11 December 2025; earlier versions were titled "Not All Factors Crowd Equally:
Modeling, Measuring, and Trading on Alpha Decay").
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.11913
- **Type:** Preprint (not peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for IDEA-004):** out-of-sample 2001–2024 evidence that **crowding-based factor
  selection fails to generate alpha** (Sharpe 0.22 against a 0.39 factor-momentum benchmark), that
  crowding predicts *tail risk* rather than average returns, and that cross-sectional factor timing
  does not work; the momentum factor is estimated to earn ~2% annualised today against ~10% in the
  1990s.
- **Caveats:** preprint; **the title changed between versions** — a concrete instance of version drift
  in the citation record, and a caution for the register's own rule about pre-publication numbers
  (compare S-152). The substantive figures quoted above are from the *earlier* version's abstract,
  read through the search index; the v3 title and metadata were confirmed directly through the arXiv
  API, but the v3 abstract text itself was not parsed here, so **the numbers are attributed to the
  earlier version and are used only as a bounded negative, never as a load-bearing citation**.
- **Verification:** arXiv API record fetched directly (identifier, v3 title, author, submission date);
  earlier-version abstract read via the search index.

### S-173 — Congressional Budget Office (2010)
"Managing Allowance Prices in a Cap-and-Trade Program." CBO study, November 2010.
URL: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/GOVPUB-Y10-PURL-gpo824/pdf/GOVPUB-Y10-PURL-gpo824.pdf
- **Type:** Government research study.
- **Supports:** the design economics of allowance reserves and price collars — a reserve of
  supplemental allowances released at a pre-set access price dampens allowance prices; a reserve
  built by withholding allowances tightens the cap — i.e. the theory of the mechanism IDEA-013 /
  IDEA-014 and the California screen share.
- **Caveats:** a design study on a hypothetical programme, not an evaluation of any actual reserve
  sale.
- **Verification:** PDF read via the search index; govinfo copy is public.

### S-174 — California Code of Regulations, title 17, §§ 95856, 95857, 95913
"The Cap-and-Trade Regulation" (unofficial electronic version published by CARB), including the
deadline for surrender of annual compliance obligations (§ 95856), untimely surrender of compliance
instruments (§ 95857) and sale of allowances from the Allowance Price Containment Reserve (§ 95913).
URL: https://ww2.arb.ca.gov/sites/default/files/2021-02/ct_reg_unofficial.pdf
- **Type:** Primary legal source (codified regulation).
- **Supports:** the California deadline is a *published statutory obligation*: annual compliance
  obligations must be fulfilled **by 1 November, 5:00 p.m. Pacific**; failure triggers an **untimely
  surrender obligation of four times** the outstanding obligation (§ 95857) — a punitive,
  price-inelastic requirement; and the reserve-sale trigger is codified, not discretionary.
- **Caveats:** an "unofficial electronic version"; the official text is the OAL edition; the
  regulation is amended over time (August 2026 amendments defer Independent Merchant Refinery
  obligations, per a secondary law-firm summary).
- **Verification:** PDF read via the search index; CARB-hosted document.

### Cycle-13 gap verdicts

- **G-02 / G-07 — CLOSED (method and published estimates exist; no downloadable series).** S-148
  publishes both a *construction method* for benchmark-specific tracked assets (inferred from
  reconstitution-day closing volume, for five named indices, 2000–2021) and the resulting estimates.
  The register's standing question — "is a benchmark-specific tracked-asset series constructible?" —
  is therefore answered *yes, and it has been done*: the remaining obstacle is data engineering
  (long-history per-security closing-auction volume is commercial), not theory. **The gap is closed
  as a construction question.** It was the only consumer, IDEA-001, which was rejected in Cycle 13 on
  independent grounds (see R-011); the gap is retained as a reusable method note, not as an open
  blocker.
- **G-03 — CLOSED.** The index funds' effective-close convention is now documented from two
  independent directions: the index manager itself (S-149: index funds and ETFs are "structurally
  incentivized to execute trades at the closing auction" because NAVs are struck on closing prices,
  with MOC orders dominant) and the peer-reviewed literature (S-150 on closing-auction volume share,
  price impact and passive attribution; S-148 on execution at the closing price on reconstitution
  day). The premise that index funds no longer trade at the effective close is therefore **false as
  stated** — the register's Cycle-5 question ("do index funds still trade at the effective close?")
  is answered yes, and the answer is published. Its only consumer, IDEA-001, was rejected.
- **G-05 — CLOSED against the single-name version of IDEA-003 (literature side).** The nearest
  published tests of *single-name* variance risk premia (S-153, S-154) find the single-name premium
  frequently zero or of the opposite sign to the index premium, with correlation risk doing the
  pricing work. IDEA-003's retained (aggregate) version is unaffected; its *single-name extension is
  now foreclosed* and must not be proposed as new. The *data* side of G-05 was already closed in
  Cycle 2, and S-169 confirms the structural change (0DTE at 28% of volume, concentrated in index
  products and a handful of single names).
- **G-01 — STILL OPEN, bounded negative re-confirmed with a sharper target.** S-155 establishes what
  a dealer FVA *is* (peer-reviewed); S-160–S-162 establish that the *funding-state* family is
  published (TGA flows → repo spreads; TGCR − IORB as the public decision-time measure). No public,
  continuous, dealer-level funding-value-adjustment series exists and none is likely to: the
  published dealer-funding-terms measures remain the quarterly, qualitative SCOOS and the daily SOFR
  percentile spreads. **G-01 stays open**, now stated as: *can any published, decision-time series
  proxy a dealer's marginal funding cost closely enough to condition a cross-sectional trade?*
- **G-04 — REOPENED IN PART (new proxy identified).** The Cycle-8 closure was right that no
  *holdings-based* timely proxy exists, but S-157 (Lou–Polk "comomentum") shows that an
  *inferred* crowding measure can be computed from public price data at decision time. This does
  **not** resolve IDEA-004: the register does not permit re-specifying around an unvalidated proxy.
  It opens **G-18**: validate a return-correlation crowding proxy against a holdings-based crowding
  measure (S-026).

### S-175 — Armstrong, C. S., Barth, M. E., Jagolinzer, A. D. and Riedl, E. J. (2010)
"Market Reaction to the Adoption of IFRS in Europe." *The Accounting Review* 85(1): 31–61.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.2308/accr.2010.85.1.31
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** Around EU IFRS adoption (2005), positive abnormal returns for firms with lower pre-adoption information quality and in countries with stronger enforcement; the reaction is consistent with expected reduction in information asymmetry. Adoption dates were known in advance (IAS regulation).
- **Caveats:** Sample concentrated in first-time EU IFRS adopters around 2005; largely pre-2010 period. Small number of adoption events.
- **Verification:** DOI checked (The Accounting Review).

### S-176 — Beuselinck, C., Joos, P., Khurana, I. K. and Van der Meulen, S. (2009)
"Mandatory IFRS Reporting and Stock Price Informativeness." Working paper / earlier version cited in literature; published version appears in later work.
DOI: (varies; register cites the mechanism)
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports:** IFRS adoption associated with increased earnings comparability/informativeness in some samples; effects concentrated in firms/countries with stronger enforcement and lower pre-adoption comparability.
- **Caveats:** Evidence mixed across samples/methods; many studies focus on 2005 EU adoption (pre-2010). Post-2010 evidence needs to be checked.
- **Verification:** Metadata noted from standard references in the dossier.

### S-177 — Dew-Becker, I. and Giglio, S. (2025)
"The Decline of the Variance Risk Premium: Evidence from Traded and Synthetic Options." Chicago Fed Working Paper No. 2025-17, September 2025.
URL: https://www.chicagofed.org/publications/working-papers/2025/2025-17
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported), working paper — not peer-reviewed.
- **Supports (for IDEA-003):** equity index options historically displayed sharply negative returns and CAPM alphas, but over the past ~15 years option alphas have become indistinguishable from zero; synthetic options never had negative alpha over the last 100 years, indicating equity investors never required high compensation for market downturns. An intermediary-based model explains both patterns, including the recent decline in the variance risk premium. Consistent with IDEA-003's state-gate framing: the unconditional harvest has weakened, so the gate on *when* to harvest is the load-bearing claim.
- **Caveats:** working paper; documents the decline in traded-option alphas but does not itself specify or test a gate; the aggregate VRP as a published object is unaffected (only harvestability is questioned).
- **Verification:** Chicago Fed Working Papers landing page fetched directly (title, authors, WP number, date, abstract confirmed).

### S-178 — Hung, M., Li, X. and Wang, S. (2014 / published 2015)
"Post-Earnings-Announcement Drift in Global Markets: Evidence from an Information Shock." *The Review of Financial Studies* 28(4): 1242–1277.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rfs/hhu062 · working-paper version: SSRN 2269909
URL: https://academic.oup.com/rfs/article-abstract/28/4/1242/1928671
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for IDEA-011 post-2010 evidence):** PEAD declines after an exogenous improvement in non-US firms' financial reporting quality; the decrease is more pronounced among firms with fewer concurrent earnings announcements, higher institutional holdings, lower limits-to-arbitrage, and in countries with stronger enforcement; driven by firms with greater reporting changes, increased analyst forecast accuracy, institutional ownership and liquidity. Supports the mispricing/slow-diffusion reading of PEAD: better information environment → smaller drift.
- **Caveats:** sample centered on the information-shock event rather than a sequence of adoption dates; does not isolate a dated two-legged announcement-to-first-report trade; pre-2010-adjacent (2005 EU IFRS-adjacent settings appear in the broader literature).
- **Verification:** SSRN abstract and Oxford Academic landing page read via search index; DOI, volume, issue and pages confirmed.

### S-179 — Song, J. and Woo, Y.-S. (2015)
"The Effects of K-IFRS Adoption on the Magnitude of Post Earnings Announcement Drift." DOI: https://doi.org/10.31203/aepa.2015.12.4.006 · Published 2015-12-30.
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported).
- **Supports (for IDEA-011 post-2010 evidence):** analysis of KRX-listed firms 2006–2013 finds K-IFRS adoption had a noticeable negative effect on the size of PEAD; under-reaction of investors decreased due to adoption; PEAD decreased for firms without analysts' earnings forecasts and with positive unexpected earnings. Consistent with adoption improving information quality and shrinking slow-diffusion drift.
- **Caveats:** single jurisdiction (Korea), sample 2006–2013; whole-firm sample did not show an outstanding effect, result concentrated in sub-samples (positive unexpected earnings; no-analyst-forecast firms).
- **Verification:** DOI landing page read via search index; title, authors, date and abstract confirmed.

## Cycle 14 (continued) gap verdicts

- **G-18 — BOUNDED NEGATIVE (no validated substitute; V-002 stays frozen).** G-18 asks whether a return-correlation crowding measure (comomentum, S-157: Lou & Polk 2021, RFS 35(6): 3272–3302) is a valid substitute for a holdings-based crowding measure (S-026: Brown, Howard & Lundblad 2021, RFS 35(7): 3231–3271, built from 13F-style hedge fund holdings with up-to-a-quarter reporting lag). S-157 shows comomentum is *correlated with* existing arbitrage-activity proxies, including institutional ownership of the winner decile (13F-based) and Chen–Da–Huang (2019) net arbitrage trading (abnormal hedge fund holdings minus abnormal short interest), and that comomentum forecasts momentum returns, volatility and skewness. But S-157 never tests whether comomentum is a valid *substitute* for the holdings-based measure, and no head-to-head validation of correlation-based vs holdings-based crowding was located in a bounded search across thirteen cycles. The closest comparison is a factor-crowding study (Dell, "Crowding and Factor Returns", SSRN 3803954) which finds its direct position-based crowding measure remains significant while the Lou–Polk correlation-based proxy adds little incremental predictive power once position-based crowding is included — i.e. the holdings-based measure subsumes rather than is replaced by the correlation-based proxy. Conclusion: comomentum is decision-time and free (S-157) but is **not a validated substitute** for S-026's holdings-based crowding. IDEA-004 must not be re-specified around an unvalidated proxy (register method). **V-002 (IDEA-004) remains frozen pending G-18 closure.** If a direct validation/comparison paper is located in a future cycle, G-18 can be reopened; otherwise it stands as a bounded negative.

## Cycle 30 sources (2026-10-20)

### S-180 — Konstantinidi, E. and Skiadopoulos, G. (2016)
"How does the market variance risk premium vary over time? Evidence from S&P 500 variance swap
investment returns." *Journal of Banking & Finance* 62: 62–75.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jbankfin.2015.10.006 · working-paper version: SSRN 2502822
(https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.2502822, 2014).
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported), peer-reviewed.
- **Supports (for IDEA-003):** the variance risk premium's time variation is predictable from
  decision-time state variables, and **volatility-trading strategies that condition on those states
  outperform both buy-and-hold and the naive short-volatility strategy even after transaction
  costs**; the best-performing specification conditions on trading activity; and broker-dealers
  command a larger premium when trading conditions (their funding) deteriorate, extending the
  broker-dealer funding result of Adrian and Shin. For the register this is **prior art for the
  state-conditioned harvest**: "gate the variance-risk-premium harvest on a decision-time state" is
  a published *strategy family*, not an unclaimed idea. IDEA-003's residual is therefore narrowed to
  (i) the *state* — an option-surface-implied imminent-crash signal rather than trading activity —
  and (ii) the *sign* claim (the premium's harvestability, not merely its size, flips).
- **Caveats:** the paper measures the premium from **variance-swap quotes**, not from listed
  options, and the conditioning variable is trading activity rather than crash risk; it predates the
  modern option market structure (0DTE and retail flow, S-169) and therefore predates S-177's
  finding that traded-option alphas have decayed to approximately zero; it does not test a
  crash-state gate.
- **Verification:** **Crossref API fetched directly** — title, both authors (Eirini Konstantinidi,
  George Skiadopoulos), journal (*Journal of Banking & Finance*), volume 62, pages 62–75, issue date
  2016-01, DOI 10.1016/j.jbankfin.2015.10.006, Elsevier BV, ISSN 0378-4266, and the Elsevier PII
  S0378426615002836 all confirmed in the response. The working-paper identifier (SSRN 2502822,
  DOI 10.2139/ssrn.2502822) was returned by the same Crossref query in a separate row and is
  recorded on that basis. The quoted findings come from search-index excerpts of the published
  abstract and article text (including the working-paper version); the Crossref reference list also
  shows the paper citing Adrian and Shin's "Liquidity and leverage" (*Journal of Financial
  Intermediation* 19: 418), which is consistent with the funding-conditions claim above.

### S-181 — Ahmed, S. (2026)
"The Variance Risk Premium Across Markets and Regimes: A Downside, Crash-Conditional Risk Premium,
Not Harvestable Carry." SSRN working paper 7046559, dated 3 June 2026.
URL: https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=7046559
- **Type:** Preprint (author-reported), **not peer reviewed**.
- **Supports (for IDEA-003 — both as prior art and as a convention warning):** the paper characterises
  the variance risk premium as "a downside, crash-conditional risk premium, compensation for a
  systematic tail rather than harvestable carry"; states that the premium "is crash-conditional only
  in an entry-timing sense (selling volatility into a jump loses)"; states that "the regime-average
  premium stays positive through every crisis and widens in high-stress and high-credit-spread
  states, when fear over-prices the volatility that subsequently mean-reverts"; and concludes that
  the premium is not harvestable carry because "even voltargeted and overlaid it retains an
  irreducible left tail". In register terms: **(a) the *interpretation* the candidate is built on —
  the premium as compensation for a tail, and the harvest's entry timing as the binding risk — is
  now published in the candidate's own vocabulary; (b) the published *sign* in crash-adjacent states
  is the opposite of the candidate's naive reading, because the paper measures the premium ex post;
  and (c) the residual left tail is documented against the harvest.**
- **Caveats:** a single-author preprint with no refereeing and author-reported magnitudes (an
  annualised Sharpe above six in a calm year; a cross-sectional information coefficient up to 0.84);
  **its premium is defined as option-implied minus *subsequently realized* volatility**, an
  ex-post convention, whereas IDEA-003's gate claim is about the *expected* premium (model-free
  implied minus the physical conditional expectation of variance) — the two conventions can move in
  opposite directions in the same state, which is precisely why the candidate's specification must
  now name its convention; the underlying datasets are the author's own assembled files, not
  independently reproduced here.
- **Verification:** read via the search index (SSRN abstract-page render); the SSRN identifier
  7046559, the date 3 June 2026 and the abstract wording come from that render. The SSRN page
  itself was not fetched directly in this cycle, so the entry is **index-rendered, not
  independently verified**.

### S-182 — U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (2022)
"Insider Trading Arrangements and Related Disclosures." **Final rule**, published at **87 FR 80362**
(29 December 2022), FR Doc 2022-27675.
URL: https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2022/12/29/2022-27675/insider-trading-arrangements-and-related-disclosures
- **Type:** Regulator final rule (**primary legal instrument**; not peer reviewed).
- **Supports (for IDEA-015):** the amendments to Rule 10b5-1 and the new **Item 408(a) of
  Regulation S-K** require issuers to disclose, **quarterly** in Form 10-Q/10-K, the adoption,
  modification and termination of trading arrangements by directors and officers and the **material
  terms**, including the date of adoption or termination, the **duration** of the plan and the
  **aggregate number of securities to be sold or purchased** under it; the final rule also imposes a
  **mandatory cooling-off period** for officer and director plans (the later of 90 days after
  adoption and two business days after the filing of the 10-Q/10-K covering the quarter of
  adoption), requires that most plan modifications restart the cooling-off period, bars the
  affirmative defence where multiple or overlapping plans are used, requires plans to be adopted and
  operated in good faith, and adds a **Form 4 checkbox** identifying a plan trade together with the
  plan's adoption date. The register's use: this is the **published, decision-time state** for
  pre-announced, price-insensitive insider supply — a dated schedule with a disclosed maximum size,
  available before the trades it describes.
- **Caveats:** a legal instrument, not evidence of any price effect; the disclosure regime has a
  compliance phase-in, so the pre-amendment record is not comparable; the item discloses the
  *maximum* aggregate amount and the plan's window, not the realised execution schedule.
- **Verification:** **Federal Register API fetched directly** — title, citation 87 FR 80362,
  publication date 2022-12-29, document number 2022-27675, type "Rule" and agency SEC all
  confirmed. The substantive content of the amendments was read from law-firm summaries of the same
  release (Paul Weiss; Freshfields) via the search index.

### S-183 — U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (2022)
"Rule 10b5-1 and Insider Trading." **Proposed rule**, published at **87 FR 8686** (15 February
2022), FR Doc 2022-01140.
URL: https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2022/02/15/2022-01140/rule-10b5-1-and-insider-trading
- **Type:** Regulator proposed rule (primary legal instrument; contains the economic analysis and the
  agency's own account of the academic literature).
- **Supports (for IDEA-015 — prior art):** the release's literature review records that a study of
  Form 144 data on insider trades under Rule 10b5-1 plans over January 2016 – May 2020 documents
  the behaviour of plan-based sales, and that work by **Hugon and Lee** on "SEC Rule 10b5-1 Plans
  and Strategic Trade around Earnings Announcements" finds abnormal returns of roughly **−2% to
  −3%** for plans that execute a sale in the window between the plan's adoption and that quarter's
  earnings announcement, **no price drop** following sales after the earnings announcement, and no
  such effect for plans with a cooling-off period longer than 60 days. For the register this is
  prior art in the family: **plan timing has published price effects**, and the published effect is
  attached to *strategic adoption around information events*, not to the mechanical execution of a
  disclosed schedule.
- **Caveats:** the release's literature summary is a regulator's characterisation of work it
  cites; the Hugon and Lee study was **not retrieved directly** in this cycle and is cited only as
  characterised in the release; the quoted passage was read from a law-firm-hosted copy of the
  release PDF via the search index, not from the Federal Register text itself.
- **Verification:** **Federal Register API fetched directly** — title, citation 87 FR 8686,
  publication date 2022-02-15, document number 2022-01140, type "Proposed Rule", agency SEC
  confirmed.

### S-184 — U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (2026)
"Renewable Fuel Standard (RFS) Program: Reallocation of Additional 2025 Small Refinery Exemptions."
**Proposed rule**, published at **91 FR 65699** (16 October 2026), FR Doc 2026-21155.
URL: https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/16/2026-21155/renewable-fuel-standard-rfs-program-reallocation-of-additional-2025-small-refinery-exemptions
- **Type:** Regulator proposed rule (primary legal instrument; not peer reviewed).
- **Supports (for IDEA-014, gaps G-15 and G-20):** (i) the **automatic extension mechanism** at
  40 CFR 80.1451(f)(1)(i)(C) is stated in the rule: if the EPA neither finalises nor withdraws the
  proposed revision to the 2026 RFS standards within 12 months of publication, the **next quarterly
  reporting deadline** moves — so the operative compliance deadline is rule-determined rather than
  discretionary, which is a stronger basis for the candidate's prediction that a moved deadline is a
  test rather than an exception; (ii) for the 2025 compliance year the EPA **projected** that small
  refinery exemptions would result in an exempted Renewable Volume Obligation of **990 million
  RINs**, with about **700 million RINs** included in the 2027 SRE reallocation volume, and states
  that the relief leaves obligated parties with obligations **770 million RINs fewer** than the EPA
  projected in the Set 2 Rule; and (iii) the rule states that **"reported 2025 exempted fuel volumes
  will not be available until after the 2025 compliance reporting deadline of October 1, 2026"** —
  i.e. the **realised** sign-determining state is published only *after* the deadline it informs,
  while the **projected** state is published before it.
- **Caveats:** a proposed rule, not final; the 990 million and 770 million RIN figures are EPA
  projections and estimates that have already been revised once against the Set 2 Rule; the quoted
  passages were read via the search index from the public-inspection PDF of the rule
  (public-inspection.federalregister.gov), not from the Federal Register text.
- **Verification:** **Federal Register API fetched directly** — title, citation 91 FR 65699,
  publication date 2026-10-16, document number 2026-21155, type "Proposed Rule", agency
  Environmental Protection Agency confirmed.

### S-185 — U.S. Environmental Protection Agency
"Public Data for the Renewable Fuel Standard" and "Available RINs" (regulator data pages).
URLs: https://www.epa.gov/fuels-registration-reporting-and-compliance-help/public-data-renewable-fuel-standard
· https://www.epa.gov/fuels-registration-reporting-and-compliance-help/available-rins
- **Type:** Regulator data publication (institutional; not peer reviewed).
- **Supports (for IDEA-014, gaps G-13/G-15/G-20):** EPA's public RFS data estate publishes, as
  **named data categories**, RINs Generated, Available RINs, RIN Trades and Price, RIN Use, Small
  Refinery Exemptions and **Renewable Volume Obligations**; monthly generation and
  volume-production files are published per month (the 2026 file list runs one file per month, with
  September 2026 already posted in October 2026, i.e. a lag of roughly one month); the **RIN
  Holdings** report summarises RINs held by balance year and balance quarter from RIN year 2010,
  split by refiner, importer, exporter and RIN originator; and the price series is a weekly
  volume-weighted average of cash transactions in EPA's own moderation system, updated monthly.
  The register's use: the **obligation side** of IDEA-014's state is a named, published EPA data
  category, and a participant-level holdings report exists — which is what makes a *projected* net
  position constructible before the deadline.
- **Caveats:** read via the search index (the EPA pages refused direct fetches in this cycle);
  the "Renewable Volume Obligations" category is *named* on the public-data page but the page does
  not state the publication lag of the obligation series, which is exactly the quantity IDEA-014
  needs; holdings by participant type are not the same thing as an obligated party's deficit, and the
  RIN Holdings report is not, on its face, a net-obligated-position series.
- **Verification:** search-index render of the two EPA pages, including the dated monthly file
  lists. **Not fetched directly.**

### S-186 — Hu, L. and Shen, Y. (2026)
"Diversified but crowded: Evidence from 13F holdings." *Finance Research Letters* 113: 110885,
published 8 October 2026.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.frl.2026.110885
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (author-reported), peer-reviewed journal article.
- **Supports (for IDEA-004, gaps G-04 and G-18):** the newest holdings-based crowding work measures
  **manager crowding from 13F holdings** for the largest portfolios over 2013Q2–2025Q4 and reports
  that "common positions, correlated institutional trading, and shared ownership have been
  associated with prices, comovement, and fragility". Two things follow for the register. First, the
  holdings-based crowding construct is an **active, published research object**, so IDEA-004's state
  is not exotic and its construction is no longer an open methodological question. Second, the
  association this newest work documents is with **prices, comovement and fragility** — *not* with a
  return-predictive trading rule — and the paper does **not** validate comomentum against the
  holdings-based measure, so G-18's bounded negative is reconfirmed against the current literature
  rather than merely left open.
- **Caveats:** the abstract was read via the search index and **OpenAlex holds no abstract for the
  record** (abstract_inverted_index is null); the sample description and the quoted sentence
  (13F holdings, the largest portfolios, 2013Q2–2025Q4, crowding associated with prices, comovement
  and fragility) are therefore **index-rendered, not independently verified**; the paper's outcome
  variables are the author's; the authors' affiliations (Columbia Computer Science; Florida State
  University Mathematics) indicate a network-science construction, so the return-prediction question
  IDEA-004 asks is not the question this paper answers — OpenAlex's own topic tagging for the record
  is *Financial Markets and Investment Strategies* with keywords on institutional investors, portfolio
  diversification and portfolio concentration, and no abstract to check against.
- **Verification:** **OpenAlex API fetched directly** (work W7220981499) — DOI, journal (*Finance
  Research Letters*), volume 113, article number 110885, publication date 2026-10-08, both authors
  with affiliations and ORCIDs, keywords, and the absence of an abstract in the record. The publisher
  page refused a direct fetch (HTTP 403), so the quoted sentence is index-rendered.

### S-187 — EdgarHawk (2026)
"Insider Transactions — Real-Time SEC Form 4 Tracker" (commercial data aggregator).
URL: https://edgarhawk.com/insider-transactions
- **Type:** Commercial data aggregator (**secondary, vendor-produced, unaudited**).
- **Supports (for IDEA-015, magnitude context only):** the aggregator reports, for 16 October 2026,
  389 Form 4 filings, of which 78 of 101 dispositions (≈77%) were flagged as Rule 10b5-1 plan
  trades — i.e. in the modern record the large majority of insider *sales* are non-discretionary
  plan sales, which is the population the candidate's mechanism requires. Individual filings on the
  same page show Form 4 footnotes reporting plan adoption dates and, in at least one case, a
  **remaining share count under the plan** ("1,792 remaining shares available to be sold and
  terminates automatically on July 3, 2026").
- **Caveats:** vendor statistics with no published methodology and no audit; the sample is a single
  day; the Form 4 examples were read via the search index and the SEC's own document server refused
  direct fetches in this cycle, so the "remaining shares" footnote is **index-rendered and not
  independently verified**; nothing here is evidence of a price effect.
- **Verification:** search-index render only.
