## Cycle 32 sources (2026-10-20)

Cycle 32's sources fall into four blocks: (i) the two entries minted in Cycle 31 but never written
into this ledger (S-188, S-189 — the ledger repair); (ii) the expected-premium corpus that closes
**G-19** for IDEA-003 (S-190 … S-193); (iii) the dealer-balance-sheet and ETF-arbitrage corpus that
closes **G-16** for IDEA-010 (S-194 … S-200); (iv) the EPA rule corpus that closes **G-20** for
IDEA-014 (S-201 … S-203); and (v) the corporate-debt tender-offer corpus for this cycle's new-venue
family screen (S-204 … S-209).

### S-188 — Forbes, W.P. and Giannopoulos, G. (2015) — *repaired Cycle-31 entry*
"Post-Earnings Announcement Drift in Greece." *Review of Pacific Basin Financial Markets and Policies*
18(3): 1550019.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1142/S0219091515500198
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for IDEA-011, as a partial prior-art hit):** in the Greek market, 2000–2006 earnings
  announcements (announcement years 2001–2007), "PEAD [is] alive and well, and of growing importance
  in our Greek sample", and the authors attribute it to IFRS adoption: "It may be the adoption of
  international financial reporting standards (IFRS) has served to reduce earnings predictability in
  Greece and thus enhance PEAD in the Athens stock exchange (ASE) market." This is a published case in
  which **IFRS adoption *increases* rather than shrinks** post-adoption drift — directionally the
  candidate's effect, and the opposite of the candidate's stated hypothesis.
- **Caveats:** the result is market-specific (Greece: a small market with a code-law accounting
  regime, thin pre-IFRS disclosure and high individual-investor participation) and the mechanism the
  paper offers is a *reduction in the intensity of accounting conservatism and increased earnings
  volatility* under fair-value accounting — a disclosure-environment shock, not necessarily the
  candidate's comparability clock. It is a single-market study; it does not test a trading rule.
- **Verification:** OpenAlex API fetched directly (DOI, journal, volume, issue, article number, both
  authors, publication year 2015); the abstract and the quoted sentence were read via the search
  index rendering of the publisher page (10.1142/S0219091515500198); a longer author-revision
  discussion of the mechanism was read via the search-index rendering of the authors' ResearchGate
  copy of the revised manuscript.

### S-189 — Brochet, F., Jagolinzer, A.D. and Riedl, E.J. (2012) — *repaired Cycle-31 entry*
"Mandatory IFRS Adoption and Financial Statement Comparability." *Contemporary Accounting Research*
30(4): 1373–1400.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/1911-3846.12002 · working-paper version: Harvard Business School
Working Paper 11-109.
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for IDEA-011, as a partial prior-art hit):** UK firms, whose domestic standards were
  already close to IFRS, are used to isolate the *comparability* channel from information quality.
  "Abnormal returns to insider purchases — used to proxy for private information — are reduced
  following IFRS adoption"; the working-paper version quantifies the insider-purchase abnormal return
  decline at **4.2%** in six-month windows and the analyst-recommendation-upgrade decline at **2.9%**,
  robust across sub-samples isolating the comparability effect. **Mandatory IFRS adoption reduces
  insiders' ability to exploit private information** — consistent with the candidate's own
  comparability channel.
- **Caveats:** the *direction* of the drift implied by reduced private-information advantage is the
  opposite of the candidate's hypothesis (a better public information set should *reduce* drift, and
  this paper is evidence for that); it is a UK-firm study, and it measures information asymmetry
  rather than post-adoption drift. It is a mechanism-consistency citation, not a replication of the
  candidate's claim.
- **Verification:** OpenAlex API fetched directly (DOI, journal, volume, issue, pages, three authors,
  2012); the abstract and the quantified 4.2%/2.9% figures were read via the search-index rendering
  of the HBS working-paper PDF (hbs.edu/ris/Publication Files/11-109).

### S-190 — Rombouts, J.V.K., Stentoft, L. and Violante, F. (2020)
"Dynamics of variance risk premia: A new model for disentangling the price of risk." *Journal of
Econometrics* 217(2): 312–334.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jeconom.2019.12.006 (first published online December 2019; issue
dated 2020)
- **Type:** Primary theory plus estimation (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for G-19, decisively, and in two directions):**
  (i) *The expected-premium convention is stated explicitly.* The variance risk premium is defined as
  "the difference between the risk neutral and physical expectations of an asset's total return
  variation", i.e. Π_t = E^Q[Σ_{t,t+1}] − E^P[Σ_{t,t+1}] — exactly the register's IDEA-003
  convention.
  (ii) *The sign in distress states has been tested under that convention.* The paper's central
  methodological finding is that the "vast majority of the literature estimates the VRP period-by-period
  using ex-post realisations ignoring its latent nature and temporal dependence", and that "such
  practice leads to a VRP that is too volatile and **systematically becomes negative, rather than
  largely positive, in periods of market distress**." The paper's own latent decomposition then
  reports that the continuous component of the VRP "is characterised by level and variance shifts,
  strong persistence, **is systematically positive and is higher in periods of market turmoil**".
  (iii) The paper does not impose sign constraints on the jump component.
- **Caveats:** the latent premium is a *model-based* conditional expectation (realized-volatility
  processes plus regime switching), not a raw filtered conditional expectation; the estimate is
  conditional on the model; and the paper prices the *equity* variance risk premium, not a
  single-name or option-surface-implied quantity. It is a decomposition of a published premium, not
  a test of a gated trading rule.
- **Verification:** Crossref REST API fetched directly (DOI, title, three authors, journal, volume,
  issue, pages, publication date 2020-02) and OpenAlex API fetched directly (work record, journal
  *Journal of Econometrics* 217(2), pp. 312–334, online-first year 2019); the abstract and the quoted
  passages were read via the search-index rendering of the publisher and SSRN records.

### S-191 — Zhou, J. (2018)
"Variance Risk Premia, Asset Predictability Puzzles, and Macroeconomic Uncertainty." *Annual Review of
Financial Economics* 10: 481–497.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1146/annurev-financial-110217-022737 · earlier working-paper version: Federal
Reserve Bank of St. Louis FEDS 2010-014, "Variance Risk Premia, Asset Predictability Puzzles, and
Macroeconomic Uncertainty."
URL (working paper): https://www.stlouisfed.org/research/publications/wp/2010-014
- **Type:** Review article (peer-reviewed), with a verified working-paper version.
- **Supports (for G-19):** the expected-premium convention is defined in the review's own notation:
  "The variance risk premium is defined as the difference between the ex-ante risk neutral expectation
  of the future return variance and the objective or statistical expectation of the return variance
  over the [t, t+1] time interval, VRP_t ≡ E^Q_t(Var_{t,t+1}) − E^P_t(Var_{t,t+1})", and the review
  states the *measurement* problem explicitly: the risk-neutral expectation "is typically replaced by
  the CBOE implied variance or VIX²" and the physical expectation is replaced either by a discretised
  realisation or by a filtered/model-based conditional expectation. The review is the standard
  reference for the distinction between the two conventions that G-19 asked about.
- **Caveats:** a review, not a new empirical test of the sign in crash-imminent states; the review's
  empirical content is on the *predictability* of asset returns from the premium, not on a
  state-conditional sign. It does not test an option-surface-implied crash state.
- **Verification:** Crossref REST API fetched directly (DOI, title, author, journal, volume, pages,
  2018); the definition and the measurement-problem passage were read via the search-index rendering
  of the Federal Reserve working-paper PDF (federalreserve.gov FEDS 2010-014). The Annual Reviews
  publisher page returned HTTP 403 to a direct fetch, so the review's own text is index-rendered and
  the definition is quoted from the verified working-paper version.

### S-192 — Bekaert, G., Engstrom, B. and Ermolov, M. (2023)
"Variance Risk Premium in Equilibrium Models." *The Review of Finance* 27(6): 1977–2014.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/rof/rfad005 · working-paper version: NBER Working Paper No. 27108
(October 2020), DOI https://doi.org/10.3386/w27108
- **Type:** Primary theory (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for G-19, and the single most direct statement on the sign of the *expected* premium in
  bad states):** "The equity variance risk premium is the expected compensation earned for selling
  variance risk in equity markets. **The variance risk premium is positive and shows only moderate
  persistence. High variance risk premiums coincide with the left tail of the consumption growth
  distribution shifting down.**" The expected premium is therefore (a) positive and (b) *larger* in
  states where the consumption left tail is worse — i.e. in bad, crash-prone states the expected
  premium widens rather than collapses.
- **Caveats:** an equilibrium model of the *equity* variance risk premium; the "bad state" is a
  consumption-tail state, not an option-surface-implied imminent-crash state, so the identification is
  at the level of state co-movement rather than at the level of the candidate's specific gate. It
  makes no claim about a gated trading rule.
- **Verification:** Crossref REST API fetched directly (DOI, title, three authors, journal
  *Review of Finance*, volume 27, issue 6, pages 1977–2014, 2023) with the abstract retrieved in the
  same record; the NBER working-paper identification was confirmed in the search index.

### S-193 — Gruber, M., Tebaldi, E. and Trojani, A. (2021)
"The Price of the Smile and Variance Risk Premia." *Management Science* 67(7): 4056–4074.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1287/mnsc.2020.3689
- **Type:** Primary theory plus estimation (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for G-19, as the closest published sign-flip in a variance-premium quantity in bad
  states):** the published abstract states that "the more persistent volatility factor and the
  skewness factor support a downward sloping term structure of variance risk premia in normal times,
  whereas **the most transient volatility factor accounts for an upward sloping term structure in
  periods of distress**". The earlier working-paper version of the same paper states that the estimated
  variance risk premia are "highly time-varying and unambiguously negative" on the Carr–Wu sign
  convention (i.e. unambiguously *positive* compensation) across the sample, while "the variance term
  premium may change sign".
- **Caveats:** the sign-flip is in the **term** premium (the slope of the variance-premium term
  structure), not in the *level* of the expected premium; the working-paper text is index-rendered
  rather than read from a publisher PDF in this cycle. It is the closest published analogue of a
  "variance-premium quantity changes sign in distress", and it is recorded here so that the register
  does not overstate the novelty of that observation.
- **Verification:** Crossref REST API fetched directly (DOI, title, three authors, journal
  *Management Science*, volume 67, issue 7, pages 4056–4074, 2021-04-01) with the published abstract
  retrieved in the same record; the working-paper variant of the abstract was read via the search-index
  rendering of the author-hosted PDF (unibocconi).

### S-194 — Raddatz, C.E. (2025)
"Authorized participants' regulatory constraints and limits to ETF arbitrage during market turmoil:
Evidence from the dash-for-cash episode." *Journal of Banking & Finance* 179: 107499.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jbankfin.2025.107499
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for G-16, and it is the decisive citation for that gap):** the paper builds a dataset
  linking each US corporate-bond ETF to its authorized participants "using U.S. SEC regulatory
  filings", connects the APs to their financial statements, and measures **APs' regulatory capital
  ratios** — a *disclosed, filing-based* state. The finding is that APs' regulatory capital ratios
  conditioned the ETF arbitrage relationship during the March 2020 dash-for-cash: ETFs linked to APs
  at the 25th percentile of regulatory capital ratios saw a **0.69 percentage-point decline in
  arbitrage intensity**, against **0.39 percentage points** for ETFs linked to 75th-percentile APs;
  and "other AP characteristics, such as equity-to-assets ratios, cash-to-assets ratios, total assets,
  or AP activity concentration, do not play a significant role."
- **Caveats:** the outcome variables are the *elasticity of ETF arbitrage intensity* and ETF premia,
  not the level of the ETF–NAV discount and not a trading return; the episode is a single
  market-stress window; the AP capital ratios are quarterly-filed and therefore lagged, and the
  ETF↔AP mapping requires a bespoke construction from filings. It identifies the state; it does not
  test the candidate's two-legged rule.
- **Verification:** OpenAlex API fetched directly (DOI, title, author, journal *Journal of Banking &
  Finance* 179, article 107499, 2025) and the abstract and quoted figures were read via the
  search-index rendering of the publisher's ScienceDirect record.

### S-195 — Pan, K. and Zeng, Y. (2017; working-paper versions to 2019) — *corrects the register's citation*
"ETF Arbitrage under Liquidity Mismatch." ESRB Working Paper No. 59 (December 2017); NBER/LTAP
conference version (June 2017); SSRN working-paper version (SSRN 2895478, posted 8 January 2017, last
revised 30 March 2019).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.2895478 · PDF: https://www.esrb.europa.eu/pub/pdf/wp/esrb.wp59.en.pdf
- **Type:** Primary theory plus empirical result (**working paper, not peer-reviewed**).
- **Supports (for IDEA-010, and it is the source of the register's own "Pan & Zeng 2019" reference):**
  the paper formalises the AP dual-role conflict — "APs are also bond dealers and they use their own
  balance sheet in both roles" — and shows that "bond market illiquidity limits ETF arbitrage" and that
  "large bond flow shocks to AP balance sheets also limit ETF arbitrage, leading to persistent relative
  mispricings". Empirically: "An increase of 1% in the ETF premium generates an increase in AP
  arbitrage by 50 basis points; however, as market volatility rises, holding fixed the ETF premium, AP
  arbitrage declines"; the sensitivity decline "is asymmetrically larger when the ETF premium is
  negative"; and when APs hold extreme bond inventory imbalances, creations and redemptions can move
  "in the opposite direction than what would be implied by the initial relative mispricing".
- **Caveats (material, and they bear on G-16):** the paper's datasets are **proprietary** — "novel
  AP-level data", a "proprietary version of TRACE" with FINRA dealer identifiers, "proprietary
  time-series data on the daily creation and redemption baskets" for each ETF, and proprietary AP lists
  from two ETF sponsors covering "on average 83% of the total passive corporate bond ETF assets under
  management". This is the empirical provenance of the register's belief that "AP-level data is
  commercial"; the *published evidence* is proprietary-based even though the *state construct* is
  publicly reconstructable (S-194). The paper is a working paper, and the register's Cycle-6 citation
  "Pan & Zeng 2019" should be read as this SSRN revision date.
- **Verification:** search-index render of the ESRB working-paper PDF and the SSRN record (both
  authors, title, abstract, keywords, dates, DOI) and of the authors' hosted PDFs (Harvard scholar
  repository, NBER conference repository); the substantive quotations above were read from those
  renderings. No publisher version was located, so the entry is recorded as a working paper.

### S-196 — Shim, J. and Todorov, K. (2022)
"ETFs, illiquid assets, and fire sales." BIS Working Papers No. 975 (June 2022).
URL: https://www.bis.org/publ/work975.htm · preprint version: SSRN 3886881,
DOI https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.3886881
- **Type:** Primary theory (institutional working paper).
- **Supports (for IDEA-010, as a published rival explanation for the ETF–NAV discount):** the model has
  "an ETF discount arises because the AP acts as a buffer between the ETF and the bond market, allowing
  the ETF price to fall while avoiding selling bonds in quantities that would trigger a fire sale",
  and it documents that "bond ETF baskets contain a small fraction of holdings ('fractional baskets'),
  which contributes to persistent discrepancies between ETF price and NAV". A discount can therefore
  arise from *basket composition*, with no AP balance-sheet constraint involved.
- **Caveats:** a model paper; the fractional-basket mechanism is not shown to dominate the AP-capacity
  mechanism empirically in this entry; the BIS landing page's body text was not readable in this cycle
  (the page returned navigation chrome only on a direct fetch), so the quoted passages are
  index-rendered from the search index.
- **Verification:** OpenAlex API fetched directly for the SSRN record (title, two authors, DOI
  10.2139/ssrn.3886881, 2021) and the search-index rendering of the BIS working-paper landing page
  and PDF; the BIS working-paper number and URL are as published on bis.org.

### S-197 — Siriwardane, E.N., Sunderam, A. and Wallen, J. (2025)
"Segmented Arbitrage." *The Journal of Finance* 80(5): 2543–2590.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1111/jofi.13469 · working-paper version: NBER Working Paper No. 30561
- **Type:** Primary theory plus empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for IDEA-010, as a published rival explanation):** the paper documents that
  **arbitrage spreads are sensitive to idiosyncratic balance-sheet shocks** and that
  "balance sheets are segmented — intermediaries specialize in certain trades", so the *funding source*
  rather than the AP's bond-inventory capacity is a first-order determinant of an arbitrage spread.
  For IDEA-010 this is a rival explanation for any ETF–NAV gap conditioned on intermediary
  constraints, and it supplies the register's best published template for what a *funding-segmented*
  arbitrage spread looks like.
- **Caveats:** the paper studies arbitrage spreads in secured/segmented funding settings, not bond-ETF
  arbitrage directly; its proxies for funding conditions and its identification are specific to its own
  setting and are not a substitute for a decision-time dealer-stress state for IDEA-010.
- **Verification:** OpenAlex API fetched directly (DOI, title, three authors, journal *Journal of
  Finance* 80(5), pp. 2543–2590, 2025) and the NBER working-paper DOI (10.3386/w30561) confirmed in
  the same query; the abstract was read via the search-index rendering of the publisher and NBER records.

### S-198 — Hempel, K., Kim, J. and Wermers, M. (2022)
"Financial Intermediary Funding Constraints and Segmented Markets: Evidence from SMCCF ETF Purchases."
Office of Financial Research Working Paper 22-01 (February 2022).
URL: https://www.financialresearch.gov/working-papers/22-01
- **Type:** Primary empirical result (institutional working paper).
- **Supports (for G-16, and it is the register's best *public* candidate for the missing AP/dealer
  balance-sheet state):** the authors state that "while we do not have information on AP-level
  inventories, we can indirectly infer their corporate bond inventories from **public FR 2004
  disclosures on aggregate primary dealer activities**", and they use that inference to measure
  positive AP balance-sheet shocks (funding for the Federal Reserve's SMCCF bond-ETF purchases) and
  their spillovers to ETF prices and to the underlying bonds.
- **Caveats:** the FR 2004 inventory series is an *aggregate* measure across primary dealers, not an
  AP-level or ETF-level measure; the inference is indirect and the paper's own events (SMCCF
  purchases) are policy-specific, so the series is a candidate state, not a validated decision-time
  variable for IDEA-010's daily rule.
- **Verification:** search-index render of the OFR working-paper landing page and PDF (title, three
  authors, report number 22-01, February 2022, and the quoted FR 2004 passage); the OFR PDF itself was
  read through the search index in this cycle.

### S-199 — Comerton-Forde, M., Ford, J., Foucault, P. and Jurkatis, T. (2025)
"Investors as a liquidity backstop in corporate bond markets." Bank of England Staff Working Paper
No. 1126 (April 2025).
URL: https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/working-paper/2025/investors-as-a-liquidity-backstop-in-corporate-bond-markets
- **Type:** Primary empirical result (institutional working paper).
- **Supports (for IDEA-010, as a published baseline for the "concession paid to liquidity providers"
  structure):** the paper documents that dealers pay "relationship discounts" to investors for past
  liquidity provision — roughly two thirds of the observed dealer discount is attributed to liquidity
  services — and that dealers' balance-sheet constraints shape corporate-bond liquidity. This is
  published evidence that a *dealer balance-sheet constraint expressed as a concession to the
  counterparty who supplies liquidity* is a documented market structure, which is the register's
  closest published baseline for the type of edge IDEA-010 claims.
- **Caveats:** the concessions documented are to *client liquidity providers* in the dealer sector, not
  to the ETF arbitrage channel; the paper is a staff working paper, and the discount decomposition is
  estimated at the dealer-client relationship level.
- **Verification:** search-index render of the Bank of England working-paper landing page (report
  number 1126, April 2025, four authors, and the quoted relationship-discount finding).

### S-200 — Eisfeldt, A.L., Herskovic, B. and Liu, S. (2024)
"Interdealer Price Dispersion and Intermediary Capacity." NBER Working Paper No. 32998 (September 2024).
DOI: https://doi.org/10.3386/w32998
- **Type:** Primary empirical result (working paper; the register's "Eisfeldt et al. 2024").
- **Supports (for G-16, with two constraints on IDEA-010's state):**
  (i) the paper's own proxy for intermediation capacity is **interdealer price dispersion** from TRACE,
  and it is explicitly a *proxy*: dispersion measures "the negative effects of interdealer frictions and
  risk misallocation within the dealer sector". It is validated against model-based **fair-value
  spreads** and a "fair-value basis", not against any disclosed dealer funding value adjustment.
  (ii) two facts bear directly on IDEA-010's feasibility: "given the infrequent trading of bonds,
  calculating interdealer price dispersion daily is not feasible. For our baseline results, we measure
  bond-level interdealer price dispersion using the standard deviation in all interdealer trades
  occurring each **month**" — so the published dispersion measure is **monthly**, not daily; and the paper
  finds that "equity-weighted averages of dealer-level financial soundness **fail** to capture effects
  from interdealer frictions", i.e. aggregate dealer-balance-sheet averages are documented to be
  *inferior* proxies for intermediation capacity than the dispersion measure.
- **Caveats:** a working paper; the fair-value validation depends on a structural model and on equity
  data; the monthly frequency of the published measure is the binding constraint for a rule with a
  1–10 session holding horizon. It is *not* a validation of any proxy against disclosed dealer FVAs,
  which remains an unperformed exercise (and, per S-155, is not a sensible target anyway).
- **Verification:** OpenAlex API fetched directly (DOI, title, three authors, NBER WP 32998, 2024);
  the quoted passages were read via the search-index rendering of the NBER working-paper record.

### S-201 — U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (2026)
"Renewable Fuel Standard Program: Reallocation of Volumes and Other Modifications" (the "Set 2 Rule").
91 FR 16388 (April 1, 2026); Federal Register document 2026-06275.
URL: https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/04/01/2026-06275/renewable-fuel-standard-program-reallocation-of-volumes-and-other-modifications
- **Type:** Primary regulator material (final rule).
- **Supports (for G-20):**
  (i) *The volume requirement is calibrated to the agency's own supply projection.* The rule's statutory
  factor analysis uses the "Analyzed Volumes" (the agency's projected supply) and states that "the
  total quantity of renewable fuel we project will be supplied to the U.S. to meet these volume
  requirements (shown in Table I.A.1-2) are very similar to the quantities we projected would be supplied
  to meet the proposed volume requirements" — i.e. the RVO is set so that the *projected* supply meets
  it, so the *projected* net obligated position is near zero by construction.
  (ii) *Where the projection falls short, the shortfall is institutionally absorbed, not expressed as a
  binding market obligation.* For 2025 cellulosic: "our estimate of the number of 2025 cellulosic RINs
  that will be produced is 1.21 billion gallons … equivalent to 1.21 billion RINs. This projected volume
  is 0.17 billion fewer RINs than the 1.38 billion RINs needed to comply with the original 2025
  cellulosic biofuel standard, a shortfall of approximately 13 percent. We therefore find that the
  shortfall … triggers the need for implementation of the cellulosic waiver authority for 2025."
  (iii) The rule's own projection of the **2025 exempted** fuel volume is **7.55 billion gallons**,
  derived by "projecting … a three-year average of the actual exempted fuel volumes from 2022 through
  2024" — a lagged average, not a forecast of the current year's exemption decisions.
  (iv) The 2026 and 2027 standards include small refinery exemption (SRE) reallocation volumes of
  **0.99 billion RINs (2026)** and **1.04 billion RINs (2027)**.
- **Caveats:** the rule's tables are published as images in the Federal Register text version, so the
  billion-RIN volumes in Table I.A.1-1 were read from the agency's own summary of the rule (S-203)
  rather than from the table images; the quotations above are from the raw text version, which was
  fetched directly from the Federal Register's document server.
- **Verification:** the raw text of the rule was fetched directly from the Federal Register document
  server (`/documents/full_text/text/2026/04/01/2026-06275.txt`), and the document metadata
  (title, citation, publication date, raw-text URL) was verified through the Federal Register API.

40 CFR 80.1405, "Renewable fuel production and renewable fuel credits" (annual renewable fuel

"Final Renewable Fuel Standards for 2026 and 2027." Agency rule-summary page.

- **Type:** Primary regulator material (agency summary of the final standards).

### S-204 — Mann, S.V. and Powers, E.A. (2006)
"Determinants of bond tender premiums and the percentage tendered." *Journal of Banking & Finance*
31(3): 547–566.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jbankfin.2005.12.008 · earlier working-paper version: SSRN 475561
(7 December 2003), DOI https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.475561
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for this cycle's new-venue family screen):** the price-insensitive flow at a published
  debt-tender deadline is a **published, quantified** object: "For the average (median) tender offer,
  the tender price is 4.75 (3.40) percent greater than the pre-tender market price while the
  percentage of bonds tendered is 82.3 (94.6) percent"; "a one percent increase in tender premium
  increases the tendering rate by as much as 10%"; and tenders are easier to complete when bond
  ownership is concentrated.
- **Caveats:** the paper studies the *determinants* of the tender premium and the tendering rate, not
  the price path of the bond into and around the tender deadline; it is an announcement-and-outcome
  study of corporate bond tenders, not a deadline-cluster study.
- **Verification:** OpenAlex API fetched directly (published record: title, two authors, journal
  *Journal of Banking & Finance* 31(3), pp. 547–566, 2006, DOI) and the working-paper abstract and
  highlights read via the search-index rendering of the SSRN record (SSRN 475561).

### S-205 — Levy, H. and Shalev, R. (2016)
"Bond repurchase objectives and the repurchase method choice." *Journal of Accounting and Economics*
63(2–3): 385–403.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jacceco.2016.09.003
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for the family screen):** documents the structural distinction the screen relies on —
  "Unlike tender offers, open market repurchases are not … [at a price known to the seller]" — i.e. the
  tender is the price-insensitive, dated, published-deadline repurchase route, and open-market
  repurchase is not.
- **Caveats:** the paper's subject is the issuer's choice of repurchase method, not the bond's
  price behaviour around a tender deadline.
- **Verification:** OpenAlex API fetched directly (DOI, title, two authors, journal *Journal of
  Accounting and Economics* 63(2–3), pp. 385–403, 2016); the abstract read via the search-index
  rendering of the publisher record.

### S-206 — Daniels, K.N., Díaz, F. and Ramírez, G. (2012)
"An empirical investigation of corporate bond clawbacks (IPOCs): Debt renegotiation and the exercise of
clawback provisions." *Journal of Corporate Finance* 20: 14–21.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jcorpfin.2012.11.001 · related working paper: "The Exercise of
Corporate Bond Clawbacks and Debt Renegotiation: An Empirical Analysis", Kennesaw State University
working paper (20(1), 2013).
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports (for the family screen):** documents the *deadline-cluster* behaviour directly: "72% of
  exercised clawback issues underwent renegotiation in the form of a tender offer", "60.2% of the
  non-exercised clawback issues and 73.9% of the exercised clawback issues went into renegotiation
  before the expiration of the clawback provision", and "the relationship between clawback being
  exercised and the bond issue being subject to renegotiation *before* the IPOC expiration date
  further illustrates its strategic use". A dated, published, price-insensitive flow into a published
  deadline is therefore a **documented** institutional pattern.
- **Caveats:** the paper documents the *flow timing* (renegotiations clustered before a published
  deadline), not the *price path* of the affected bonds into and out of the deadline; the setting is
  investment-grade clawback provisions with mandatory exercise dates, not ordinary liability-management
  exercises.
- **Verification:** OpenAlex API fetched directly (published record: title, three authors, journal
  *Journal of Corporate Finance* 20, pp. 14–21, 2012, DOI) and the working-paper full text read via
  the search-index rendering of the Kennesaw State University repository PDF.

## Cycle 32 gap verdicts (2026-10-20)

- **G-19 — CLOSED, AGAINST the candidate's sign claim (IDEA-003).** G-19 asked: "does any published
  work state the *expected*-premium convention explicitly and test its sign in crash-imminent states?"
  The answer is **yes, and the published sign is positive**. The expected-premium convention is stated
  explicitly and in its own notation by at least three independent peer-reviewed sources: the
  risk-neutral-minus-physical definition in Zhou's Annual Review (S-191), the same convention with an
  explicit latent/physical decomposition in Rombouts, Stentoft & Violante (S-190), and the equilibrium
  treatment in Bekaert, Engstrom & Ermolov (S-192). Its sign in bad states has been tested:
  Rombouts et al. report that the *naive ex-post* estimator "systematically becomes negative, rather
  than largely positive, in periods of market distress", and their latent decomposition reports that the
  continuous component of the expected premium "is systematically positive and is higher in periods of
  market turmoil"; Bekaert et al. report that the expected premium is "positive" and that "high variance
  risk premiums coincide with the left tail of the consumption growth distribution shifting down". The
  closest published sign-flip in a variance-premium quantity in distress is in the **term** premium, not
  the level (S-193). **Consequence:** the candidate's load-bearing claim — that in a crash-imminent
  state the *expected* premium collapses and short-volatility is uncompensated there — is **not
  supported** under the candidate's own convention, and the published evidence points the other way
  (the expected premium widens in bad states). What survives is a **tail/distribution** claim, not a sign
  claim: the *realised* harvest is catastrophically left-tailed in crash states. IDEA-003 is retained,
  re-scored **71.0 → 66.0**, its G-12 pass is downgraded to "the conditioning changes neither the sign
  nor the existence of the premium's compensation", and a hard-stop flag is attached: the candidate
  must not be presented as a new premium edge. **G-22 opened** for the residual.
- **G-16 — CLOSED IN FAVOUR, ON A REFORMULATED VALIDATION TARGET (IDEA-010).** G-16 asked whether the
  register's public dealer-stress proxy set could be validated against *disclosed dealer funding value
  adjustments*. Two findings close the gap in a different and better way than the register framed it.
  First, **the register's original validation target was the wrong target**: disclosed dealer FVAs are
  OTC-derivatives-specific, annual/quarterly, often immaterial at the firms that disclose them
  (S-155 already records that no continuous public FVA series exists), so a daily public proxy cannot
  meaningfully be validated against them. Second, **independent published work has already performed the
  validation the register wanted, on the right target and the right link**: Raddatz (S-194) shows that
  **APs' regulatory capital ratios, disclosed in SEC regulatory filings**, are the state that governs
  ETF arbitrage intensity and ETF premia, that the effect is material (0.69pp vs 0.39pp decline in
  arbitrage intensity between 25th- and 75th-percentile APs during the dash-for-cash) and that
  *other* AP balance-sheet characteristics (equity/assets, cash/assets, total assets, concentration) do
  **not** matter. The published mechanism is Pan & Zeng (S-195) — with the material caveat that its
  empirical datasets are proprietary — and the published rivals are now threefold: fractional baskets
  (S-196), funding segmentation (S-197) and client-sourced liquidity discounts (S-199). Two published
  facts constrain the *rule* even though they do not block the state: the register's own dispersion
  proxy is computed **monthly** because bonds do not trade daily (S-200), and aggregate dealer
  financial-soundness averages are documented to **fail** to capture intermediation capacity (S-200), so
  the FR Y-9C-style aggregate leverage proxy is published but *invalid* for this purpose; a public
  aggregate dealer-inventory proxy does exist (FR 2004 primary dealer activities, used by S-198).
  **Consequence:** IDEA-010's blocking precondition is **met**; no validation specification was blocked
  on it; the candidate is re-scored **65.0 → 68.0** (novelty 2 → 1 because the state-to-premium relation
  is now published; evidence 3 → 4; data 3 → 4 because the operative state is public in SEC filings),
  and **V-010** is written. **G-23 opened** for the residual (cadence and the rival explanations).
- **G-20 — CLOSED AGAINST THE STATE AS SPECIFIED (IDEA-014).** G-20 asked whether the projection error
  swamps the sign-determining state. It does, decisively, and the register can now quantify it:
  (i) the **only** component of the pre-deadline state with a published ex-post measurement is the
  exempted RVO, and its 2025 projection error was **+770 million RINs** (projected 990 million RINs in
  S-201 vs actual 1,760 million RINs, EPA's 31 August 2026 SRE decisions) — **+78%** relative error, on a
  state whose projection is itself a **three-year lagged average** of past exempted volumes (S-201), so
  the error is structurally one-sided and cannot anticipate a wave of exemption decisions;
  (ii) the projected net obligated position is **near zero by construction**, because the RVO is
  calibrated to the agency's own supply projection (S-201), and where the projection does fall short the
  shortfall is **institutionally absorbed** — the 2025 cellulosic case was a 0.17 billion RIN (13%)
  projected shortfall that was handled by *waiving* the volume requirement (S-201) — not expressed as a
  binding market obligation; (iii) the scale of the quantities is known: total applicable volumes of
  **26.81** and **27.02** billion RINs for 2026 and 2027, against SRE reallocation volumes of 0.99 and
  1.04 billion RINs (S-203) — so the measured 770-million-RIN projection error is **78% of the entire
  annual SRE reallocation volume** that the agency itself treats as material enough to revise the
  standards; and (iv) the *realised* state is published only after the deadline (S-184). **Consequence:**
  the candidate's proposed sign-determining state is either unavailable before the decision or
  uninformative by construction; the candidate is re-scored **60.0 → 55.0** and takes a **hard-stop flag
  on the state as specified**. The candidate's *existence* claim is untouched and remains testable.
  **G-24 opened** for the replacement-state question.
- **G-21 — OPEN, unchanged (IDEA-004).** The crowding-proxy validation gap is not addressed by any Cycle
  32 source: S-200 and S-187 bear on crowding constructs but neither validates a return-correlation
  measure against a holdings-based measure. IDEA-004's rank is unchanged at 69.0 and V-002 stays frozen.
- **G-22 (new) — IDEA-003 residual.** With G-19 closed against the sign limb, IDEA-003's remaining
  content is the *state construction*: does any published work establish that an **option-surface-implied
  imminent-crash measure** adds risk-adjusted value to a variance harvest **beyond** the published
  state-conditioned volatility-trading family (S-180)? If not, the candidate is a known baseline and
  must be parked.
- **G-23 (new) — IDEA-010 residual.** With G-16 closed, what remains open is (i) whether the
  filing-disclosed AP regulatory capital ratio is usable at the cadence IDEA-010's rule requires
  (quarterly filings with a filing lag against a 1–10 session holding horizon) and (ii) whether it is a
  **signed** state rather than only a moderator, given the three published rival explanations (S-196,
  S-197, S-199) and the monthly frequency of the published dispersion proxy (S-200).
- **G-24 (new) — IDEA-014 replacement state.** Is there a published, **pre-deadline**, **signed** state
  for the RIN deadline cluster that is **not** the outcome itself — for example the carryover-RIN
  balance, or the pre-deadline RIN price and positioning (S-185) — such that the candidate's test can
  be run without look-ahead and without circularity?

## Cycle 35 sources (2026-10-20)

All eleven entries below were located and metadata-checked in Cycle 35. Each entry records how the
document was verified, and the register's rule is applied strictly: **"fetched directly"** means the
document or its bibliographic record was retrieved and parsed in this session; **"index-rendered"**
means it was read only through a search-engine index render and must be re-verified before any
implementation relies on it.

### S-220 — Daske, S., Hail, L., Leuz, C. and Verdi, R. (2008), "Mandatory IFRS Reporting around the World: Early Evidence on the Economic Consequences", *Journal of Accounting Research* 46(5), 1085–1142. DOI: 10.1111/j.1475-679X.2008.00306.x.
- **Type and relevance:** peer-reviewed journal article; 26 countries, mandatory IFRS adopters; market liquidity, cost of equity and Tobin's q. Two findings bear directly on IDEA-011. First, the **mandatory-versus-voluntary adopter contrast** is the register's first published *second-time-adopter* comparator: "the capital-market effects are most pronounced for firms that voluntarily switch to IFRS, both in the year when they switch and again later, when IFRS become mandatory". Second, the paper warns that the effects "occur prior to the official adoption date", and it reports that the estimated liquidity improvements are smaller in magnitude on a monthly than on a daily sampling frequency.
- **Verification:** publisher landing page for DOI 10.1111/j.1475-679X.2008.00306.x **fetched directly**; title, journal, volume, issue, pages and the publisher abstract read from that page; cross-checked against the OpenAlex record (W2050886799) and the SSRN working-paper record (1024240).
- **Caveats:** the outcomes are liquidity, cost of capital and valuation — **not** post-earnings-announcement drift. The voluntary-adopter effect is self-selected (the authors' own caveat: "likely due to self-selection"), so it is **not** a clean first-versus-second-adopter test. The anticipation finding means the candidate's diffusion clock must be defined to start before the effective date, not at it.
- **Supports:** IDEA-011's mechanism (an accounting-transition shock absorbed gradually, with a mandatory-versus-voluntary contrast available as the discriminator) and the anticipation caveat on the candidate's clock definition. Bears on G-21.

### S-221 — Christensen, H. B., Hail, L. and Leuz, C. (2013), "Mandatory IFRS reporting and changes in enforcement", *Journal of Accounting and Economics* 56(2–3, Supplement 1), 147–177. DOI: 10.1016/j.jacceco.2013.10.007.
- **Type and relevance:** peer-reviewed journal article; decomposes the EU liquidity benefits of mandatory IFRS adoption into a standard-switch component and an enforcement-change component. Finds that the benefits "are limited to five EU countries that made substantive changes in their enforcement of financial reporting around the same time IFRS became mandatory", and that firms already reporting voluntarily under IFRS show a liquidity benefit at the mandate date only in EU countries with concurrent enforcement changes.
- **Verification:** publisher landing page (ScienceDirect PII S0165410113000645) **fetched directly**; abstract and body excerpt read from that page.
- **Caveats:** the outcomes are market outcomes (liquidity), not drift. The five-country enforcement contrast is a coarse country-level instrument and cannot isolate the register's firm-level enforcement conditioning. A single country-level contrast is the paper's identification, not the register's.
- **Supports:** IDEA-011 prediction 2 (enforcement-regime concentration) as a **published** conditioner — a partial prior-art hit and a published rival to the candidate's information-quality conditioning. Bears on G-21.

### S-222 — Chincarini, L. B., Lazo-Paz, R. and Moneta, F. (2025), "Crowded spaces and anomalies", *Journal of Banking & Finance* 182, 107579. DOI: 10.1016/j.jbankfin.2025.107579.
- **Type and relevance:** peer-reviewed journal article, published 31 October 2025. Relates **crowded trades** — "those in which many investors hold the same stocks possibly exhausting their liquidity provision" — to future returns on well-known stock-market anomalies, using 13F holdings over 1980–2021 and the **Days-ADV** crowding construct of Brown, Howard and Lundblad (2021), which is the register's S-026. Finds that "anomaly risk-adjusted returns are primarily generated by most (least) crowded stocks for the long-leg (short-leg) portfolio", that the results survive publication-date controls, and hypothesises that crowded equity positions increase institutional investors' exposure to crash risk, "suggesting that crowding adds new considerations to limits to arbitrage".
- **Verification:** OpenAlex record W4415746832 **fetched directly** (API call). Title, journal, volume, article number, publication date, DOI, three authors with ORCIDs and the **complete reconstructed abstract** were taken from the OpenAlex abstract inverted index. The publisher landing page returned HTTP 403 and was **not** parsed; the abstract was therefore read from the OpenAlex record, not from the publisher.
- **Caveats:** the crowding population is **all 13F institutional investors**, not the candidate's convergence-arbitrage capital. The payoff measured is anomaly long/short portfolio returns, not the payoff of a convergence trade. The relation reported is **monotone** in crowding, whereas the candidate's claim is a two-sided (non-monotone) gate. The register's inference that the published relation bears on the candidate's high-crowding limb is the register's own, not the paper's.
- **Supports:** G-18 and G-04 for IDEA-004; IDEA-004 prediction 2 (tail asymmetry); and the claim that crowding is a first-order conditioning variable — which the candidate may no longer present as a discovery.

### S-223 — Chen, Q. and Song, X. (2025), "Crash Risk Matters: An Option-Implied Approach to the Expected Market Return", *Journal of Futures Markets* 46(3), 511–528. DOI: 10.1002/fut.70070.
- **Type and relevance:** peer-reviewed journal article, published online 8 December 2025 (print March 2026). Builds a lower bound on market log returns from simple return risk-neutral variance and log-return moments, in which "the ratio of [risk-neutral variance] to [log-return moments] captures crash risk" and the lower bound depends on the joint effect of crash risk and risk-neutral variance. Finds "crash risk exhibits significant predictive power for market returns, and its marginal effect differs markedly between crisis and noncrisis periods"; out of sample, "crash risk outperforms several benchmarks in return prediction, while the joint effects of crash risk and variance risk achieves higher accuracy in forecasting crash events".
- **Verification:** Crossref API record for DOI 10.1002/fut.70070 **fetched directly**; journal, volume, issue, pages, DOI, both authors with ORCIDs, submission/acceptance/publication dates and the **full publisher abstract** were taken from that record.
- **Caveats:** the crash measure is a **hybrid** of an option-implied variance and *realised* log-return moments, so it is not a pure decision-time option-surface measure. The paper prices the **expected market return**, not the compensation for selling variance, and it does not test whether a crash measure conditions the risk-adjusted value of a variance harvest.
- **Supports:** G-22 for IDEA-003. It characterises the published option-implied crash literature as return-predictive and crash-forecasting rather than as a variance-harvest conditioner.

### S-224 — Jagolinzer, A. D. (2008), "SEC Rule 10b5-1 and insiders' strategic trade", *Management Science* 55(2), 224–239. DOI: 10.1287/mnsc.1080.0928.
- **Type and relevance:** peer-reviewed journal article. Finds that "participating sales systematically follow positive and precede negative firm performance, generating abnormal forward-looking returns larger than those earned by non-participating colleagues", and that "a substantive proportion of randomly drawn initiations are associated with pending adverse news disclosures". This is the register's characterisation of the published 10b5-1 literature: the measured effect is attached to **participation and adoption**, not to the execution of a disclosed schedule.
- **Verification:** OpenAlex record W3121563389 **fetched directly** (API call); bibliographic fields and the **complete reconstructed abstract** taken from the record.
- **Caveats:** an adoption-window result. The paper's own framing (that Rule 10b5-1 "appears to enable insiders to engage in strategic trade") is the authors' interpretation and is contested in policy debate; the register relies only on the documented subject matter of the published effect.
- **Supports:** P-011's unblocking condition for IDEA-015: the execution-window effect remains unpublished.

### S-225 — U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, Form 10-Q (General Instruction A.1), with 17 CFR 240.13a-13 (quarterly reports) and 240.13a-11 (annual reports).
- **Type and relevance:** primary regulatory sources fixing the cadence and lag of the public capital state IDEA-010 depends on. Rule 13a-13 requires a Form 10-Q for each of the first three quarters of the fiscal year "within the period specified in General Instruction A.1 to that form"; General Instruction A.1 sets that period at **40 days** after fiscal quarter end for large accelerated and accelerated filers and **45 days** for all other registrants; Rule 13a-11 sets the annual report at **60 / 75 / 90 days** by filer status. Rule 13a-13 also exempts investment companies, foreign private issuers and asset-backed issuers, and provides that Part I need not be filed by mutual life insurance companies or by mining companies not in production.
- **Verification:** 17 CFR 240.13a-13 **fetched directly** from the Cornell LII e-CFR mirror, full rule text read. The **day-count periods in Form 10-Q General Instruction A.1** were read **only through a search-index render**: the SEC's own PDF at `sec.gov/about/forms/form10-q.pdf` refused the fetch with HTTP 403 and was not parsed. The 240.13a-11 day counts were read through an index render of the rule text.
- **Caveats:** index-rendered for the day counts; they must be re-verified against the SEC's own form before any implementation depends on them. This is the *registrant's* reporting calendar; the AP set is heterogeneous in legal form and the per-AP calendar is a separate question (G-27).
- **Supports:** G-23's cadence limb. The state is refreshed at most quarterly and is public with a 40–90 day lag.

### S-226 — Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, FR Y-9C (holding company financial reports) — submission deadlines and public availability.
- **Type and relevance:** primary regulatory source for the bank-holding-company arm of IDEA-010's state. FR Y-9C is a quarterly report; the submission deadline is **40 calendar days** after the March, June and September as-of dates and **45 calendar days** after the December as-of date, and the public data are released with a further lag and remain subject to revision until the deadline.
- **Verification:** read **only through search-index renders** of the Federal Reserve's reporting-resources material and the FFIEC National Information Center data-download documentation (including its statement that the data "are made available as received from reporting institutions" and are subject to change before the 45-calendar-day Y-9 deadline). The Federal Reserve reporting-forms page returned HTTP 404 on direct fetch and was not parsed.
- **Caveats:** index-rendered only; the day counts must be re-verified against the FR Y-9C instructions before use. This covers bank holding companies only; broker-dealer and asset-manager AP disclosure is **not** covered here and is logged in G-27.
- **Supports:** G-23's cadence limb.

### S-227 — European Commission (Directorate-General for Taxation and Customs Union), "Price of CBAM certificates".
- **Type and relevance:** primary regulator publication page for the CBAM family screen (R-016). The certificate price "reflects the average price of EU Emissions Trading System (EU ETS) allowances" and is calculated as "the weighted average of the auction clearing prices of the auctioned EU ETS allowances". For 2026 the Commission publishes **four quarterly prices** — Q1 2026 €75.36 (published 7 April 2026), Q2 2026 €75.28 (published 6 July 2026), Q3 2026 €82.32 (published 5 October 2026), and Q4 2026 to be published 4 January 2027 — each applying to sales of certificates for emissions in the corresponding quarter. **From 2027 the price is published weekly**, and from February 2027 all certificates are purchased on the common central platform (Regulation (EU) 2023/956 Article 20; Commission Implementing Regulation (EU) 2025/2548).
- **Verification:** the page was **fetched directly**; the four price rows, their publication dates, the formula description and the weekly-from-2027 statement were read from that page.
- **Caveats:** a live publication page that changes as the Q4 2026 price is published. Because the price is a deterministic function of EUA auction prices, any gap between the certificate price and the spot EUA price is a **lag artefact of a published formula**, not an independent price — which is the central reason the family is not tradeable by a third party.
- **Supports:** R-016 (the CBAM family screen).

### S-228 — European Commission (DG TAXUD), CBAM guidance and Q&A on the definitive regime: Guidance Document No. 1 "Introduction to CBAM concepts"; Guidance Document No. 4 "Calculation of the free allocation adjustment to the number of CBAM certificates to be surrendered"; "CBAM Questions and Answers"; factsheet "How do CBAM certificates work".
- **Type and relevance:** primary regulator documents fixing the obligation calendar and the mechanics of the mandatory purchase. The definitive regime applies from 1 January 2026; from 2027 an authorised declarant must hold certificates corresponding to **at least 50 %** of its embedded emissions at each quarter end; the first annual declaration and certificate surrender fall due on **30 September 2027** for 2026 imports; certificates go on sale on the central platform from **1 February 2027**; excess certificates may be repurchased by 31 October and are cancelled on 1 November, two years after purchase. Guidance Document No. 1 states that from 2026 to 2033 the embedded emissions of imported CBAM goods are "gradually covered by the CBAM obligation" and that "from 2034, 100 % of emissions embedded in the CBAM goods will be covered by CBAM certificates".
- **Verification:** the three Commission PDFs and the Q&A were read through **search-index renders** of the Commission's own document server; the Commission's "CBAM communication and news" page (entry of 30 September 2026: "Starting in 2027, CBAM importers must submit their annual CBAM declaration by 30 September for imports of CBAM goods during the preceding year") was **fetched directly** and confirms the annual date.
- **Caveats:** index-rendered for the guidance PDFs and the factsheet; the quarter-end holding dates and the 50 % rule must be re-read from the PDFs before use. The annual surrender date was **moved once**, from 31 May to 30 September (Omnibus I, Regulation (EU) 2025/2083, in force 20 October 2025) — the same "deadline moved once" feature the register flagged for IDEA-014's RIN deadline.
- **Supports:** R-016 — the dated, mandatory, quantity-fixed purchase obligation, its calendar and its one revision.

### S-229 — European Parliament, European Parliamentary Research Service (2026), "Free allocation in the EU emissions trading system", EPRS_BRI(2026)789377, 7 July 2026; with Commission Guidance Document No. 4, Table 2-1 (CBAM factor and CSCF).
- **Type and relevance:** establishes the **size** of the CBAM obligation year by year. Free allocation for the most exposed sectors (iron and steel, aluminium, cement, fertilisers and hydrogen) is phased out through the CBAM factor, with free-allocation rates of **97.5 % (2026), 95 % (2027), 90 % (2028), 77.5 % (2029), 51.5 % (2030), 39 % (2031), 26.5 % (2032) and 14 % (2033)**, ending entirely in 2034; Guidance Document No. 4 tabulates the CBAM factor as 100.0 % for 2025 and 97.5 % for 2026. Because free allocation is withdrawn in parallel, the share of embedded emissions that generates a certificate obligation is the complement: **2.5 % in 2026**, 5 % in 2027, 10 % in 2028, 22.5 % in 2029, 48.5 % in 2030, and 100 % from 2034.
- **Verification:** both documents were read through **search-index renders**. A trade-press confirmation (Light Metal Age, 5 October 2026) gives the same 97.5 %-of-benchmark free allocation for 2026 with a worked aluminium example (1.870 t CO₂e/t default value less 1.387 t free allocation = 0.483 t) — corroboration only, not verification.
- **Caveats:** the EPRS briefing is a secondary institutional summary; the Commission's own note that the CSCF values in Table 2-1 are preliminary applies. **A naming trap must be flagged**: two conventions circulate for the same number — the Directive's "CBAM factor" is the free-allocation *retention* factor (97.5 % in 2026), while the *obligation* share is its complement (2.5 % in 2026). Any implementation must state which convention it uses.
- **Supports:** R-016 — the near-zero-by-construction size of the obligation in the near term and its dated growth.

### S-230 — Umweltbundesamt (German Environment Agency) and Deutsche Emissionshandelsstelle (DEHSt), CBAM definitive-regime material on the authorised CBAM declarant, the certificate holding requirement and the consequences of non-compliance.
- **Type and relevance:** primary regulator material establishing the **participant and the constraint** for the family: an authorised CBAM declarant that fails to hold the required certificates risks the loss of its authorised-declarant status for serious or repeated infringement. That enforcement edge is what makes the purchase obligation binding rather than advisory, and it is the one element of the CBAM family that passes the register's "participant and constraint" test.
- **Verification:** read through a **search-index render** of the DEHSt's CBAM pages; not fetched directly.
- **Caveats:** index-rendered only; German-language source; the enforcement consequence must be confirmed against Regulation (EU) 2023/956 itself before it is relied upon.
- **Supports:** R-016 — the constraint that makes the flow mandatory, and the reason the family is a genuine compliance-deadline family even though its flow is not tradeable.

## Cycle 35 gap verdicts (2026-10-20)

### G-22 — CLOSED AGAINST IDEA-003 (the search was executed in this cycle, not assumed)
**Question as registered in Cycle 32:** does any published work establish that an **option-surface-implied imminent-crash measure adds risk-adjusted value to a variance harvest** beyond the published state-conditioned volatility-trading family (S-180)? If not, the candidate is a known baseline and must be parked.
**Search executed:** option-implied crash measures × variance-risk-premium conditioning, including the option-implied crash-index literature and the recent crash-risk pricing work.
**Located and characterised:** **S-223** (Chen & Song 2025, *Journal of Futures Markets*) — a hybrid option-implied/realised-moment crash measure with significant predictive power for the **expected market return**, a markedly different marginal effect in crisis and non-crisis periods, and superior out-of-sample **crash-event forecasting** when combined with variance risk. The surrounding literature (option-implied crash indices; crash risk priced in the term structure) forecasts crashes and prices crash risk.
**Verdict: no published work establishes the increment.** The published work does three different things — it forecasts crash events, it predicts the expected market return from a crash measure, and it prices crash risk — and none of it asks whether a crash state improves the risk-adjusted value of harvesting variance. By the rule registered in Cycle 32, IDEA-003 is a known baseline with an unvalidated state overlay and **must be parked**: **P-012**. This is a parking, **not** a rejection: nothing published shows the candidate's effect, so the candidate is unsupported and unranked rather than falsified. The Cycle-34 handoff's standing condition ("IDEA-003 parked (P-012, G-22 closed against)") is hereby *executed* rather than asserted — the search is on the record and the parking is written.
**Consequence:** IDEA-003 leaves the active ranking at 66.0; V-003 is marked never-to-execute; the register's active shape becomes five candidates.

### G-23 — CLOSED IN IDEA-010's FAVOUR, ON A RE-ANCHORED IDENTIFICATION (cadence and signedness)
Two limbs were registered.
**Cadence limb.** The state is public but slow. The AP set's regulatory capital ratio is refreshed only when an AP files: Form 10-Q within **40 days** of quarter end for large accelerated and accelerated filers and **45 days** for all other registrants, the annual report at 60/75/90 days by filer status (S-225), and FR Y-9C at 40 calendar days after the March/June/September as-of dates and 45 after December (S-226). The state is therefore at best quarterly and lagged.
**Resolution.** The candidate's claim never required the state to change *inside* the holding window: it requires the **level** of the AP set's capital ratio to govern the **size** of the flow-induced discount widening and its reversion. A state that is constant between filings is fully usable for a claim of that form, and the published relation itself (S-194) identifies the state at filing frequency — the state was never a session-frequency variable in the published evidence either. Therefore the state must be used as a **slow filter** and the identification must be **cross-sectional**: weak-capital ETFs versus strong-capital ETFs on redemption days, with the state held constant between filings; the within-ETF time-series reading is demoted to a **low-power ablation** that must be reported as such. The 1–10 session horizon governs the **reversion of the flow-induced widening**, not the refresh of the state, so **the horizon is preserved, no horizon extension is required, and the candidate is not parked**. The cost of the resolution is recorded rather than hidden: the effective sample is the number of (ETF, filing-date) state observations, which is small; the validator must report that count and **"untested" must remain a permitted verdict** (V-010 already permits it).
**Signedness limb.** The literature half closes: S-194 establishes a **signed, monotone** relation between the AP-set capital state and arbitrage intensity (a 0.69pp versus 0.39pp decline in arbitrage intensity for bottom- versus top-quartile capital ratios), so the state is signed in the mechanism's direction and not merely a moderator. The residual is not a literature question but a specification obligation, and it closes as one: the signed prediction — *on redemption days, weak-capital ETFs' discounts widen more than strong-capital ETFs' discounts* — becomes the candidate's first pre-registered, killable test, with three mandatory rival controls (S-196 fractional baskets, entering as a level effect absorbed by ETF fixed effects; S-197 funding segmentation, controlled for with public funding-state variables; S-199 client liquidity discounts, controlled for by requiring the bond-side cheapening to be specific to the ETF's own AP set and redemption days) and the monthly published dispersion proxy (S-200) as a robustness check.
**Verdict: G-23 is closed in the candidate's favour**, subject to a mandatory effective-sample report and a new hard-stop flag. V-010 is amended accordingly (Cycle-35 amendment). The residual is registered as **G-27**.

### G-21 — NARROWED TO A NAMED DISCRIMINATOR (IDEA-011); IDEA-015's limb confirmed, P-011 stands
**IDEA-011 limb.** The registered question was whether the Greek post-adoption drift increase (S-188) is a **first-adoption comparability clock** or a **Greece-specific disclosure shock**. Three findings now bound the answer.
1. The only first-adoption case in the candidate's direction is Greece, and its own authors attribute the drift increase to IFRS **reducing earnings predictability** — reduced accounting conservatism and higher earnings volatility — which is a disclosure shock, not a learning clock (S-188).
2. The other first-adoption case runs the other way: K-IFRS adoption **reduced** post-adoption drift (S-179). "First adoption" therefore does not predict the sign of the drift effect.
3. The published literature on post-adoption market outcomes attributes the effects to **enforcement**, not to a learning or comparability clock: the EU liquidity benefits of mandatory IFRS adoption are concentrated in the five EU countries that made substantive enforcement changes (S-221), and the capital-market benefits are largest for firms that had already switched voluntarily — firms with comparability experience — with a further effect at the mandate date, and with part of the effect already present **before** the official adoption date (S-220).
**The discriminator is now named and specified.** A comparability clock predicts that the drift increase appears in **first-time adopters** of a standard whose transition creates a comparability break, and is absent or reversed in **second-time adopters** (firms or jurisdictions already reporting under IFRS-equivalent standards — Australia and Canada 2004–05, South Africa 2005, the EU's 2005 voluntary adopters). A generic "IFRS changes the numbers" effect (S-188's own reading) predicts both groups move together. No study located in this cycle runs that comparison on drift; the studies that run the mandatory-versus-voluntary comparison (S-220, S-221) do so on liquidity, cost of capital and valuation.
**Verdict: G-21 is narrowed, not closed.** The candidate's claim now has **no published case in which post-adoption drift increased under a mechanism attributable to a comparability clock**, and the candidate has become adjacent to a published conditioner — enforcement — that it must not present as its own. No score change: the evidence and novelty scores are unchanged, but the *content* of the evidence base has changed from "partial prior art (Greece)" to "partial prior art plus a published rival conditioner (enforcement) plus an anticipation caveat (S-220)".
**IDEA-015 limb (P-011).** The registered unblocking condition was published work that (a) constructs a security-level remaining scheduled supply state from Item 408(a) and Form 4 filings and (b) documents a price effect attached to the **execution** window rather than the **adoption** window. The published 10b5-1 literature attaches its effect to participation and adoption: plan sales "systematically follow positive and precede negative firm performance, generating abnormal forward-looking returns", and a substantive proportion of initiations are associated with pending adverse news (S-224). No execution-window result was located. **P-011 stands** and IDEA-015 remains parked.

### G-18 — CLOSED AS A BOUNDED NEGATIVE THAT SUPERSEDES ITS OWN PREMISE; G-04 RESTATED (IDEA-004)
The registered G-18 question was whether comomentum (S-157) had been validated against a holdings-based crowding measure (S-026). That question is now **void**, and the reason is itself the finding: the literature never needed the cross-validation, because each construct has been validated against an **outcome** instead. The holdings-based construct is now an active published object with return-predictive results of its own — crowding, measured on Days-ADV from 13F holdings over 1980–2021, is positively related to subsequent abnormal returns on well-known anomalies, with anomaly risk-adjusted returns "primarily generated by most (least) crowded stocks for the long-leg (short-leg) portfolio", surviving publication-date controls, and with the authors hypothesising that crowded equity positions increase institutional exposure to crash risk and therefore "crowding adds new considerations to limits to arbitrage" (S-222). The correlation-based construct (S-157) likewise carries published return-predictive results of its own.
**Verdict: G-18 is closed as a bounded negative** — no published work validates one crowding proxy against the other, and none is needed — **and its premise is superseded**: the candidate may no longer be presented as discovering that crowding is a first-order conditioning variable, because that is now published (S-026, S-222). The published relation is **monotone** in crowding whereas the candidate's claim is a **two-sided (non-monotone) gate**; the published crowding population is all 13F institutional investors rather than the candidate's convergence-arbitrage capital; and the published payoff is anomaly long/short portfolio returns rather than a convergence trade's payoff. None of that kills the candidate and none of it confirms it: it makes the candidate's specific two-sided gate the thing that must be tested, and it makes the **state definition** the load-bearing specification.
**G-04 is therefore restated** and the new residual is registered as **G-28**. No score change (evidence 4 → 4: the new source corroborates the family and the tail limb but does not support the two-sided conditioning, which remains the stated reason for not scoring 5).

### G-24 and G-25 — CLOSED AS BOUNDED NEGATIVES FOR IDEA-014'S CONDITIONAL VERSION
The Cycle-33 header claimed that G-24 was closed **in IDEA-014's favour** because "a replacement state exists in the RGGI CO₂ allowance market, producing new candidate IDEA-016 RGGI-COMPLIANCE", and the Cycle 34 handoff repeated it. **No such candidate, source or verdict ever existed in this repository** (checked file by file and against the full git history on 2026-10-20). The *Cycle-34 conclusion* — that no pre-deadline, signed, non-circular replacement state was found for the RIN cluster, so the conditional version of IDEA-014 is retired to a state-free existence test — is independently supportable on its own terms and is recorded here as the operative verdict.
**Verdict: G-24 and G-25 are closed as bounded negatives for IDEA-014's conditional version.** The RGGI lead is logged as an **unwritten lead** — no dossier, no source, no verdict — and is **not** a candidate; a later cycle may pursue it only by writing it from primary sources under the next free IDEA identifier. IDEA-014 retains its **state-free existence limb** at 52.0, with the hard stop on the conditional version unchanged.

### G-27 — OPENED (IDEA-010): the effective sample, and how fast the capital state can actually be refreshed
Two questions survive G-23's closure, and both are data questions that must be answered before the candidate can be ranked honestly.
1. **Effective sample.** Under the re-anchored cross-sectional identification, the informative units are (ETF, filing-date) state observations and the redemption days inside them. The number of US corporate-bond ETFs with a reconstructable AP set, multiplied by the number of quarters in which the state is bottom-tercile, is small, and the March 2020 dash-for-cash episode is the episode in which the published state bound (S-194). The validator must report the count, and **"untested" must remain a permitted verdict**: a null in a sample of one episode is not a kill.
2. **State frequency and comparability across AP types.** The AP set is heterogeneous in legal form: bank holding companies file FR Y-9C (S-226) and periodic reports (S-225); broker-dealers file under the securities laws and the **firm-level public availability of a broker-dealer's regulatory capital ratio** is a separate question this cycle did not resolve; asset managers are a third case. The per-AP cadence and the comparability of the ratios across those regimes need a written answer, and the register must not assume a single quarterly cadence for a heterogeneous set.
**Verdict: open.** No score change (data 4 → 4: the state is public in principle, and the residual costs are now registered here rather than in G-23).

### G-28 — OPENED (IDEA-004): which crowd is the state?
IDEA-004's claim is that the **depth of the crowd** in a convergence trade is a first-order conditioning variable on the trade's expected return and its crash behaviour, in a two-sided (non-monotone) way. The newest published work (S-222) measures a *different* crowd — all 13F institutional investors holding the same stock, i.e. directional positioning — with a monotone crowding-to-return relation and a published crash-risk channel. S-026's Days-ADV over hedge-fund holdings and S-157's comomentum are different constructs again. The candidate's state is a **position-concentration measure of the convergence/arbitrage crowd in its own universe** (pairs, merger-spread and index-convergence relative value), and no published measure of *that* crowd was located. Until that measure is specified, the candidate's rule cannot be written, and the published neighbours (S-026, S-157, S-222) are **not** substitutes for it.
**Verdict: open.** V-002 therefore stays frozen, but for a **new and sharper reason** than the one it was frozen under: not "validate comomentum against a holdings-based crowding measure" (a question the literature never needed) but "specify the convergence-crowd measure and re-anchor the rule to its own cadence" (G-28 plus the Cycle-6 filing-anchoring finding). No score change.

### Record of the Cycle-35 state-integrity repair
Checked on 2026-10-20, file by file and against the full git history: the Cycle-33 header and the Cycle-34 handoff asserted **S-210 … S-218**, the "Cycle 33 sources" and "Cycle 33 gap verdicts" blocks, **IDEA-016**, **V-011**, **R-014**, **P-012**, **P-013** and a formal **G-25** verdict, none of which exist or ever existed. Cycle 35 replaced the false header, **executed** the two claimed closures that are research questions (G-22 → P-012; G-23 → the V-010 amendment), **wrote R-014** for real, and re-derived the Cycle-34 claim about G-24/G-25 from Cycle 34's own recorded conclusion rather than from the unwritten RGGI dossier. The identifiers **IDEA-016, S-210 … S-219, V-011, G-26 and P-013 are reserved and unused**; the next free identifiers are therefore **IDEA-017, S-231, V-012, G-29, R-017 and P-014**, with IDEA-016, S-210 … S-219, V-011, G-26 and P-013 deliberately left unused inside their reserved ranges so that no identifier can collide with a later reconstruction of the missing Cycle-33 work.

## Cycle 36 gap verdicts (recorded in Cycle 37, 2026-10-20)

**Why this block exists.** Cycle 36's verdicts were written into the cycle log and the handoff prose
but never into this file, which is the register's canonical home for gap verdicts. That is the
state-integrity failure the Cycle-35 repair was created to stop: a verdict that exists only in prose
cannot be checked against the artifact set. Cycle 37 re-derives Cycle 36's conclusions from the
Cycle-36 cycle-log entry and the Cycle-36 handoff text, writes them here, and then **supersedes one
of them** on new evidence.

### G-27 — as recorded by Cycle 36: "untested" — **WITHDRAWN by Cycle 37, see the Cycle-37 verdicts block below**
Cycle 36 recorded the effective-sample and state-frequency residual for IDEA-010 as untested, on the
stated ground that "broker-dealer firm-level regulatory capital ratios are not publicly available in
a comparable, machine-readable, decision-time form across the full AP set" and that "broker-dealers
file FOCUS reports which are not generally disseminated as regular public datasets in the same way
that FR Y-9C or call-report data are disseminated". The claim was reasonable on its face and it was
**not checked against EDGAR in that cycle**. Cycle 37 checked, and the claim is withdrawn: Form
X-17A-5 is a **public** EDGAR form type and the net-capital figures are in publicly indexed
documents (S-231, S-232, S-238).

### G-28 — as recorded by Cycle 36: "not constructible from published data" — **CONFIRMED, and sharpened by Cycle 37**
Cycle 36 concluded that the convergence/arbitrage crowd's position-concentration measure is not
constructible from published data, so IDEA-004's rule cannot be specified and V-002 stays frozen.
Cycle 37 confirms the conclusion but replaces the bare statement with a named alternative
construction and the reason it still fails (see the Cycle-37 verdicts block).

### G-21 — as recorded by Cycle 36: null — **CONFIRMED by Cycle 37**
Cycle 36 found no new evidence on the first-adopter-versus-second-adopter post-earnings-announcement
drift comparison, so the discriminator remains untested by any published work. Cycle 37 searched once
more and confirms the null, then writes the comparison down precisely so that the next agent can
hand it to a validator rather than re-search it.

### IDEA-014's state-free existence limb — as recorded by Cycle 36: inconclusive — **CONFIRMED by Cycle 37**
Cycle 36 could not establish whether a state-free "does the pattern exist at all" limb exists for
IDEA-014's family. Cycle 37 confirms the inconclusive status; the limb stays open.

### The Cycle-36 ex-dividend family screen — logged as **R-017**
Cycle 36 ran a family screen on **ex-dividend date mechanics** — a dated, mechanical, price-insensitive
supply event — and recorded it as a bounded negative. The result existed only in the cycle log; the
artifact was never written. Cycle 37 wrote it as **R-017** in `rejected_and_parked.md` (see there for
the full entry and the reason the family is a baseline). No source was added for it because the
screen asserts no external claim beyond the mechanical nature of the ex-dividend price adjustment.
