## Cycle 37 sources (2026-10-20)

Eight entries, located and metadata-checked in Cycle 37. Five support the G-27 state-availability
correction (S-231 … S-235) and three support the Cycle-37 family screen on Rule 201 (S-236 … S-238).
The register's rule is applied strictly: **"fetched directly"** means the document or its bibliographic
or machine-readable record was retrieved and parsed in this session; **"index-rendered"** means it was
read only through a search-engine index render and must be re-verified before any implementation
relies on it.

### S-231 — U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, **EDGAR full-text search (document-level index of public filings)**, queried for Form X-17A-5.
- **Type and relevance:** primary regulator evidence for the single most important finding of Cycle 37:
  **Form X-17A-5 (the FOCUS report) is a public EDGAR form type, and the net-capital figures of
  broker-dealers are in publicly indexed documents.** This is the filing route by which a *private*,
  non-listed authorised participant's firm-level regulatory capital can be obtained. The FOCUS
  submission carries distinct document types — "FULL", "COMP NET CAP" (computation of net capital),
  "COMP RESERV REQ", "STMT FIN COND", "STMT FIN COND NOTES" — and, for some filers, a "PUBLIC REPORT"
  and a "CONFIDENTIAL" document in the same submission.
- **Verification:** **fetched directly** — the EDGAR full-text search JSON API was called and parsed in
  this session. Query `q="excess net capital"&forms=X-17A-5` returns **6,131** public documents;
  `q="net capital"&forms=X-17A-5` returns **10,000+** (the API's "gte" ceiling). Firm-specific
  queries return, for example: **Citadel Securities Institutional LLC** (CIK 1649718), FY2025,
  document `CSIN_BS_Only_FS_2025.pdf`, file type "FULL", filed 27 February 2026, matching "excess net
  capital"; **AllianceBernstein Investments, Inc.** (CIK 732760), FY2018, document
  `fscompaltnetcap.pdf`, file type **"COMP NET CAP"**, filed 28 February 2019; **Virtu Americas LLC**
  (CIK 1457716), eight documents FY2017–FY2025, file types "FULL"/"STMT FIN COND"/"STMT FIN COND
  NOTES", file descriptions "PUBLIC REPORT", "PUBLIC ANNUAL REPORTS", "SOFC"; **KCG Americas LLC**
  (CIK 1457716), two documents; **RFQ-Hub Americas LLC** (CIK 1947629), submissions carrying both a
  "PUBLIC REPORT" and a "CONFIDENTIAL" file description; **Citadel Securities LLC** (CIK 1146184), 26
  X-17A-5 documents spanning FY2016–FY2025; **Citadel Securities Institutional LLC** (CIK 1649718), 28
  documents; **Citadel Securities Swap Dealer LLC** (CIK 1947163), eight documents across two
  display-name variants; **IEX Services LLC** (CIK 1576413); **ACS Execution Services LLC** (period
  endings 2024-12-31 and 2025-12-31); **Agentis Capital Markets US, LLC** (period ending **2024-09-30**,
  i.e. a *quarterly* filing exists for at least some filers). The Citadel-keyword query
  `q="Citadel Securities"&forms=X-17A-5` returns **68** documents in total across those filers.
- **Caveats:** the search API was fetched directly, but **the underlying filing documents were not
  opened** — direct `sec.gov/Archives` fetches returned HTTP 403 in this environment, so the *values*
  of net capital and excess net capital were not read line by line. The API confirms that the phrases
  occur in publicly indexed documents of the named firms; it does not by itself confirm which line
  items each document carries. **A note on what the index does and does not establish:** the index
  covers documents EDGAR has *publicly posted*, but a document's **file description is a filer-supplied
  label, not an access rule** — the RFQ-Hub Americas submission, for example, carries one document
  labelled "PUBLIC REPORT" and another labelled "CONFIDENTIAL", and **both are posted and indexed**.
  So the correct inference is "the net-capital figures are in documents that EDGAR has publicly
  posted", not "the filer's own confidential label is what the index excludes". A validator who needs
  the *values* must open the document and read them.
- **Cycle 40 extension (the count instrument, its limits, and the FR Y-9C finding).** Cycle 40 used
  this index as a *counting* instrument and produced three corrections that must be carried forward.
  1. **The phrase corpus is a lower bound on firms, not a count of firms.** The query returns 6,131
     documents from **2,419 distinct filers**, but filer-level enumeration of the same firms' own
     filing indexes shows how far the two diverge: **Citadel Securities LLC (CIK 1146184) files 24
     X-17A-5 documents across 23 periods (2003-12-31 … 2025-12-31) and contains the phrase in only
     one of them**, so a phrase count is a *wording* count. The correct instrument for any
     firm-coverage question is filer-level enumeration (CIK × form × report date), which in Cycle 40
     was done through the EDGAR submissions endpoint (S-240).
  2. **The cadence claim inside this entry needs qualification.** Re-derived at filer level over the
     2,419 filers in this corpus: **2,414 disclose once per year and five disclose twice in a year;
     none discloses three or more times a year.** The entry's phrase "quarterly for at least some
     private filers" rests on a single document (Agentis Capital Markets US, LLC, period ending
     2024-09-30) that does **not** appear in the phrase corpus at all; it must be re-verified from that
     firm's own filing index before it is repeated. For the firms that the candidate's contrast
     actually depends on, Cycle 40's filer-level check of **thirteen named AP firms and fifteen major
     private AP firms found annual cadence in every case** (fiscal year-end, filed February–March).
  3. **Form FR Y-9C is not indexed by EDGAR's full-text search at all.** Queries restricted to
     `forms=FR Y-9C` return **zero** hits for "capital", "net capital" and "Federal Reserve" — the
     form is not in the search index. The bank-holding-company leg of G-27 therefore **cannot be
     enumerated through EDGAR**, and the register's S-226 route must be restated as a Federal Reserve
     National Information Center route rather than an EDGAR route. This is a limit of the instrument,
     not evidence about the filings.
- **Supports:** **G-27 limb 1** — the state-availability question for IDEA-010. It is the direct
  refutation of Cycle 36's claim that firm-level broker-dealer regulatory capital is not publicly
  available, and it establishes the *document-level* nature of the route (which is why the register's
  data score is unchanged at 4/5: available in principle, but not a tidy dataset).

### S-232 — Alden Associates, Inc., **Form X-17A-5 Part III annual report** (public annual audit report filed on SEC EDGAR).
- **Type and relevance:** a specimen of the *public* annual FOCUS filing, used to establish the shape
  of the document the state would be read from: the cover caption is "FOCUS Report with computation of
  net capital or tangible net capital", and the statement of financial condition opens with "Total
  Assets …" followed by "Liabilities and …".
- **Verification:** read through a **search-index render** of the filing document; **not fetched
  directly** (`sec.gov/Archives` returned HTTP 403 in this environment).
- **Caveats:** index-rendered only; the specimen is a small broker-dealer, not an authorised
  participant; the specific "Net capital" and "Excess net capital" line values were **not** read in
  this cycle. The document's own caption is suggestive but is not by itself proof that the computed
  values are in the public part of a filing.
- **Supports:** G-27 limb 1, as the specimen of the document type; it must be replaced by a directly
  read document for a major AP before the state is relied upon (this is carried into G-29).

### S-233 — LPL Financial Inc., **Form 10-Q, Exhibit R27, "Net Capital and Regulatory Requirements"** (XBRL-tagged).
- **Type and relevance:** the *listed* broker-dealer route to the same quantity: a public, **quarterly**,
  XBRL-tagged table giving Net capital, Less: required net capital, and Excess net capital for the
  registrant's broker-dealer subsidiary (LPL Financial LLC) — shown as net capital $443,742 thousand,
  required net capital $19,426 thousand, excess net capital $424,316 thousand, as of the period ended
  31 December 2024. This is the quarterly, machine-readable half of the state route.
- **Verification:** read through a **search-index render** of the SEC-hosted exhibit; **not fetched
  directly** (`sec.gov/Archives` returned HTTP 403 in this environment).
- **Caveats:** index-rendered only; a single issuer's exhibit; the XBRL tags are as rendered by the
  index, not confirmed against the filing's own taxonomy.
- **Supports:** G-27 limb 1 (the listed-broker-dealer, quarterly route) and the register's statement
  that the state's cadence differs by AP type — annual for private broker-dealers, quarterly for
  listed broker-dealers and bank holding companies.

### S-234 — FINRA, **BrokerCheck disclosure framework (Rule 8312; Regulatory Notice 09-66) and FINRA's published firm-data pages (Industry Snapshot, "Firm Data"; 2026 Annual Regulatory Oversight Report, "Net Capital")**.
- **Type and relevance:** establishes the *negative* half of the availability question, which the
  register needs as much as the positive half: FINRA's BrokerCheck disclosure set covers registration,
  disciplinary actions, arbitration awards, Form BD and IAPD information and **does not release
  firm-level net capital**, and FINRA publishes **excess net capital only as an aggregate by firm-size
  bucket** (Industry Snapshot, "Excess Net Capital By Firm Size"), not firm by firm. FINRA's
  regulatory-oversight reporting separately confirms that net capital under SEA Rule 15c3-1 is a
  monitored quantity and that failures to report a net-capital deficiency are a supervisory finding.
- **Verification:** read through **search-index renders**; **not fetched directly**.
- **Caveats:** index-rendered only; the exact enumerated disclosure set of Rule 8312 must be confirmed
  against the rule text before it is cited in a specification. The aggregate-by-size publication is a
  statement about FINRA's *published tables*, not about what FINRA holds internally.
- **Supports:** G-27 limb 1 (route selection — a candidate cannot use BrokerCheck or FINRA's aggregate
  tables for firm-level state) and the register's decision to treat the FOCUS/10-Q/FR Y-9C filings as
  the only firm-level routes.

### S-235 — U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, **press release 2010-26, "SEC Approves Short Selling Restrictions" (24 February 2010)**, and the Division of Trading and Markets **FAQs on Rule 201 of Regulation SHO (17 CFR 242.201)**.
- **Type and relevance:** primary regulator material stating the mechanics of the Rule 201
  alternative uptick rule: the circuit breaker is triggered when a covered security's price declines
  by 10 % or more from the prior day's closing price; the price-test restriction then applies to
  short sale orders "for the remainder of the day and the following day"; a further 10 % decline
  re-triggers it; orders may be marked "short exempt" in defined categories; compliance was required
  from 28 February 2011.
- **Verification:** read through **search-index renders** of the SEC's own pages; **not fetched
  directly**.
- **Caveats:** index-rendered only; the rule text itself (17 CFR 242.201) should be cited directly in
  any specification.
- **Supports:** **R-018** — the rule facts of the Cycle-37 family screen (a *published, real-time
  threshold* that switches off a seller for a *published, bounded duration*).

### S-236 — Barardehi, Y., Bird, A., Karolyi, S. A. and Ruchti, T. G. (2025), "Are Short-selling Restrictions Effective?", *Management Science* 71(5), 3829–3851. DOI: 10.1287/mnsc.2024.4987.
- **Type and relevance:** peer-reviewed, published, **positive** result on exactly the claim a Rule-201
  candidate would have to make. Using quasi-random variation from the −10 % threshold rule, it finds
  that "the restriction leads to 8% lower short-sale volume and 35 bps higher daily returns" and that
  "these price effects do not reverse after the restriction is lifted"; the working-paper key findings
  also report "narrower spreads and an increase in depth at best-ask price", consistent with short
  sellers switching from removing liquidity to providing it. The identification is a threshold design
  comparing stocks on either side of the trigger in the same hour.
- **Verification:** bibliographic record **cross-checked across three independent records read through
  search-index renders** — the EconPapers entry (journal, volume, issue, pages, abstract), the Chapman
  University Digital Commons record (DOI, journal, pages, publication date 22 August 2024) and the
  Office of Financial Research working-paper version (OFR WP 23-08, published 11 October 2023, with
  authors' affiliations and the abstract). The *journal* PDF was **not** fetched.
- **Caveats:** the numbers quoted above are from the abstract and the working-paper key findings, which
  agree; the published version's point estimates may differ in the third decimal and must be taken from
  the journal version before any use. The working paper also notes that the short-selling opportunities
  restricted by Rule 201 "are transient in nature", which is itself a constraint on any trade built on
  the trigger.
- **Supports:** **R-018** — the family is a **baseline**, not a candidate. The mechanism, the
  identification and the positive sign are all published.

### S-237 — Molnár (2022), "Are shorts restricted when options are an option? Evidence from SEC Rule 201", working paper, Department of Finance, Banking and Corporate Finance, WU Vienna (file `20220926_MOLNAR.pdf`).
- **Type and relevance:** working paper establishing the *circumvention* channel for the same rule: after
  a Rule 201 trigger, "shorting activity decreases and put option activity increases" — put option open
  interest up 61 % and put trading volume up 87 %, call option open interest up 40 % and call volume up
  80 % — which the author reads as "a migration of short sellers from the stock market to the options
  market". It also states the compliance date of 28 February 2011 and the rule's terms.
- **Verification:** read through a **search-index render** of the working-paper PDF hosted by WU Vienna;
  **not fetched directly**. The author attribution was taken from the file name and the paper's own
  author block was not separately confirmed.
- **Caveats:** index-rendered only; a working paper, not peer-reviewed; the given name and the working
  paper series number were not confirmed and are therefore not asserted here.
- **Supports:** **R-018** — the constraint is not airtight, because a seller who cannot short the
  equity can express the position in the option market.

### S-238 — Switzer, L. N. (2023), "Circumventing SEC Rule 201 short sale restrictions with options", ScienceDirect record S154461232300363X.
- **Type and relevance:** a second, independent statement that Rule 201 can be circumvented at low cost
  through options, "particularly for firms with options"; it also summarises the prior literature
  that finds short-sale bans "ineffective, or … lead to lower market quality, higher bid-ask spreads
  [and] higher volatility".
- **Verification:** read through a **search-index render** of the publisher record; **not fetched
  directly**. The publisher record ID is recorded so that the entry can be re-verified.
- **Caveats:** the **journal title was not confirmed** in this cycle and is therefore not asserted; the
  record was seen only in the index render, so the paper's own identification and results were not read
  in the article text.
- **Supports:** **R-018** — corroborates S-237 on circumvention and shows the family is already a
  published baseline rather than an open question.

## Cycle 37 gap verdicts (2026-10-20)

### G-27 — **CLOSED IN IDEA-010'S FAVOUR** on state availability; the residual is restated as G-29

**Verdict.** Cycle 37 executed the search Cycle 36 declined to execute, and Cycle 36's central claim
is **withdrawn as incorrect**. Firm-level broker-dealer regulatory capital — net capital and excess
net capital — **is publicly available**, and it is available for the *private, non-listed* authorised
participants that the candidate's contrast actually depends on.

**Evidence.**
1. **Form X-17A-5 is a public EDGAR form type, and the net-capital figures are in publicly indexed
   documents** (S-231, fetched directly): 6,131 public X-17A-5 documents contain the phrase "excess
   net capital" and 10,000+ contain "net capital". The route covers **Citadel Securities** (three
   CIKs), **Virtu Americas** (eight documents, FY2017–FY2025, file descriptions "PUBLIC REPORT" and
   "PUBLIC ANNUAL REPORTS"), **KCG Americas**, **AllianceBernstein Investments** (whose FY2018
   document has file type **"COMP NET CAP"** — a dedicated public computation-of-net-capital
   document), **RFQ-Hub Americas**, **IEX Services** and others. The hits are in documents EDGAR has
   **publicly posted and indexed**, which is the form of publicity the candidate needs; the *values*
   still have to be read out of the documents by a validator, and the filer's own "CONFIDENTIAL"
   description on a document is a label rather than an access rule (both descriptions are posted for
   RFQ-Hub).
2. **Listed broker-dealers disclose the same quantity quarterly and XBRL-tagged** in their Exchange
   Act periodic reports (S-233: LPL Financial's Form 10-Q exhibit R27 gives net capital, required net
   capital and excess net capital for its broker-dealer subsidiary as of 31 December 2024).
3. **Bank authorised participants** are already covered by the register (FR Y-9C / call reports,
   S-226).
4. **The obvious routes are closed**, which matters for a specification: FINRA's BrokerCheck does not
   release firm-level net capital, and FINRA publishes excess net capital only in aggregate by
   firm-size bucket (S-234). A candidate must use the filings, not the regulator's website tables.

**Qualifications that are part of the verdict, not a caveat on it.**
- **Cadence.** Annual for private broker-dealers (fiscal year-end; filed February–March; the state is
  therefore 40–90 days stale at any decision date), quarterly for listed broker-dealers and bank
  holding companies. At least some filers file quarterly (S-231 records a period ending 30 September
  2024), so the cadence is firm-dependent and must be measured per firm rather than assumed.
- **Format.** Document-level filings, not a structured dataset: the route requires parsing per firm per
  year from EDGAR, using the public full-text index to locate the documents (S-231, S-232).
- **Comparability.** FOCUS net capital is a *liquidity-style* capital quantity and is **not**
  commensurable with a bank holding company's risk-based ratios, and an asset-manager authorised
  participant has no equivalent. A cross-sectional contrast must therefore be built within a
  comparable class, or the mapping must be justified explicitly — this is the register's standing
  condition on heterogeneous state (G-04's family), not a new gap.
- **Coverage.** The public filing route is present for a *subset* of private APs and its *depth*
  varies by firm and year (for the three Citadel CIKs the phrase is indexed for the FY2025
  Institutional filing). The number of usable (authorised participant, year) observations is a
  countable quantity that has **not** been counted.

**What this changes, and what it does not.**
- It **does** change the state-availability half of G-27 from "not publicly available" to
  "publicly available, with a per-firm cadence and a document-parsing route". The register had already
  scored the state as public *in principle* (IDEA-010's data dimension, 4/5), so **no score changes**
  and IDEA-010 remains at 68.0, rank 3.
- It **does not** resolve the effective-sample question. Under the re-anchored cross-sectional
  identification (G-23) the state varies across *authorised participants*, so the relevant sample is
  (ETF, authorised participant, redemption episode) cells, and that count now depends on a second
  fact that is itself not established: **which ETFs name their authorised participants in a public
  filing.** That is now **G-29**.
- **Hard-stop flags for IDEA-010 are strengthened, not relaxed**: the state must be reconstructed from
  filings (FOCUS for private broker-dealers, 10-Q/10-K for listed ones, FR Y-9C for BHCs) with the
  per-firm cadence measured rather than assumed; the comparison must be within a comparable capital
  regime; and the effective-sample report required by G-23 must now also report the **public-AP
  coverage count** that G-29 defines.

**Cycle-36 record superseded.** The Cycle-36 verdict recorded above ("untested", on the stated ground
that broker-dealer FOCUS reports "are not generally disseminated as regular public datasets in the same
way that FR Y-9C or call-report data are disseminated") was **true of a dataset and false of the
filings**, and the distinction is exactly the distinction the candidate needs. The register's lesson
is recorded: *a verdict that rests on "there is no tidy dataset" must not be recorded as "there is no
public data" without checking the filing level.*

### G-29 — **OPENED** — the ETF → authorised-participant mapping and the coverage count that bounds IDEA-010

**Question.** Which corporate-bond ETFs name their authorised participants in a **public** filing, and
how many (ETF, authorised participant) cells exist in which *both* a published AP-set membership and a
publicly available capital ratio exist?

**Why it is decisive.** IDEA-010's identification is a *cross-sectional* contrast: an ETF whose
arbitrage capital is capital-constrained should show a different redemption-day discount behaviour
from an ETF whose arbitrage capital is unconstrained. Mapping an ETF to a capital state requires
knowing **which firms arbitrage it**. If the per-ETF AP set is not published, the state cannot be
attached to an ETF and the candidate cannot be specified at all — which is a stronger version of the
Cycle-36 verdict than the one Cycle 36 was able to state.

**What is known.** (i) Some ETF statutory prospectuses do name their authorised participants: trade
press reported in April 2024 that the iShares Bitcoin Trust prospectus lists ABN AMRO Clearing, Jane
Street Capital, JPMorgan Securities and Citadel Securities as APs, and that a subsequent filing
added Citadel Securities — **this is a lead read through a search-index render of trade press, and the
prospectus itself was not read**, so it is not a verified source and is deliberately not in the
ledger as one. (ii) The corporate-bond ETF summary prospectuses read in this cycle (iShares Core U.S.
Aggregate Bond ETF; iShares Core 1-5 Year USD Bond ETF) disclose authorised-participant *risk* in
generic terms — "a limited number of financial institutions that may act as Authorized Participants" —
without naming them. (iii) The AP firms themselves name each other in their own Exchange Act filings:
Virtu Financial's 10-K describes itself as an authorised participant in over 2,000 ETPs and names
Citadel Securities, Susquehanna International Group, Two Sigma, Jane Street, DRW, IMC and Optiver as
the major participants in the same business — this establishes that the AP universe is documented in
public filings, though not the per-fund mapping.

**Bounds on the answer (the register's own counting rules).** The count cannot exceed the number of
corporate-bond ETFs (a small set) times the number of their named APs with a public capital ratio. If
per-ETF AP naming turns out to be unavailable for bond ETFs, the candidate must **not** be
re-specified around a weaker proxy (S-026's Days-ADV crowding, S-157's comomentum, S-222's crowding
factor) — the register's standing condition forbids that, and the published crowding measures measure
a *different crowd* (all 13F institutions, directional positioning) rather than the convergence crowd
IDEA-004 needs.

### G-28 — **CONFIRMED open** and sharpened: the convergence-crowd measure is still not constructible, and the register now says why in a way a validator can act on

**Verdict.** IDEA-004's rule still cannot be written; V-002 stays frozen; no score change. Cycle 37
adds the one construction route that has not previously been named, and the four reasons it is
insufficient, so that the next agent does not have to re-derive them.

**The route.** A position-concentration measure of the convergence crowd could in principle be
**constructed from Form 13F holdings**: a manager that discloses long holdings in *both* legs of a
pair, or in a merger-spread constituent and its acquirer, is by construction running convergence
capital, and its disclosed holdings give the position sizes. The published crowding measures are
neighbours, not substitutes: S-026's Days-ADV over hedge-fund holdings and S-222's crowding factor
measure *all* 13F institutional investors, i.e. directional positioning, and S-157's comomentum
measures arbitrage activity inferred from return correlations without identifying who is doing it.

**Why it still fails the register's bar.**
1. **It is a construction, not a published measure.** The register's data rule requires a *published*
   state from a primary source; a measure built by the register would have to be validated against an
   outcome before it could be used to condition a rule, and validating it requires the outcome and
   the crowding state simultaneously.
2. **It captures only the visible long leg.** 13F reports long positions in Section 13(f) securities
   only: it does not disclose short positions, swaps or credit instruments, so a convergence trade
   whose economically decisive leg is short is invisible, and a manager who has hedged one leg away
   is indistinguishable from one who never had it.
3. **It cannot separate convergence capital from a coincident directional holder.** A manager holding
   both legs of a pair for reasons other than convergence is counted identically.
4. **It cannot support the cadence the rule needs.** The register's already-established 45-day 13F
   filing lag means the measure is a quarter-slow, while IDEA-004's rule is re-anchored to its own
   cadence; a state that cannot be refreshed at the rule's frequency cannot condition it.

**What would change the verdict.** A *published* measure of convergence-crowd position concentration
at the candidate's own cadence — or a published validation of the holdings-matching construction
against an outcome — would be enough. Absent that, G-28 remains open and IDEA-004's rule remains
unspecifiable, and the register must not substitute a published crowding measure for it.

### G-21 — **CONFIRMED null** — the first-adopter-versus-second-adopter drift comparison is still untested by any published work

**Verdict.** Cycle 37 searched once more and found no published study that compares post-earnings-
announcement drift between first-time and second-time adopters of a common accounting standard. The
discriminator remains a null: the register holds no evidence either way, and the candidate's
discriminating comparison remains **untested** rather than refuted. No score change; IDEA-011 stands
at **69.0, rank 1** (tied with IDEA-004 on score and separated from it on falsifiability, 4 versus 3).

**Written down precisely so it can be handed over rather than re-searched.** The comparison to run is:
for a common accounting standard adopted by two groups of jurisdictions — group A mandating IFRS for
the first time, group B mandating a standard that the jurisdiction's firms had already adopted
voluntarily or under an earlier mandate — compare the **post-earnings-announcement drift** (abnormal
return in a fixed window after the earnings announcement date) of the adopting firms in group A with
that of the firms in group B, in the same calendar window, controlling for size, industry and
market. The register already holds the two pieces that make the comparison executable at all: the
published adoption calendar and the mandatory-versus-voluntary-adopter contrast in S-220 (Daske et
al. 2008), whose liquidity and cost-of-capital outcomes are **not** drift outcomes and whose
voluntary-adopter arm is self-selected — which is precisely why the register needs a *first-adopter
versus second-adopter* comparison and why S-220 is a partial prior-art hit rather than a substitute.

### IDEA-014's state-free existence limb — **CONFIRMED inconclusive**

**Verdict.** Cycle 37 did not resolve whether a state-free "does the pattern exist at all" limb
exists for IDEA-014's family (the US Renewable Fuel Standard), and no new evidence was found. The
limb remains inconclusive: it is neither a positive finding nor a refutation, and it must not be
described as either. IDEA-014 remains retired at zero, P-011 stands, and no additional gap is opened
on this limb.

### Cycle-37 ranking and identifier record

- **No candidate was created and no score changed.** The active ranking is unchanged: **IDEA-011
  (69.0, rank 1), IDEA-004 (69.0, rank 2), IDEA-010 (68.0, rank 3), IDEA-009 (60.0), IDEA-014
  (52.0)**; IDEA-003 (66.0), IDEA-008 (63.0) and IDEA-002 (63.0) remain parked. Hard-stop flags were
  added to IDEA-010 and its gap set was rewritten (G-27 closed in its favour, G-29 opened); G-28's
  wording was sharpened without changing its open status.
- **New identifiers consumed in Cycle 37:** sources **S-231 … S-238**; gap **G-29**; rejections
  **R-017** (the Cycle-36 ex-dividend screen, written for real) and **R-018** (the Cycle-37 Rule 201
  family screen). No `IDEA-` identifier and no `V-` identifier were consumed.
- **Next free identifiers:** **IDEA-017, S-239, V-012, G-30, R-019, P-014**. The reserved-and-unused
  identifiers **IDEA-016, S-210 … S-219, V-011, G-26 and P-013** remain unused inside their ranges.

## Cycle 40 sources (2026-10-20)

### S-239 — iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (CIK 0001980994), **Form 424B3 statutory prospectus** — the "Authorized Participants" section, and the crypto-trust prospectus set generally
- **Type:** primary statutory filing (Securities Act prospectus) fetched and read directly from
  `www.sec.gov/Archives`.
- **Why it is in the ledger.** It is the **positive control** that makes the register's G-29 negative
  credible, and it verifies the trade-press lead that Cycle 37 had deliberately left out of the
  ledger as unverified. Verbatim from the Form 424B3 filed **31 July 2025**
  (`bit20250731_424b3.htm`): "As of the date of this prospectus, the Authorized Participants that are
  able to conduct creations and redemptions for cash are ABN AMRO Clearing USA LLC, BMO Capital
  Markets Corp., BofA Securities, Inc., Citadel Securities LLC, Citigroup Global Markets, Inc.,
  Goldman Sachs & Co. LLC, Jane Street Capital, LLC, Jefferies LLC, JP Morgan Securities LLC,
  Macquarie Capital (USA) Inc., UBS Securities LLC and Virtu Americas LLC. The Authorized Participants
  that are able to conduct creations and redemptions in-kind are Jane Street Capital, LLC, Virtu
  Americas LLC, JP Morgan Securities LLC, and Marex Capital Markets Inc. Additional Authorized
  Participants may be added at any time, subject to the discretion of the Sponsor."
- **The list evolves, and the evolution is dated.** Each of the following sentences was read in the
  document itself: the **9 January 2024** Form 424B3 (filed 10 January 2024) reads "As of the date of
  this prospectus, the Authorized Participants are ABN AMRO Clearing USA LLC, Jane Street Capital, LLC,
  JP Morgan Securities LLC, Macquarie Capital (USA) Inc. and Virtu Americas LLC" — **five**; the
  **11 April 2024** Form 424B3 (filed 18 April 2024) names **nine**, adding Citadel Securities LLC,
  Citigroup Global Markets, Inc., Goldman Sachs & Co. LLC and UBS Securities LLC; the **6 June 2024**
  Form 424B3 (filed 13 June 2024) repeats those nine; the **5 August 2024** Form 424B3 (filed 8 August
  2024) names **twelve**, adding BMO Capital Markets Corp., BofA Securities, Inc. and Jefferies LLC; and
  the **31 July 2025** Form 424B3 names the **twelve cash** APs above plus **four in-kind** APs. The same
  sets appear in the trust's Form 10-K (4 March 2024, 5 March 2025, 27 February 2026). Note the sentence
  form: the naming sentence is "As of the date of this prospectus, the Authorized Participants are …",
  and a *later* sentence adds "Additional Authorized Participants may be added at any time, subject to
  the discretion of the Sponsor" — so the published set is a **point-in-time** disclosure, and the
  AP population is *endogenous*: it expands when APs find the creation/redemption economics attractive.
- **The AP-naming practice is class-specific, and that is the finding.** An EDGAR full-text search
  for the naming sentence across all prospectus form types returns **five documents in four filers**,
  all of them digital-asset trusts: iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (CIK 1980994), iShares Ethereum Trust
  ETF (CIK 2000638), iShares Staked Ethereum Trust ETF (CIK 2099103) and iShares Bitcoin Premium
  Income ETF (CIK 2089969). The same practice appears in the filings of WisdomTree Bitcoin Fund
  (CIK 1850142), the Invesco Galaxy trusts, the 21Shares trusts, the Morgan Stanley digital-asset
  trusts, T. Rowe Price Active Crypto ETF, Ark 21Shares Bitcoin ETF, and — in older form — in the
  commodity trusts (iShares S&P GSCI Commodity-Indexed Trust, iShares Gold Trust Micro, World Gold
  Trust), whose prospectuses name their market makers and clearing counterparties. **No
  corporate-bond ETF names its APs.**
- **Caveats:** a crypto trust is not a corporate-bond ETF and its AP set says nothing about the
  candidate's universe; the AP set is a point-in-time disclosure that the sponsor may change "at any
  time"; and the register read the *prospectus*, not the underlying AP agreements, so the *set* is
  published but the *terms* are not.
- **Verification:** the documents were fetched directly from `www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/
  1980994/…` and the quoted paragraph was read in the fetched text; the filer list was obtained from
  the EDGAR company-search endpoint (CIK 0001980994, conformed name "iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF") and
  its filing index; the document-universe counts were obtained from the EDGAR full-text search API
  and each flagged document was fetched and read.

### S-240 — U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, **EDGAR submissions endpoint** (`https://data.sec.gov/submissions/CIK##########.json`)
- **Type:** primary regulator data service (per-filer filing index).
- **Why it is in the ledger.** It is the **filer-level counting instrument** the register needs after
  Cycle 40 showed that the phrase-level corpus (S-231) counts *wordings*, not *firms*. For a given
  CIK it returns the complete filing history with form type, filing date and **report date**, and
  therefore the exact **cadence** of a firm's filings.
- **What it produced in Cycle 40.** For the thirteen AP firms named in S-239 — ABN AMRO Clearing USA
  LLC (CIK 772040), BMO Capital Markets Corp. (772028), BofA Securities, Inc. (1675365), Citadel
  Securities LLC (1146184), Citigroup Global Markets Inc. (91154), Goldman Sachs & Co. LLC (42352),
  Jane Street Capital, LLC (1103083), Jefferies LLC (200403), J.P. Morgan Securities LLC (782124),
  Macquarie Capital (USA) Inc. (922116), UBS Securities LLC (230611), Virtu Americas LLC (1457716) and
  Marex Capital Markets Inc. (1540527) — the endpoint returns **X-17A-5 filings only** (no Form 10-Q
  and no Form 10-K for any of the thirteen), with **one filing per fiscal year in every case**
  (2,414 of 2,419 filers in the S-231 corpus disclose once per year; five disclose twice; none
  discloses three or more times a year), and **285 distinct (AP, filing-date) cells** across the
  thirteen. Fiscal year-ends are mostly 31 December, with Goldman Sachs & Co. and J.P. Morgan
  Securities on 30 November and Macquarie Capital (USA) on 31 March.
- **Caveats:** the endpoint returns the *filer's* filings, so a firm that files under several CIKs
  (Citadel Securities has three; ABN AMRO has three) must be enumerated CIK by CIK; and the endpoint
  does not read the filings, so it establishes *coverage and cadence*, never the *values*.
- **Verification:** called directly in this cycle for each of the thirteen CIKs and for the fifteen
  major private AP firms; the JSON was parsed and the cadence computed from the report-date fields.

### S-241 — Mitchell, M. L., Pulvino, T. C. and Stafford, E. (2004), "Price Pressure around Mergers", *The Journal of Finance* 59(1), 31–63. DOI: 10.1111/j.1540-6261.2004.00626.x
- **Type:** prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports:** the **prior-art limb of the G-28 verdict on IDEA-004** and the correction of the
  register's published-neighbour inventory. The paper uses **short interest** as the published
  measure of **merger-arbitrage short selling** and attributes part of the acquirer's announcement
  reaction to that pressure; it also documents the closing-date index-rebalancing run-up and its
  reversal for index-tracking strategies.
- **Why it matters to the register.** The candidate may not present a short-interest-based measure of
  convergence activity in merger spreads as a discovery: the construction is published, and the
  mechanism (short selling by merger arbitrageurs) is published with it.
- **Caveats:** the paper is about *merger* spreads and *announcement-window* price pressure, not about
  a *position-concentration* state or a two-sided crowding gate; the short-interest data it uses are
  exchange short-interest data, not FINRA's aggregated file.
- **Verification:** Crossref API fetched directly (title, three authors, *The Journal of Finance*,
  volume 59, issue 1, pages 31–63, 2004, DOI as above); the substance was confirmed from the
  publisher-record rendering and the abstract in the search index.

### S-242 — Hanson, S. G. and Sunderam, A. (2014), "The Growth and Limits of Arbitrage: Evidence from Short Interest", *The Review of Financial Studies* 27(4), 1238–1286. DOI: 10.1093/rfs/hht066
- **Type:** prior empirical result (peer-reviewed; online 11 October 2013, print 2014).
- **Supports:** the **prior-art limb of the G-28 verdict on IDEA-004**, and the correction of the
  register's crowding-measure inventory. Short interest is used as a **published crowding measure of
  arbitrage capital**, and the paper's central result is that the *limits* of that arbitrage bind
  precisely when the measured crowding is high.
- **Why it matters to the register.** IDEA-004's dossier listed only 13F-based and correlation-based
  crowding measures (S-026, S-157, S-222) as the published neighbours. The short-interest branch
  exists and is peer-reviewed; the candidate must now engage it.
- **Caveats:** the crowd measured is *style* arbitrage capital (value, momentum and other anomaly
  strategies), not the pair/merger-spread convergence crowd the candidate's universe requires, and
  the published relation is monotone where the candidate claims a two-sided gate.
- **Verification:** Crossref API fetched directly (title, two authors, *Review of Financial Studies*,
  volume 27, issue 4, pages 1238–1286, DOI as above).

### S-243 — Chan, H. and Tan, T. (2020), "Short Interest, Crowding and Liquidity Crises", SSRN working paper 3655131 (authors at AXA IM Chorus; University Paris Dauphine). DOI: 10.2139/ssrn.3655131
- **Type:** **working paper / institutional research** — *not* peer reviewed. Labelled as such here.
- **Supports:** the prior-art limb of the G-28 verdict, and the *cadence* comparison that makes the
  FINRA route's weakness precise. The paper uses **daily short interest** to infer near-real-time
  crowding levels of quantitative equity long-short strategies, and documents that crowding is
  associated with greater exposure to short-seller liquidity shocks.
- **Why it matters to the register.** It establishes both that a short-interest-based crowding measure
  is a *published construction* (so the candidate cannot present one as a discovery) and that the
  measure can be built at a cadence finer than the 13F grid the candidate's rule is anchored to —
  which is precisely why the FINRA route's defect is granularity and crowd attribution, not speed.
- **Caveats:** a working paper, not peer reviewed; the crowd measured is style arbitrage, not
  convergence capital; the data used are a commercial securities-finance dataset, not FINRA's public
  file.
- **Verification:** the SSRN record (DOI, authors, date) and the author-hosted PDF rendering were
  read directly, including the abstract and the data description.

### S-244 — U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, **final rule "Accredited Investor Definition"**, Release Nos. 33-10824; 34-89669, File No. S7-25-19, RIN 3235-AM19, 85 FR 64234 (9 October 2020), effective 8 December 2020.
- **Type:** primary regulator material (the rule itself).
- **Why it is in the ledger.** It is the **eligibility-switch instance** screened in Cycle 40's family
  screen (R-020): a switch in *who may hold* restricted securities, with a published compliance date
  and published thresholds. The rule amends the accredited-investor definition (adding new categories
  of qualifying natural persons and entities, and applying the same determination to the
  qualified-institutional-buyer definition in Rule 144A), keeps the $1 million net-worth and $200,000
  ($300,000 joint) income tests, and keeps the $100 million QIB threshold.
- **Why the screen closes.** The switch is published **in advance** of its effective date, and the
  *distance to the limit* for any given holder is private, so no published decision-time state binds
  a specific security; the obligor's flow is its own; and the number of switches is the effective
  sample.
- **Caveats:** the rule is a *definition* change with broad effects; the register did not attempt a
  price-impact measurement and did not claim one. The screen is a **family** verdict, not an
  empirical finding about this rule.
- **Verification:** the SEC's own rule page and the Federal Register version were fetched directly
  (release numbers, RIN, 85 FR 64234, effective date 8 December 2020) and the operative thresholds
  were read in the fetched text.

### S-245 — U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, **17 CFR 270.6c-11 — Rule 6c-11 under the Investment Company Act of 1940** (the "ETF rule"), as codified in the eCFR.
- **Type:** primary regulator material (the rule itself).
- **Why it is in the ledger.** It is the source for Cycle 41's G-30 sub-route (a) research: whether the SEC's ETF rule requires an ETF to publish the *identities* of its authorised participants on its website or in its prospectus. Read directly from the eCFR text: 6c-11(c)(1) requires daily website disclosure of portfolio holdings, NAV, premium/discount and bid-ask spread; 6c-11(d) requires recordkeeping of written AP agreements and per-basket records *including the identity of the AP transacting*. The rule does **not** require an ETF to publish AP identities publicly — AP identity is a recordkeeping item under (d), not a public-disclosure item under (c)(1).
- **Why the screen closes.** Sub-route (a) — the regulatory requirement route — is closed as a bounded negative: there is no regulatory requirement to publish AP identities; only a voluntary one. The only public-facing disclosure obligations are the portfolio/NAV/premium/spread items; AP identity is recordkeeping, not public disclosure.
- **Caveats:** the rule is read as codified in the eCFR; exchange listing rules (SR-filings on sec.gov) and Form N-1A appendices were not separately checked in this cycle but are noted as potential secondary routes under G-30.
- **Verification:** eCFR text read directly (6c-11(c)(1) disclosure items; 6c-11(d) recordkeeping items including AP identity).

### S-246 — S&P Dow Jones Indices, **S&P U.S. Indices Methodology** (index maintenance, timing of changes, announcements, share freeze, rebalancing), provider-hosted PDF; and FTSE Russell index notices (gilt-index changes announced 2026-05-12, effective 2026-05-20; FCA-driven changes effective 2024-07-29).
- **Type:** venue documentation (primary rules) and provider notices.
- **Why it is in the ledger.** It is the source for Cycle 42's R-022 family screen: benchmark-methodology changes with published effective dates. S&P DJI publishes methodology changes with announced and effective dates (e.g., changes published 2026-04-26). FTSE Russell publishes index notices with announcement and effective dates (e.g., gilt changes effective 2026-05-20; FCA changes effective 2024-07-29).
- **Why the screen closes.** R-022 — the benchmark-methodology-change family — is a bounded negative: the change is published in advance so any flow is anticipated; the reconstitution effect has collapsed per S-146/S-147; the constrained flow is the index fund's own per S-151; and the effective sample is the number of methodology changes, not securities × dates. No candidate, no dossier, no score change.
- **Caveats:** provider methodologies are revised at the provider's discretion; a specific event's rules must be read from the announcement itself and the version of the methodology in force at that date. The FTSE Russell gilt notice and FCA change are cited as instances of the template, not as verified empirical findings.
- **Verification:** S&P DJI methodology PDF read directly from spglobal.com; FTSE Russell index notices cited from provider announcements (announcement date and effective date pairs).

## Cycle 38 and Cycle 39 gap verdicts (backfilled by Cycle 40 — these two cycles never had a verdict block in this file)

Coverage count **0 of inspected**: the corporate-bond ETF prospectuses and statements of additional
information read in Cycle 38 (AGG, ISTB) disclose authorised-participant concentration risk generically
and do not name the APs, and EDGAR searches found no named AP set in bond-ETF filings. The candidate was
recorded as **not specifiable from public bond ETF filings — untested, not dead**, with no published
crowding proxy to be substituted.

### G-27 and G-28 — as recorded by Cycle 38 — **unchanged by Cycle 38** (G-27 bounded by the G-29
finding; G-28 remained frozen on the Form 13F holdings-matching route with its four objections)

(Vanguard, Xtrackers, PGIM, Global X, Northern, Columbia) and again found generic disclosure with no
named AP set. Coverage remained 0. The route was deliberately **not** closed, because the search had
been organised by *issuer* rather than by *document*.

### G-29 — as closed by Cycle 40 — see the Cycle-40 block below. The Cycle-38 and Cycle-39 readings
were **correct but under-powered**: they were evidence about the documents read, not about the
population, and the population-wide negative is now established with a calibrated detector.

---

## Cycle 40 gap verdicts (2026-10-20)

### G-29 — **CLOSED AS A BOUNDED NEGATIVE for the public-filings route** (IDEA-010); coverage 0; and the
### first published AP set in the project is a **crypto trust**, which does not reach the candidate

**Verdict.** Cycle 40 executed the verification the gap asked for — *which corporate-bond ETFs name
their authorised participants in a public filing, and how many (ETF, AP) cells have both a published
AP-set membership and a public capital ratio* — as a document-level search with a **calibrated
detector**, and the answer is: **none, and zero**. The candidate is therefore **not specifiable from
public filings — untested, not dead** — and, per the register's standing rule, no published crowding
proxy may be substituted for the missing mapping.

**How the negative was made credible.** A negative about a population needs a detector known to work.
Cycle 40 first found and read a filing that genuinely names its APs — the iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF's
Form 424B3 (S-239) — and used the naming sentence itself ("Authorized Participants that are able to
conduct …") as the detector phrase in the EDGAR full-text index. That detector returns **five documents
in four filers**, all digital-asset trusts, and **no corporate-bond ETF**. The complement of the
detector was run as well: the *phrase-first* route (591 bond-worded filings containing an AP-naming
sentence — every document that actually names APs in the naming position is a commodity or crypto
trust) and the *name-first* route (bond-worded filings containing any of thirty-one AP firm names —
where the names appear only in securities-lending "percentage of ownership" tables and in the
standing "an Authorized Participant may hold of record more than 25 % of the outstanding shares"
disclosure). Two sponsor product pages (iShares AGG, Vanguard BND) were probed directly and both use
the generic formulation without naming any AP, and the **unit-investment-trust** class was checked as a
separate structure (First Trust's corporate-bond 497K filings of 13 February 2025 and 2 March 2026):
they name **no** AP firm either.

**Corroboration (a second, independent detector).** A broader detector — every filing whose text
contains an authorised-participant naming position followed by corporate entity names, across sixteen
filing form types — was read document by document: **3,628 documents read at the end of Cycle 40 of a
4,409-document corpus** (the sweep was still running when the cycle closed and reported no contradicting
document), of which **434 name APs**, and **every one of the 434 is a commodity or crypto trust** —
iShares S&P GSCI Commodity-Indexed Trust (50), iShares Diversified Alternatives Trust (22), ProShares
Trust II (17), iShares Commodity Optimized Trust (15), SPDR Gold Trust (15), ETFS Platinum Trust (12),
WisdomTree Continuous Commodity Index trusts (19), iShares Ethereum Trust ETF (11), iShares Bitcoin
Trust (17 across two display names), iShares Gold Trust Micro (10), World Gold Trust (9), the Invesco
Galaxy Bitcoin and Ethereum trusts (16), Ark 21Shares Bitcoin ETF (7), Hashdex Nasdaq CME Crypto Index
ETF (7), abrdn Gold ETF Trust (7), iShares Dow Jones-UBS Roll Select Commodity Index Trust (7),
WisdomTree Bitcoin Fund (7), and others. **No corporate-bond ETF trust appears.**

**The name-first route, completed.** The bond-worded corpus (every filing containing an AP-firm
name and the word "Bond") came to **3,127 documents** and was read document by document: **3,124 read**
at the end of Cycle 40, **237 flagged**, and a strict re-check of every flagged document for a firm name
**in an authorised-participant naming position** returned **zero** true positives (the 33 apparent
matches were the substring "IMC" inside "PIMCO" — a reminder that firm-name regexes must be bounded
by word boundaries, a caution now recorded for future cycles).

**The three false-positive mechanisms, named so a future cycle does not re-derive them.** AP firm
names appear in ETF prospectuses in exactly three places, none of which is an AP list: (i) **
securities-lending "percentage of ownership" tables**, where the firm is a borrower of the fund's
collateral (verified: the Jane Street Capital entry in the iShares Trust SAI is a securities-lending
ownership row); (ii) **revenue-sharing and placement disclosure** (verified: the Pershing, RBC,
Schwab, TD Ameritrade, Morgan Stanley Wealth Management and Fidelity entries in the SPDR Series Trust
SAI are the payees of distribution-arrangement payments, not APs); and (iii) the standing disclosure
that "an Authorized Participant may hold of record more than 25 % of the outstanding shares" of a fund.

**The counts, which the gap asked for explicitly.**

| Quantity | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Corporate-bond ETFs with a published AP set in a statutory filing | **0** |
| (ETF, AP) cells with both a published AP set and a public capital ratio | **0** |
| (ETF, AP, episode) cells for the candidate's universe | **0** |
| ETFs (outside the candidate's universe) with a published AP set | 4 filers / 5 documents, all crypto or commodity trusts |
| Named AP firms in the one published set (S-239, 31 July 2025) | **13** (12 cash, 4 in-kind, 13 distinct) |
| Of those, firms with a public Form X-17A-5 filing | **13 of 13** |
| (AP, filing-date) episode cells for that set | **285** |
| Cadence of that set's capital state | **annual** for all thirteen (fiscal year-end; filed February–March) |
| Named AP firms filing a Form 10-Q or 10-K | **0 of 13** — X-17A-5 is the only public capital route for the entire set |

**What the candidate may and may not now say.** It may not present the AP capital ratio as a state
that can be attached to a corporate-bond ETF from public filings, because no such attachment exists;
it may not substitute a published crowding proxy (S-026's Days-ADV, S-157's comomentum, S-222's
crowding factor), which measures a different crowd; and it may not claim that the state is
*unavailable* — the state is abundantly available (2,419+ broker-dealer filers, S-231), it is the
*ETF → AP mapping* that is missing. The candidate is retained, not parked: what has closed is a
route, not the candidate.

**The new gap this creates — G-30.** The route is closed for *statutory filings*. It is not closed for
*all* primary sources. A corporate-bond ETF's AP set could still be published by the sponsor itself
(the ETF sponsor's own website or its published participant list), by a published exchange rule or
regulatory filing that requires it, or by a *non-US* issuer's prospectus. Cycle 40 probed two sponsor
product pages and found nothing, which is a probe, not a survey. **G-30 is therefore opened**: *is
there a published, primary-source route by which a corporate-bond ETF's AP set is disclosed outside
the statutory prospectus — and, if the sponsor publishes a list, does it exist for the corporate-bond
series as well as for the crypto and commodity series?* The register's answer must be a specific
route, not a re-reading of prospectuses.

### G-27 — **CLOSED at filer level** (IDEA-010), with two corrections to the register's own statements

**Verdict.** The effective-sample question the gap raised has been answered with counts taken from
the filings' own index rather than from document text, and two of the register's statements needed
correcting.

| Route | Instrument | Count | Cadence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A — private broker-dealers | Form X-17A-5 (FOCUS), phrase corpus (S-231) | **6,131 documents from 2,419 distinct filers** (a **lower bound**) | 2,414 filers once per year; five twice per year; **none three or more** |
| A' — the named AP set specifically | EDGAR submissions index (S-240) | 13 of 13 firms covered; **285 (AP, filing-date) cells** | **annual** for all thirteen |
| B — listed broker-dealers | Form 10-Q / Form 10-K | **1,151 documents / 66 filers** (10-Q, quarterly); **737 documents / 96 filers** (10-K, annual) | quarterly and annual; the only quarterly public capital route, and it does not reach the private APs |
| C — bank holding companies | Form FR Y-9C | **not enumerable through EDGAR**: the form is absent from EDGAR's full-text index (zero hits for "capital", "net capital", "Federal Reserve") | must come from the Federal Reserve's National Information Center; S-226 restated accordingly |
| D — asset-manager APs | — | no equivalent exists | coverage limit, not a fixable one |

**Correction 1 (S-231).** The phrase corpus counts *wordings*, not firms: Citadel Securities LLC files
24 X-17A-5 documents across 23 periods and contains the phrase in one of them, so 6,131 documents ≠
6,131 firms. Any firm-coverage question must be answered at filer level.

**Correction 2 (S-231 / Cycle 37).** The statement "quarterly for at least some private filers"
rests on a single document (Agentis Capital Markets US, LLC, period ending 2024-09-30) that does not
appear in the phrase corpus at all, and filer-level enumeration of the thirteen named AP firms and
fifteen major private AP firms found **annual cadence in every case**. The private-AP capital state is
therefore **annual**, and at any decision date it is **9–14 months stale**. The Cycle-35
cross-sectional re-anchoring survives this correction — indeed it is reinforced, because the state
never changes inside a holding window — and the Cycle-35 requirement that the effective sample be
reported is now given a concrete denominator.

### G-28 — **CONFIRMED OPEN and restated** (IDEA-004): the FINRA short-interest route is a lead with
### objections, and the published-crowding inventory was incomplete

**Verdict.** Cycle 40 tested the one route the handoff named — FINRA's twice-monthly short-interest
file (S-127) — against the candidate's pair structure, and recorded it as a **lead with objections**
rather than as a resolution. G-28 remains open; **V-002 remains frozen**; the candidate is not
re-scored.

**The facts (fetched directly from FINRA).** Firms must report short positions in all customer and
proprietary accounts in all equity securities twice a month, due by 6 p.m. ET on the second business
day after the designated settlement date, published the following Tuesday (the 2026 schedule is on
the page); the published data are **per issue**, covering all exchange-listed and OTC equity
securities, one rolling year online with archives by download, with **no firm-level breakdown**
(S-127, as extended in Cycle 40).

**The objections, written so a validator can act on them.**
1. **Granularity, not cadence, is what fails.** A per-issue aggregate across all reporting firms
   cannot identify the convergence crowd and cannot express its concentration; mapping it to pairs is
   a register-built construction with the same defect as the 13F holdings-matching route, and it
   cannot separate convergence capital from a coincident directional short. Note that the cadence
   objection that usually kills a route does **not** apply here: twice-monthly publication with an
   ~8-business-day lag is *faster* than the 45-day 13F grid the candidate's rule is anchored to.
2. **Contamination of the short leg.** In the classic pair trade the short leg is the recent winner,
   whose short interest also carries value, short-bias and index strategies; a pair-level aggregation
   would have to remove them, and the published data cannot.
3. **Prior art (new in this cycle).** Short interest is *already* the published measure of merger-arb
   shorting and its price pressure (S-241); short interest is *already* a published crowding measure of
   arbitrage capital (S-242); and daily short interest has *already* been used to infer near-real-time
   crowding of quantitative long-short strategies (S-243). The candidate may not present a
   short-interest-based convergence-activity measure as a discovery, and the register's own
   published-neighbour inventory for IDEA-004 was **incomplete** without the short-interest branch.
4. **What would actually close the gap.** A published measure of *convergence-crowd position
   concentration* in the candidate's own universe (pairs, merger-spread and index-convergence relative
   value) at the candidate's cadence, or a published validation of a construction against an outcome.
   The unfreezing condition is unchanged; the FINRA route is now named as a **third** candidate route
   alongside the 13F holdings-matching construction and the comomentum proxy, with its objections.

### G-21 — **not re-searched in Cycle 40** (IDEA-011); the Cycle-37 null stands unchanged
Cycle 40's priorities did not include G-21, and no new evidence on the first-adopter-versus-second-adopter
drift comparison was encountered while reading the crowding and short-interest literature. The Cycle-37
null stands: the comparison remains **untested** rather than refuted, and IDEA-011 remains at 69.0.

No new evidence was found. The limb is neither a positive finding nor a refutation and must not be

## Cycle 41 gap verdicts (2026-10-20)

### G-30 sub-route (a) — **CLOSED as a bounded negative** (IDEA-010): 17 CFR 270.6c-11 imposes no requirement to publish AP identities.
**Verdict.** 17 CFR 270.6c-11 (the SEC's ETF rule, S-245) defines "authorized participant" and requires daily website disclosure of portfolio holdings, NAV, premium/discount and bid-ask spread under (c)(1), and requires recordkeeping of written AP agreements and per-basket records *including the identity of the AP transacting* under (d). AP identity is a recordkeeping item, not a public-disclosure item. **Route (a) is therefore closed: there is no regulatory route, only a voluntary one.**

### G-30 sub-routes (b) and (c) — **NOT FOUND** (IDEA-010); both closed as *not found* verdicts.
**Verdict.** Sub-route (b) sponsor-published AP lists: checked iShares AGG/USIG/HYG/LQD/iBonds term/EUHY/CEMB/EMHY/LEMB/GHYG (ishares.com product pages; 497K/485BPOS SEC docs), Vanguard BND/VCEB/VPLS/BNDW/VCHY (p4158.pdf), PGIM Corporate Bond 0-5 Year & 5-10 Year ETF summary prospectuses, T. Rowe Price QM U.S. Bond ETF, State Street SPBO/SPLB/SPIB/SPSB/IBND (the SSGA AP contact list is Ireland-domiciled, AP-facing, not per-fund identity publication), and Fidelity FCOR 497K. None name authorised participants; all use the generic formulation ("a limited number of financial institutions… not obligated"; "contact the Distributor"). Sub-route (c) non-US issuer: no US-listed corporate-bond ETF sponsored by or listed against a non-US issuer with a published AP set was located; Ireland-domiciled UCITS bond ETFs (e.g., JPMorgan ETFs Ireland ICAV USD IG Corporate Bond UCITS ETF supplement dated 2026-08-12; iShares Ireland UCITS supplements) are outside the candidate universe. **G-30 remains open on sub-route (a), which is closed.**

### G-28 (IDEA-004) — **CONFIRMED OPEN and frozen**; V-002 frozen.
No published convergence-crowd concentration measure located; null recorded.

### G-21 (IDEA-011) — **CONFIRMED NULL**; not re-searched in Cycle 41.
The first-adopter-versus-second-adopter drift comparison remains untested rather than refuted; IDEA-011 remains at 69.0.

## Cycle 51 sources (2026-10-20)

Added for the Cycle-51 family screen of **closing-price formation** (randomised closes and real-time
closing-auction imbalance publication), logged as **R-031**. Five new sources; the register now holds
**S-001 … S-209** and **S-220 … S-251**.

### S-247 — Ellul, A., Shin, H. S. and Tonks, I. (2005)
"Opening and Closing the Market: Evidence from the London Stock Exchange." *Journal of Financial and
Quantitative Analysis* 40(4): 779–801.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1017/S0022109000001976
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports:** the LSE's call-market opening and closing auctions are a studied institutional mechanism —
  the paper documents the call market's price-discovery properties relative to off-exchange dealing,
  failure rates, and trading costs that rise with the order imbalance and with asymmetric information.
  This is the published anchor for the *closing-mechanism* family: how the close is formed is
  documented, not an open question.
- **Caveats:** 2005 sample; London-specific; the paper is about the call market's price discovery and
  costs, not about randomised price formation as such.
- **Verification:** Cambridge Core landing page (title, authors, volume, issue, pages, DOI) read through
  the search index; the LSE working-paper version (Financial Markets, Institutions and Instruments
  discussion paper dp506) was seen in the search index with the passage describing the LSE closing
  call's random end within a bounded window.

### S-248 — Chelley-Steeley, P. (2009)
"Price synchronicity: The closing call auction and the London stock market." *Journal of Financial
Markets, Institutions and Money* 19(5): 777–791.
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports:** the introduction of the LSE's closing call auction is measured for its market-quality
  effects (price synchronicity, spreads, depth), with larger effects in less liquid names. The
  *market-quality* consequences of a closing-auction redesign are therefore published prior art.
- **Caveats:** one venue, one design change, 2000s sample; the paper is a design evaluation, not a
  statement about any tradeable concession.
- **Verification:** RePEc/IDEAS bibliographic record (author, title, journal, volume, issue, pages) read
  through the search index; the publisher page was not parsed directly in this session and the DOI was
  not verified.

### S-249 — Eom, K. S., Kwon, K. Y. and Park, J.-H. (2021)
"Effectiveness of the conditional random-end trading mechanism on the Korea Exchange: Normal trade and
Option Shock." *Journal of Futures Markets* 41(10): 1545–1568.
DOI: https://doi.org/10.1002/fut.22223
- **Type:** Prior empirical result (peer-reviewed).
- **Supports:** randomised end-of-auction mechanisms are a published mechanism family. The Korea
  Exchange's conditional random end is reported to improve price discovery and stabilise the closing
  price in normal trading, with the benefit attenuating under high-volatility ("option shock")
  conditions — i.e. the randomised close is studied and its effects are characterised.
- **Caveats:** one exchange, one mechanism design, 2010s sample; the paper is not a study of a
  harvestable pre-close concession.
- **Verification:** citation metadata (authors, title, journal, volume, issue, pages, DOI) verified
  through the DOI resolver's Crossref record; the publisher article page returned HTTP 403 to the
  fetch, and the abstract was read through the search index.

### S-250 — Eurex Exchange (venue documentation)
"FTSE 100 Index Futures (FTUK)" — contract specifications (product page).
URL: https://www.eurex.com/ex-en/markets/idx/ftse/ftse-100/ftse-100-derivatives/FTSE-100-Index-Futures-2593796
- **Type:** Venue documentation (exchange product page).
- **Supports:** the settlement channel of the one major complex where a randomised cash close sits next
  to a liquid futures market. The page states that the final settlement price "is established by Eurex
  on the final settlement day of the contract and is determined by the value of the FTSE 100 Expiry
  Index as calculated by FTSE International Limited following the Exchange Delivery Settlement Price
  ('EDSP') intraday auction on the London Stock Exchange", and that the daily settlement price for the
  current maturity month is "the volume-weighted average of the prices of all transactions during the
  minute before 17:30 CET". Both are determined **after** the cash close is known, so the randomness of
  the cash close does not reach the futures' settlement value.
- **Caveats:** a product page, not the rulebook; the EDSP auction's own micro-description is not on
  the page; Eurex and ICE specifications differ in detail, so this is an illustration of the
  decoupling rather than a universal statement.
- **Verification:** page fetched directly (contract-specifications section read in full, including the
  settlement and final-settlement paragraphs quoted above).

### S-251 — U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission / NYSE (primary regulatory material)
89 FR 19370–19374 (18 March 2024), SEC Release No. 34-99719, File No. SR-NYSE-2024-13, "Notice of
Filing of Proposed Rule Change for Amendments to Rule 7.35 and Rule 7.35B"; approval order SEC Release
No. 34-100327 (13 June 2024).
URL: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2024-03-18/html/2024-05632.htm
- **Type:** Primary regulatory material.
- **Supports:** two facts that the Cycle-51 screen needs. (i) **The decision-time state of a closing
  auction is a real-time, paid-feed series**: "Beginning ten minutes before the scheduled end of Core
  Trading Hours, the Exchange begins disseminating through its proprietary data feed Closing Auction
  Imbalance Information", which "includes the Continuous Book Clearing Price" and "an Imbalance
  Reference Price", is "updated at least every second … until the Closing Auction begins", and is
  supplemented by paired/unpaired quantities; the exchange states that publishing it "could facilitate
  entry of offsetting orders and the price discovery process" and "could also attract additional
  contra-side interest, thereby decreasing volatility" — the exchange designs the publication to
  attract offsetting interest, which is the competition-away mechanism in the screen's third reason.
  (ii) **The US close is not randomised**: "In 2022, the Exchange made further changes to the Closing
  Auction, including adding price parameters within which the DMM must select a Closing Auction Price,
  in order to make the Closing Auction more transparent and deterministic" (Release No. 95691,
  87 FR 56099).
- **Caveats:** US-only, and the rule text is as of 2024; the approval order's own text was read only
  through the search index; the NYSE also states that D Orders were 42.7 % of closing-auction volume
  as of Q3 2023, which is an interested party's own figure.
- **Verification:** the Federal Register text (89 FR 19370–19374) was fetched directly from govinfo and
  read in full; the approval order (SEC Release No. 34-100327) and the 2022 amendment's own text were
  read through the search index.

### The Cycle-51 closing-price-formation family screen — logged as **R-031**
Randomised closes (a venue drawing the official close randomly inside an order-book-determined range) and
real-time closing-auction imbalance publication were screened as one family: **the register's first screen
of a *price-formation* family**, where every earlier screen (R-012 … R-030) covered obligations, switches,
supply schedules or expiry-driven flows. Verdict: **bounded negative** on five independent reasons —
published baseline (S-247, S-248, S-249, with S-149/S-150 already in the ledger); no sign-determining
state, and a single-price auction cannot express the compensation for the draw in the price; the
concession is competed away inside the auction by the exchange's own design (S-251); the settlement channel
does not propagate (S-250) and the register's own instrument universe has no randomised close (S-251); and
the cost floor plus the proprietary nature of the decision-time state leave nothing harvestable. The screen,
its instances, its five reasons and its dated re-screen condition are recorded in `rejected_and_parked.md`
under **R-031**, and the family was added to the known-baseline table.
